# B) CEL-SHADED / TOON-BANDED 3D WITH OUTLINES

## Guilty Gear Xrd — the best source, and I have the full slide deck

The official handout PDF is public: **https://www.ggxrd.com/Motomura_Junya_GuiltyGearXrd.pdf** (4.9 MB, full speaker notes). Free video: **https://archive.org/details/GDC2015Motomura** (MPEG4 512 MB / OGG 244 MB). GDC Vault: https://www.gdcvault.com/play/1022031/GuiltyGearXrd-s-Art-Style-The. ArcSys announcement: https://www.arcsystemworks.com/guilty-gear-xrds-art-style-the-x-factor-between-2d-and-3d-talk-from-gdc-2015-is-now-available-online/

Verified numbers and methods, quoted from the deck:

- **Band count: 2. It is a hard `step`, not a ramp texture.** "In Cel-shading, the surface is either lit, or not, and no value in between." "In the main part of the calculation, a generic 'step' function is used to decide if that pixel is lit or not… What matters here are the following: The Threshold, the Light Vector, and the Normal Vector. And that's all." **Correct your brief here — GGXrd uses no shading ramp.**
- **Threshold control via vertex colour.** "We used a channel from the Vertex Colors as an offset on the Threshold. This made it possible for the artist to make certain areas on the mesh get darker more easily… The artist may even set the value to Zero, making the area always shaded no matter what." This is their answer to shading breakups.
- **Normal editing.** "To get rid of unintentional shading, we modified the normals on every major feature of the model. The faces of the Characters especially needed to be hand crafted to get a clean anime look." Done in Softimage with a live in-DCC shader preview matching the game.
- **Colour via two flat lookup textures, not a ramp.** "The base texture defines the color of the surface when it's lit. While the Tint texture defines how dark it gets when shaded. Multiplying the two textures, we get the shaded color." Both textures are "just square areas consisting of single colors… we simply use them as color lookups, and do not draw-in any details."
- **Outlines: inverted hull, in the vertex shader, width driven by vertex colour.** "we use a traditional 'Inverted Hull' method… A second set of darker polygons are generated in the shader and are expanded in the normals direction… we were able give the outline variable widths in places we saw fit, even erasing it completely at times." Why not post-process: "we could preview the result right in the modeling viewport."
- **Interior lines: axis-aligned beams + distorted UVs.** "All the lines are drawn as Axis Aligned beams on the texture, and the UV is lined up alongside them. How much the UV overlaps these beams, defines how thick that line becomes. As long as the pixels are Aligned to an Axis they don't get any jaggie." Downside accepted: heavily distorted UVs, which is fine because no detail lives in the textures.
- **Poly/bone counts:** "around **40000 triangles** in average" per character; "The bone count is around **500** per character in average."
- **No normal maps at all** on characters — vertex normals, vertex colours and UVs carry the data, because vertex attributes interpolate linearly and are resolution-independent at extreme close-ups.
- **No global lighting on characters.** "Each character has his or her own dedicated light vector that lights their idle pose in the best way."
- **Animation:** interpolation between keys is off entirely. "Every frame now is a key frame, and the animator poses the character in the best way possible." Plus deliberate per-keyframe mesh deformation "to add Imperfection," and heavy scale animation (which they had to implement themselves in UE3). Note: the deck does **not** state an "animate on 2s/3s" number — that claim is not in the primary source.
- Engine: Unreal Engine 3 + Autodesk Softimage. 1080p PS4 / 720p PS3, up from 480p sprites.

## Hi-Fi Rush — full GDC 2024 slide deck also public

**https://media.gdcvault.com/gdc2024/Slides/GDC+slide+presentations/Tanaka_Kosuke_3D_Toon_Rendering.pdf** (13.8 MB, full notes). Talk page: https://gdcvault.com/play/1034330/3D-Toon-Rendering-in-Hi. Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gdBACyIOCtc. Summary: https://80.lv/articles/the-making-of-hi-fi-rush-s-3d-toon-rendering-style. Third-party notes: https://www.foth.top/article/gdc-2024-hifirush-toonrendering-notes/

- **Band count: 2, verbatim.** "Hi-Fi RUSH's toon shading uses a simple 2-tone lit/shade toon shading model." Key light is **forward-rendered in UE4's base pass** (not deferred) specifically to avoid engine modifications.
- **Outlines are POST-PROCESS, and split into two independent passes** — a normal outline and a depth outline. Confirmed by their G-buffer stencil bits: `HBK_AO_STENCIL_SKIP_NORMAL_OUTLINE 16`, `HBK_AO_STENCIL_SKIP_DEPTH_OUTLINE 32`. No inverted hull anywhere in the deck.
- **Shading-breakup control is a per-material stencil bitmask packed into unused PBR G-buffer channels.** They kept the stock UE4 layout (RGBA8 × 3) and repurposed `GBufferAO` and `Specular` as bitmasks: `SKIP_SHADOW_MAIN 1`, `SKIP_SHADOW_2/3/4 2/4/8`, `ENV_SKIP_AMBIENT_CUBEMAP 16`, `ENV_SKIP_STATIC_SHADOWMAP 32`, `ENV_SKIP_SSAO 64`, plus `CHARACTER_MASK 4`. "GBuffer stencil requires no additional render passes and is practically free." Artists set these per material instance, and some per mesh actor. **This is the single most portable idea in the deck** — one mask channel that lets art switch off outlines/AO/shadows per surface.
- **Character self-shadow** is plain `if (NdotL &gt; threshold)` on the **vertex normal** — everywhere except the face.
- **Face shadows use a "threshold map," not NdotL.** Authoring is fully documented: bake vertex-normal face shadows with a directional light swept 180° in **5° steps = 36 textures**; artists retouch each by hand; an in-house tool merges them into one texture with "distance field-like interpolation." At runtime: `lightDirHorizontal = Normalize(lightDir - Dot(lightDir, headBoneUp))`; `threshold = ArcCos(Dot(lightDirHorizontal, headBoneForward)) / PI`; then `height = TexSample(thresholdMap, uv); if (height &gt; threshold) baseColor else shadowColor`. Texture flips horizontally when the light crosses to the other side. Credited to miHoYo's Jack He, Unity Seoul 2018.
- **Comic shader:** halftone dots on lit areas, hatching lines on shadowed areas, both generated procedurally from SDFs — hatching is "the same shader code, just with UV.y fixed to 0." Anti-aliased with `smoothstep(Radius-Width, Radius+Width, Dist)` instead of `step`. Projected with **triplanar world-space UVs — "Only 3 planes, XY, YZ, XZ"** — using the G-buffer world normal, so patterns look printed on surfaces rather than stuck to the screen. Halftone size scales with scene luminance.
- UE4, **60 FPS at native resolution**, both characters and environment toon-shaded. Artists place "toon process box volumes" throughout levels to tune shading locally.

## Wind Waker

Accessible sources are thinner than the two above. Verified at the level of technique, not parameters: vertex lighting is computed normally, then the brightness value is **swapped for a value from a lookup texture holding the desired shading curve** (a ramp LUT — GameCube had no programmable shaders, so this is a TEV texture indirection), and shadows/highlights are then tinted rather than simply darkened. **One dominant light per object** — when several lights are present the game elects the strongest and uses only that, producing the harsh binary falloff. — https://boingboing.net/2020/01/25/this-video-explains-how-cel-sh.html , https://www.resetera.com/threads/video-how-the-wind-waker-defined-cel-shading.166993/

**Band count and outline method: unverified** from any primary or reverse-engineering source I could reach. Useful secondary analysis: https://medium.com/@gordonnl/wind-waker-graphics-analysis-a0b575a31127 (Medium blocks automated fetch). A working modern reimplementation with a documented feature list — two-tone highlight/shadow, soft band transition, rim highlight, time-of-day and weather palettes, multi-light (an extension beyond the original's single light) — is https://github.com/janhohenheim/bevy_wind_waker_shader

## Sable

Flat shading with thin black outlines, no shading apart from occasional dotting. The team's stated problem was depth readability under flat shading, solved by **layering three things**: shadows ("helps players figure out where they sit on a surface"), **per-biome fog** (called "really, really key" for mid-to-long distance, also drives the day/night cycle), and **outlines that fade with distance** — used both to convey perspective and to disguise pop-in. World is 10 km × 10 km. Outline implementation detects **depth or material discontinuities** (post-process, not inverted hull). — https://www.gamedeveloper.com/marketing/how-shedworks-refined-the-art-of-sable-in-pursuit-of-readability , https://www.cookandbecker.com/en/article/170/sable-exploration-through-line-art.html

**Exact line width, palette values and band count: unverified.**

## Borderlands

**Gearbox explicitly rejects the "cel-shaded" label.** The technique is "hand-painting with integrated inks": hand-drawn textures scanned and coloured in Photoshop, *plus* software that draws graphic-novel outlines around characters and objects, sharpens shadows, and draws lines on hills and inclines. The ink is authored into the albedo; the outline is a post-process on top. Randy Pitchford, 2009: "Borderlands is not cel-shaded." — https://www.destructoid.com/borderlands-explains-its-not-cel-shaded-actually-art-style/ , https://www.gamespot.com/articles/behind-borderlands-11th-hour-style-change/1100-6253257/

GDC 2010 talk: "Borderlands &amp; The 11th Hour Art Style Change (Don't Try This at Home)," Randy Pitchford and Brian Martel.

**Band count: not applicable** — the value structure lives in the painted texture, not in a lighting ramp.

## Dragon Quest XI

**No public technical breakdown exists.** The only first-party source is Epic's non-technical spotlight interview, which confirms UE4 and **Enlighten** for dynamic GI, and describes the central problem as blending Toriyama-style cel-shaded characters against photoreal environments without either clashing. Band count, outline method and shader details are all **unverified**. — https://www.unrealengine.com/spotlights/how-dragon-quest-xi-came-to-life-with-unreal-engine-4

## Jet Set Radio

Art-direction sources only; no technical documentation survives in accessible form. Verified: internally called "manga dimension"; Dreamcast implementation was "extremely challenging"; **characters shipped without outlines and sharp shading until the middle of development**, before the team had the tools. Director Masayoshi Kikuchi chose cel shading to express art director Ryuta Ueda's flat illustrated look. **Outline technique: unverified.** — https://segabits.com/blog/2022/01/25/exclusive-classic-sega-interview-smilebits-takayuki-kawagoe-talks-jet-set-radio-future-with-xbox-nation-magazine/ , https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_Set_Radio

## Band-count summary (verified only)

| Game | Bands | Outline method |
|---|---|---|
| Guilty Gear Xrd | **2** (hard `step`, vertex-colour threshold offset) | **Inverted hull**, vertex-shader extrude, vertex-colour width, can be zeroed |
| Hi-Fi Rush | **2** ("2-tone lit/shade") | **Post-process**, split normal-edge and depth-edge passes, per-material skip bits |
| Wind Waker | unverified (ramp LUT, one dominant light) | unverified |
| Sable | flat (no shading bands) | post-process depth/material discontinuity, fades with distance |
| Borderlands | n/a (painted inks) | post-process outline + inked albedo |
| DQXI, Jet Set Radio | unverified | unverified |

General reference on ramp construction (1D ramps are typically 256×1; use `texelFetch` rather than a filtered sample for strict cel; blur the ramp for soft cel): https://panthavma.com/articles/shading/toonshading/. Canonical parameterised toon shader with real defaults — `smoothstep(0, 0.01, NdotL)` for the band, `_Glossiness 32`, specular `smoothstep(0.005, 0.01, …)`, `_RimThreshold 0.1`, `_AmbientColor 0.4` — https://roystan.net/articles/toon-shader/

---

# C) FLAT-SHADED VOXEL &amp; LOW-POLY TOY LOOK

## Why it reads as "toy," and the direct answer on outlines

Flat shading evaluates lighting once per polygon from the face normal, so every face is a uniform colour chip with a hard value step at each 90° corner — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shading. **That value step does the job an outline would do.** Of everything verified, **only Townscaper shows evidence of outlines**; Crossy Road, Minecraft, Teardown and Hytale have none documented anywhere.

The Townscaper outline is community-observed, not dev-confirmed: "It looks like the outlines are created from slightly enlarged geometry that's only visible from the backside" — classic inverted hull. https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=29074015

## Crossy Road (Hipster Whale) — PRIORITY, with one correction

**The tool is Qubicle, not MagicaVoxel.** No source ties Crossy Road to MagicaVoxel; that appears only in fan tutorials.

Verified:
- Unity engine — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crossy_Road
- Art by **Ben Weatherall in Qubicle**, learned within a year — https://www.pocketgamer.biz/making-of-crossy-road/ ; Qubicle's store page claims the title: https://store.steampowered.com/app/454550/Qubicle_Voxel_Editor/
- **The angled camera IS the art direction.** Matt Hall "wanted to rotate the camera because voxels look better at an angle." Andy Sum objected, then conceded: "there was no denying that it looked 100 times better."
- The readability rule is stated as a *gameplay* rule, by Sum: "I wanted to have really obvious hit boxes and sharp edges so that you could play the game in a precise way and easily judge whether or not you could make the right hop."
- 12 weeks total development — https://gdcvault.com/play/1021897/Crossy-Road-A-Whale-of
- Style taxonomy places it in **"vector voxel art": "uses only 90 degree bends when representing curves… a very clean style that is easy to read at varying scales,"** partly chosen for smartphone hardware — https://www.gamedeveloper.com/art/voxelart-styles-in-video-games

**Unverified — do not invent:** voxel resolution per character, colour count per character, orthographic vs perspective, FOV, tilt angle, light count/direction, shadow style, presence of outlines (nothing mentions them), and any explicit "no AA" rule. The GDC talk abstract covers design and monetisation, not art. The PocketGamer interview is the single best primary art source and it is short.

Community reverse-engineering (not authoritative): "Extremely lowpoly," "flat low polygon models with no curves," "Few if any textures," unlit/legacy-diffuse shader rendering "solid color and brightness regardless of lighting" — https://discussions.unity.com/threads/crossy-road-art-style-how.323323/

## Minecraft — the AO algorithm you can actually copy

Official: "Smooth lighting… blends light levels across block faces and darkens corners using ambient occlusion." "For each corner of a side, the game checks diagonally for full blocks… no full blocks meaning full brightness and both sides diagonally being full blocks meaning the darkest level possible." — https://minecraft.wiki/w/Light

Exact values: sample "the three adjacent blocks plus the block which touches the face." All four transparent → intensity **1.0**; all four opaque → **0.2**; the vertex value is the average of the four, then interpolated across the face by the rasteriser. — https://greyminecraftcoder.blogspot.com/2020/04/lighting-1144.html

The canonical implementation, from Mikola Lysenko who wrote the original Minecraft AO mod — https://0fps.net/2013/07/03/ambient-occlusion-for-minecraft-like-worlds/

```js
function vertexAO(side1, side2, corner) {
  if (side1 &amp;&amp; side2) return 0
  return 3 - (side1 + side2 + corner)
}
// mandatory anisotropy fix, or quads shade differently by triangulation:
if (a00 + a11 &gt; a01 + a10) { /* flipped quad */ } else { /* normal quad */ }
```

Greedy meshing numbers — https://0fps.net/2012/06/30/meshing-in-a-minecraft-game/ : 8×8×8 solid cube 3072 quads naive → 384 culled → **6 greedy**; sphere 4770 → 2100; noisy terrain 2198 → 1670. "The greedy mesh has no more than 8× as many quads than the optimal mesh." **Important tension: merging is blocked by differing colour, light value, normal AND per-vertex AO** — greedy meshing and vertex AO work against each other.

Per-face directional shading is real and configurable per element (`shade`, and `shade_direction_override` in JE 26.3) — https://minecraft.wiki/w/Model — but the commonly cited multipliers (top 1.0 / bottom 0.5 / N-S 0.8 / E-W 0.6) are **unverified from a primary source**.

## Teardown — the counter-example (raytraced, not flat)

Primary sources, all with real numbers:
- **Voxel size: 0.1 m (10 cm)** at default scale — `GetShapeSize` returns "the size of one voxel in meters (with default scale it is 0.1)" — https://teardowngame.com/modding/api.html
- Authoring in **MagicaVoxel**; max object 256³ voxels, "Objects of half that size in all axis (128 × 128 × 128) are generally OK" — https://www.teardowngame.com/modding/
- **Palette index *is* the material:** 255 indexed entries; "the color at index 9 is always grass, no matter what actual color it is." Each entry carries roughness, emissiveness, reflectivity and physical type. One byte per voxel. Spraycan shading eats 8 slots per material, "cutting the usable number of entries in the palette down from 255 to a mere 28." — https://blog.voxagon.se/2020/12/03/spraycan.html
- **Shadow volume at 5 cm resolution**, eight neighbouring voxels packed per byte in octree fashion, mips at 10/20/40 cm, final shadow texture "256 + 32 + 4 = 292 Mb." **AO = 5 rays per pixel.** Soft shadows by jittering light position (30 cm radius example), which also hides voxel-grid artefacts. **~9 ms at 1080p on a GTX 1080** for all AO + lighting + fog + reflections including denoise. — https://blog.voxagon.se/2018/10/17/from-screen-space-to-voxel-space.html
- Stratified hemisphere sampling; adaptive raymarch of **4–16 steps** by sample probability, halving diffuse cost; 4×4 denoise kernel; 16 area lights in the worked example — https://blog.voxagon.se/2018/04/24/stratified-sampling.html
- Frame teardown by Steven Wittens: **one draw call per voxel object, 36 vertices / 12 triangles = a box**, shader raymarches the 3D texture inside it; 2 extra mips to skip empty 2³/4³ blocks (dimensions must be multiples of 4); a 1-bit volumetric shadow map over the whole play area (Marina: 1752×100×1500 texture, 262 MB, each texel packing 2×2×2 voxels); AO cosine-weighted, **2 samples/pixel**; denoise blended with reprojected previous frame "at least 4 times per frame"; **TXAA** with per-frame camera jitter; emissive voxels do not self-illuminate, lights are placed by hand — https://acko.net/blog/teardown-frame-teardown/

## Townscaper (Oskar Stålberg)

- Grid: fixed hexagonal grid → quads → quads again → points moved. Wave Function Collapse picks modules per cell, then decorates. Marching-cubes-style **corner segment tiles**, not whole-cell tiles. Windows and doors cut with the **stencil buffer**. — https://www.gamedeveloper.com/game-platforms/how-townscaper-works-a-story-four-games-in-the-making
- **Palette: 15 building colours + ground** (index 0 red … 14 white, 15 ground) — https://townscaper.fandom.com/wiki/List_of_colors , https://github.com/alvaro-cuesta/townsclipper
- Brick texture trick, quoted: "Take a really low rez texture. Shrink every other pixel to the size of a single pixel on your screen. Now you got yourself a nice random brick texture. Townscaper does that dynamically in a pixel shader." WebGL reproduction: https://reindernijhoff.net/2021/11/townscapers-rendering-style-in-webgl/
- Later lighting panel with sun position; night auto-adds warm interior lights — https://80.lv/articles/townscaper-adds-lighting-options-flying-towns
- **AO technique: unverified.** A description circulates of a layered mesh reprojected to a texture on each edit, blurred, sampled in the vertex shader — no primary source found. Talk: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Uxeo9c-PX-w

## Hytale — the most explicit official art-direction document found

https://hytale.com/news/2025/12/an-introduction-to-making-models-for-hytale — all verbatim:
- **"We only use 2 primitives: Cubes (6 sides) Quads (2 sides)."** No spheres, edge loops, triangles or pyramids. Rationale: models stay easy to make, unwrap and animate without weight painting or rigging.
- **Texel density: characters/attachments 64 px per unit, props/blocks 32 px per unit.** Textures non-square, multiples of 32 px.
- Stretch limits: "avoid going under 0.7X and over 1.3X the stretch for a node in one axis."
- **Colour rule: "Avoid pure white and pure black, as this will break lighting ingame."** Shadows carry hue — "a hint of purple in the shadows" — not desaturation.
- **Lighting: "We paint lights and shadows inside textures and use real lights/shadows to bring everything together."** No PBR; "in-house light propagation techniques."
- Silhouette discipline: "We work as simply as possible, then slightly increase geometry and polygon count when needed to improve the silhouette," so "players can read the world they navigate, regardless of how cluttered the space is."
- Exact colour counts per texture, outline use, AO specifics: **not stated.**

## Remaining titles

| Game | Verified | Not verified |
|---|---|---|
| **Cube World** (Wollay) | Exists; voxel ARPG, procedural world "made up entirely of cubes" — https://store.steampowered.com/app/1128000/Cube_World/ | **Everything technical.** Wollay's blog is gone (picroma.com/blog 403, wollay.blogspot.com 404). No numbers exist in any reachable source. |
| **Riverbond** (Cococucumber) | Exists; destructible voxel world — https://blog.playstation.com/2019/05/09/introducing-riverbond-a-voxel-shoot-and-slash-featuring-indie-crossovers/ | No technical numbers. |
| **Trove** | Devs use **Qubicle**; community assets are `.qb` — https://trove.fandom.com/wiki/Common_Voxel_Editors | Per-asset voxel limits exist in their creation docs but the host 403s. No verified number. |
| **Staxel** | Exists — https://playstaxel.com/ | **No technical art documentation at all.** Drop it. |
| **Moonglow Bay** | Store copy says "Vivid Voxel Art" — https://store.steampowered.com/app/1361400/Moonglow_Bay/ ; "presented like a diorama with its isometric camera view" is a reviewer's phrasing, not the dev's | Tools, palette, lighting, AO all unverified. |

## The canonical flat-voxel lighting model (copy this)

Sean Barrett's `stb_voxel_render.h` — https://github.com/nothings/stb/blob/master/stb_voxel_render.h

Vertex format: "per-vertex 'lighting' or 'ambient occlusion' value (**6 bits**)" and "per-face **5-bit normal**." Lighting: "half-lambert directional plus constant, all scaled by vertex ao."

```glsl
vec3 ambient_color = dot(normal, ambient[0].xyz) * ambient[1].xyz + ambient[2].xyz;
ambient_color = clamp(ambient_color, 0.0, 1.0);
ambient_color *= amb_occ;
lit_color = albedo * ambient_color;
```
`ambient[0].xyz` = negated directional light dir (half-lambert, so nothing goes fully black); `ambient[1].rgb` = light colour; `ambient[2].rgb` = constant term — "light ranges from ambient[2]-ambient[1] to ambient[2]+ambient[1]." **One directional + one constant + multiplied per-vertex AO on a flat albedo. No shadow map required.**

## Style split worth flagging

https://www.gamedeveloper.com/art/voxelart-styles-in-video-games distinguishes two buckets you should not blend:
- **Pixelated Voxels (Flat Shaded):** "With any form of hard shadows applied the piece and style will be ruined." Minimal shadows means "the style benefits from vibrant colors." (Fugl, Voxelnauts.)
- **Vector Voxel Art:** angled camera, real lighting, 90° bends. (Crossy Road.)

So: "Crossy Road look" = flat normals + directional light + hard shadow. "Pure flat toy look" = flat normals + AO only + **no** directional shadow.

## Export pipeline reality

- **MagicaVoxel OBJ export** ships `.obj` + `.mtl` + a **256×1 PNG palette**, "Each pixel maps to a color in the palette." It merges same-colour adjacent voxels (2×2×2 cube → 12 triangles, the minimum) but **cannot merge across a colour change on the same face** — that is the real triangle driver. A moderately complex character lands ~**1,600 triangles**. — https://luciopaiva.com/magicavoxel-threejs-howto/
- **Qubicle** base edition exports unoptimised OBJ/FBX/Collada; the **Mesh Module DLC** unlocks low-poly mesh optimisation — https://store.steampowered.com/app/454550/Qubicle_Voxel_Editor/
- **Vertex colours beat a texture atlas** for this look: no UVs, no atlas bleed, no mip artefacts at small size, and you can merge many objects into one draw call. Cost: greedy meshing stops at every colour boundary.

---

# D) three.js / WebGL SPECIFICS

**Current release: r186** (`"version": "0.186.0"` — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/package.json). **Docs URLs changed**: the `threejs.org/docs/#api/en/...` hash form is obsolete; canonical is now `https://threejs.org/docs/pages/&lt;ClassName&gt;.html`.

## 1) MeshToonMaterial + gradientMap

Docs https://threejs.org/docs/pages/MeshToonMaterial.html · Source https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/src/materials/MeshToonMaterial.js

The actual GLSL — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/src/renderers/shaders/ShaderChunk/gradientmap_pars_fragment.glsl.js

```glsl
vec3 getGradientIrradiance( vec3 normal, vec3 lightDirection ) {
	// dotNL will be from -1.0 to 1.0
	float dotNL = dot( normal, lightDirection );
	vec2 coord = vec2( dotNL * 0.5 + 0.5, 0.0 );
	#ifdef USE_GRADIENTMAP
		return vec3( texture2D( gradientMap, coord ).r );
	#else
		vec2 fw = fwidth( coord ) * 0.5;
		return mix( vec3( 0.7 ), vec3( 1.0 ), smoothstep( 0.7 - fw.x, 0.7 + fw.x, coord.x ) );
	#endif
}
```

Four load-bearing facts:
1. **`dotNL` is NOT clamped** — it is remapped `[-1,1] → [0,1]`. The **midpoint texel (u=0.5) is the terminator**, and the entire left half of the ramp serves surfaces facing away from the light. This is why a 3-band ramp often looks like 2 bands.
2. **Only the red channel is read**, splatted to `vec3`. It is a monochrome irradiance multiplier, **not a colour LUT** — you cannot get purple shadows this way; tint via `color`/`map`.
3. `coord.y = 0.0` — it is 1×N along u only. Height must be 1.
4. Without a gradientMap the fallback is a hardcoded 2-tone `0.7 → 1.0` step with `fwidth` AA.

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/src/renderers/shaders/ShaderChunk/lights_toon_pars_fragment.glsl.js shows `RE_Direct_Toon` multiplies the ramp by `directLight.color` (so a high-intensity light blows past 1.0 and flattens your bands), and `RE_IndirectDiffuse_Toon` is **plain Lambert — ambient light is not banded**.

**Correction to your assumption: gradientMap must be `NoColorSpace`, NOT `SRGBColorSpace`.** Docs verbatim: "gradientMap represents non-color data. Any texture assigned must have `texture.colorSpace = NoColorSpace` (default)." Since it is the default, the correct action is to **not touch it**. Setting sRGB applies a decode that non-linearly crushes band positions.

Also: **`DataTexture` already defaults both filters to `NearestFilter`** (unlike base `Texture`). Build one like this:

```js
function makeGradientMap( bands ) {
	const data = new Uint8Array( bands );
	for ( let i = 0; i &lt; bands; i ++ ) data[ i ] = Math.round( i / ( bands - 1 ) * 255 );
	const tex = new THREE.DataTexture( data, bands, 1, THREE.RedFormat );
	tex.minFilter = THREE.NearestFilter;
	tex.magFilter = THREE.NearestFilter;
	tex.generateMipmaps = false;
	tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.ClampToEdgeWrapping;
	// colorSpace left at NoColorSpace (default) — non-color data
	tex.needsUpdate = true;
	return tex;
}
```

**Band behaviour, accounting for the remap:**
- **2 texels** — pure lit/unlit split at `dotNL == 0`. Hardest look. This is what GGXrd and Hi-Fi Rush do.
- **3 texels** (`0,128,255`) — texel 0 covers `dotNL ∈ [-1,-0.33]`, texel 1 `[-0.33,0.33]`, texel 2 `[0.33,1]`. Classic cel.
- **5 texels** — approaches a smooth ramp; reads as stylised soft shading rather than cel.
- Because half the texels serve the dark hemisphere, hand-write a non-linear ramp for a strongly-lit look: `new Uint8Array([0,0,0,90,200,255,255,255])` gives a deep flat shadow, tight terminator, flat highlight.

**Shipped gradient maps confirmed present:**
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/examples/textures/gradientMaps/threeTone.jpg
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/examples/textures/gradientMaps/fiveTone.jpg
- Directory: https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/tree/dev/examples/textures/gradientMaps
- They are **JPEGs**, so they carry compression ringing at band edges. Build a `DataTexture` for clean bands.

**Example page: `webgl_materials_variations_toon` no longer exists — it was renamed.** Use **https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_materials_toon** (subtitle "Toon Material with OutlineEffect"), source https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_materials_toon.html. Note the gradient-map loop in that example has two off-by-one bugs (`c &lt;= colors.length` and `/ colors.length * 256`) — use the corrected version above.

## 2) Inverted-hull outline

**`OutlineEffect.js` IS an inverted hull — confirmed.** Path `examples/jsm/effects/OutlineEffect.js` · https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/jsm/effects/OutlineEffect.js · Docs https://threejs.org/docs/pages/OutlineEffect.html · **WebGLRenderer only — will not work with WebGPURenderer.**

`createMaterial()` returns `side: BackSide`, and the vertex shader flips and extrudes:

```glsl
vec4 calculateOutline( vec4 pos, vec3 normal, vec4 skinned ) {
	float thickness = outlineThickness;
	const float ratio = 1.0;
	vec4 pos2 = projectionMatrix * modelViewMatrix * vec4( skinned.xyz + normal, 1.0 );
	vec4 norm = normalize( pos - pos2 );
	return pos + norm * thickness * pos.w * ratio;
}
```

**Thickness space — the important answer: neither view nor world space. It is clip space multiplied by `pos.w`, which cancels the perspective divide, so outline width in NDC is constant regardless of distance.** That is why the default is a tiny `0.003` — it is a fraction of screen height, not a world distance.

Parameters: `defaultThickness 0.003`, `defaultColor [0,0,0]`, `defaultAlpha 1.0`, `defaultKeepAlive false`. Per-material override goes through `userData`, not the constructor:

```js
mesh.material.userData.outlineParameters = { thickness: 0.01, color: [0,0,0], alpha: 1.0, visible: true, keepAlive: true };
```

Two gotchas: `outlineAlpha` is seeded from `originalMaterial.opacity` before overrides, so transparent materials get transparent outlines; and `defaultKeepAlive: false` purges the outline-material cache every frame for off-screen objects, which thrashes shader compilation in scenes with churn.

**Roll-your-own, shortest version** (avoids the per-frame cache entirely, shares geometry so costs no extra VRAM):

```js
function addOutline( mesh, thickness = 0.02, color = 0x000000 ) {
	const mat = new THREE.MeshBasicMaterial( { color, side: THREE.BackSide } );
	mat.onBeforeCompile = ( shader ) =&gt; {
		shader.uniforms.uThickness = { value: thickness };
		shader.vertexShader = 'uniform float uThickness;\n' + shader.vertexShader
			.replace( '#include &lt;begin_vertex&gt;', 'vec3 transformed = position + objectNormal * uThickness;' );
	};
	const outline = new THREE.Mesh( mesh.geometry, mat );
	mesh.add( outline );
	return outline;
}
```
This is **object-space** thickness (shrinks with distance). For constant screen width instead, after `#include &lt;project_vertex&gt;`:
```glsl
vec3 clipNormal = normalize( ( projectionMatrix * vec4( normalMatrix * objectNormal, 0.0 ) ).xyz );
gl_Position.xy += clipNormal.xy * uThickness * gl_Position.w;
```
`objectNormal` only exists after `&lt;beginnormal_vertex&gt;`, which is why replacing `&lt;begin_vertex&gt;` works and anything earlier does not.

**Pitfall list:**
1. **Hard/split normals tear the hull open** — the number one failure. A cube has 3–4 different normals per corner position; extruding each separately pulls corners apart into disconnected quads. Fix: `mergeVertices(geometry)` from https://threejs.org/docs/pages/BufferGeometryUtils.html then `computeVertexNormals()`, and use that geometry **for the outline only** so the beauty pass keeps its hard edges. For crisp silhouettes, store the smoothed normals in a second attribute (`aSmoothNormal`) instead.
2. **Non-uniform scale breaks object-space extrusion** — a mesh scaled `(1,1,5)` gets a 5× thicker outline on one axis. Normalise scale or extrude in view space via `normalMatrix`.
3. **Keep the outline opaque and depth-writing.** `depthWrite: false` or `transparent: true` makes it draw over the object or z-fight.
4. **Open geometry has no backface** — planes and cloth produce no outline. Inverted hull needs a closed manifold.
5. **Multi-mesh glTF outlines every child**, giving internal lines where parts meet. Merge children into one geometry for the outline at load time (merging at selection time is "terribly slow") — https://discourse.threejs.org/t/unsure-of-correct-approach-to-apply-outline-shader/68515
6. **No interior lines.** Inverted hull is silhouette-only. Creases need a post-process — the two are complements, exactly as GGXrd used hull outlines *plus* a separate interior-line technique.
7. **Skinned meshes** must be extruded after skinning or the outline detaches; `OutlineEffect` orders `&lt;skinning_vertex&gt;` before `calculateOutline`.

## 3) RenderPixelatedPass

Source https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/jsm/postprocessing/RenderPixelatedPass.js · Docs https://threejs.org/docs/pages/RenderPixelatedPass.html · Example https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_postprocessing_pixel · Example source https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_postprocessing_pixel.html

```js
constructor( pixelSize, scene, camera, options = {} )
```

| Param | Default | Range in the official example |
|---|---|---|
| `pixelSize` | required | `.min(1).max(16).step(1)` |
| `options.normalEdgeStrength` | `0.3` | `.min(0).max(2).step(0.05)` |
| `options.depthEdgeStrength` | `0.4` | `.min(0).max(1).step(0.05)` |

**Trap:** defaults use `||`, not `??`, so **passing `0` silently becomes the default**. To disable an edge type, set the property after construction. Use `setPixelSize()` at runtime — assigning `.pixelSize` does nothing until the next resize.

Render targets: both `NearestFilter`, both `HalfFloatType`; the beauty target carries a `DepthTexture`; the normal target has none. Sizing is `renderResolution = (width / pixelSize) | 0` (floored), and the `resolution` uniform is a `Vector4` packing `(w, h, 1/w, 1/h)`. The normal buffer comes from `scene.overrideMaterial = new MeshNormalMaterial()` — **view-space normals, encoded `[-1,1] → [0,1]`** — which means **a full second scene draw, so the pass costs 2× geometry throughput**.

**Edge detection, exactly.** 4-tap von Neumann neighbourhood (N/S/E/W, no diagonals), one low-res texel out.

Depth — sums only *positive* differences, so the edge fires on the **near** side of a discontinuity:
```glsl
float depthEdgeIndicator(float depth, vec3 normal) {
	float diff = 0.0;
	diff += clamp(getDepth(1, 0) - depth, 0.0, 1.0);
	diff += clamp(getDepth(-1, 0) - depth, 0.0, 1.0);
	diff += clamp(getDepth(0, 1) - depth, 0.0, 1.0);
	diff += clamp(getDepth(0, -1) - depth, 0.0, 1.0);
	return floor(smoothstep(0.01, 0.02, diff) * 2.) / 2.;
}
```
The tail **quantises to exactly three levels: 0.0, 0.5, 1.0** — deliberate, to avoid anti-aliased grey fringes. The `0.01/0.02` thresholds are on **raw non-linear depth**, tuned for the example's `near: 0.1, far: 10` ortho camera; with a perspective camera and a large far plane, distant edges stop being detected.

Normal — angular difference gated so only one side of a crease lights up:
```glsl
float neighborNormalEdgeIndicator(int x, int y, float depth, vec3 normal) {
	float depthDiff = getDepth(x, y) - depth;
	vec3 neighborNormal = getNormal(x, y);
	vec3 normalEdgeBias = vec3(1., 1., 1.); // This should probably be a parameter.
	float normalDiff = dot(normal - neighborNormal, normalEdgeBias);
	float normalIndicator = clamp(smoothstep(-.01, .01, normalDiff), 0.0, 1.0);
	float depthIndicator = clamp(sign(depthDiff * .25 + .0025), 0.0, 1.0);
	return (1.0 - dot(normal, neighborNormal)) * depthIndicator * normalIndicator;
}
// ...four taps summed, then:
return step(0.1, indicator);   // strictly binary
```
`normalEdgeBias` is **hardcoded to `vec3(1,1,1)`** — the source comment concedes it should be a parameter. It bakes a fixed directional preference into edge highlights, visible as asymmetry when you orbit. Fork the shader if you need symmetry.

Combination — depth edges **darken**, normal edges **lighten**, depth wins ties:
```glsl
float Strength = dei &gt; 0.0 ? (1.0 - depthEdgeStrength * dei) : (1.0 + normalEdgeStrength * nei);
gl_FragColor = texel * Strength;
```
It is a multiply on the existing colour, so hue is preserved — not a flat ink line. That is why `depthEdgeStrength` caps at 1 (reaching black) while `normalEdgeStrength` caps at 2.

**Camera snapping is NOT in the pass — confirmed, zero snapping logic.** Drop it into a project and the pixel grid will swim. All the snapping lives in the example HTML and you must port it. The key function shifts the **frustum** by the sub-pixel remainder rather than quantising the camera position, so motion stays smooth while the grid stays locked to world space (ortho only):

```js
function pixelAlignFrustum( camera, aspectRatio, pixelsPerScreenWidth, pixelsPerScreenHeight ) {
  const worldScreenWidth  = ( camera.right - camera.left ) / camera.zoom;
  const worldScreenHeight = ( camera.top - camera.bottom ) / camera.zoom;
  const pixelWidth  = worldScreenWidth  / pixelsPerScreenWidth;
  const pixelHeight = worldScreenHeight / pixelsPerScreenHeight;
  const camPos = new THREE.Vector3(); camera.getWorldPosition( camPos );
  const camRot = new THREE.Quaternion(); camera.getWorldQuaternion( camRot );
  const camRight = new THREE.Vector3( 1, 0, 0 ).applyQuaternion( camRot );
  const camUp    = new THREE.Vector3( 0, 1, 0 ).applyQuaternion( camRot );
  const fractX = camPos.dot( camRight ) / pixelWidth  - Math.round( camPos.dot( camRight ) / pixelWidth );
  const fractY = camPos.dot( camUp )    / pixelHeight - Math.round( camPos.dot( camUp )    / pixelHeight );
  camera.left   = - aspectRatio - ( fractX * pixelWidth );
  camera.right  =   aspectRatio - ( fractX * pixelWidth );
  camera.top    =   1.0 - ( fractY * pixelHeight );
  camera.bottom = - 1.0 - ( fractY * pixelHeight );
  camera.updateProjectionMatrix();
}
```
This is exactly the "snap then shift back by the snap error" technique from David Holland and yal.cc, expressed as a frustum offset.

Plus `pixelAlignCamera()`, which rounds spherical `theta`/`phi` to a rotation increment and `zoom` to a zoom increment. Example GUI defaults: `snapping: true, pixelAlignedPanning: true, pixelAlignedObjects: true, rotationSnap: 15, cameraRotationSnap: 9, cameraZoomSnap: 0.1`.

The animate loop uses **save → mutate → render → restore**, which is the part most people miss: snapped values are used only for the render, then reverted so `OrbitControls` keeps operating on smooth state.

Its texture helper is the counterpoint to gradient maps — colour maps **do** want sRGB:
```js
function pixelTexture( texture ) {
  texture.minFilter = THREE.NearestFilter;
  texture.magFilter = THREE.NearestFilter;
  texture.generateMipmaps = false;
  texture.colorSpace = THREE.SRGBColorSpace;
  return texture;
}
```

## 4) Other examples and low-res rendering

| Example | Page | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Toon + OutlineEffect | https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_materials_toon | https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_materials_toon.html |
| Pixelated pass | https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_postprocessing_pixel | https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_postprocessing_pixel.html |
| Sobel edge | https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_postprocessing_sobel | https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_postprocessing_sobel.html |
| Selective outline (OutlinePass) | https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_postprocessing_outline | https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_postprocessing_outline.html |

There is no `webgl_postprocessing_edge` and no `cel` example. WebGPU siblings exist for all four. **Sobel is a luminance edge detector** — it traces texture detail and lighting gradients as well as geometry, so it is the wrong tool for clean cel outlines; the depth+normal approach is geometrically correct. Its `resolution` uniform must be updated on resize or edges get the wrong width.

`EffectComposer` — https://threejs.org/docs/pages/EffectComposer.html · `OrthographicCamera` — https://threejs.org/docs/pages/OrthographicCamera.html (ortho is effectively mandatory; `pixelAlignFrustum` has no perspective equivalent).

**Low-res rendering — two approaches, different jobs:**

(a) **Low-res canvas + CSS upscale.** Cheapest, but pixelates all canvas output and gives you no depth/normal buffers for edge detection.
```js
renderer.setPixelRatio( 1 );  // otherwise retina silently undoes the effect
renderer.setSize( window.innerWidth / SCALE, window.innerHeight / SCALE, false );
```
The `false` is load-bearing — docs: "Setting `updateStyle` to false prevents any style changes to the output canvas," so the backing store shrinks while CSS size stays. Pair with `canvas { image-rendering: pixelated; }`.

(b) **`WebGLRenderTarget` at low resolution + NearestFilter** — required for any edge detection. This is what `RenderPixelatedPass` does internally.
```js
const rt = new THREE.WebGLRenderTarget( width / SCALE, height / SCALE, {
	minFilter: THREE.NearestFilter,   // MUST override — default is LinearFilter
	magFilter: THREE.NearestFilter,   // the one that controls the upscale
	type: THREE.HalfFloatType,
	depthBuffer: true
} );
```
Two traps: **`minFilter` defaults to `LinearFilter`** on render targets (unlike `DataTexture`), and **`samples` defaults to 0 — leave it there**, MSAA anti-aliases exactly the hard pixel edges you want. Docs https://threejs.org/docs/pages/WebGLRenderTarget.html

**Flat shading in three.js costs nothing geometrically.** three.js does not split vertices; it derives the face normal from screen-space derivatives:
```glsl
#ifdef FLAT_SHADED
	vec3 fdx = dFdx( vViewPosition );
	vec3 fdy = dFdy( vViewPosition );
	vec3 normal = normalize( cross( fdx, fdy ) );
#endif
```
https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/src/renderers/shaders/ShaderChunk/normal_fragment_begin.glsl.js — so **you do not need `toNonIndexed()` + `computeVertexNormals()`**. Just set `flatShading: true`.

**`VOXLoader` already does greedy meshing** (`buildMesh()`; `VOXMesh` is deprecated) — https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/jsm/loaders/VOXLoader.js. It sweeps 3 axes, emits a face only when exactly one side is solid, greedy-merges the mask keyed on colour index, and outputs `position` + `index` + a per-quad `color` attribute converted with `SRGBColorSpace`, then `computeVertexNormals()` + `MeshStandardMaterial`. Because each quad owns its four vertices, normals come out flat regardless.

## 2026-09-13T13:41:17.884Z — assistant (source line 1661)

Here is how I read the three references and what I'm building for each. In-engine renders are being produced now; they'll land on the style board as "Round 2" so you can compare them side by side with the reference.

**A. Chunky outlined tactics** (the green isometric one). Big pixels, saturated flat colours, a near-black outline on every object *and* on the tile edges, flat terrace blocks, even light, no shadows.
- Painted voxels, 3-px pixels, 2-band shading, outlined tiles.
- Painted sprites with a thick outline on the same outlined board.
- Same as the first, plus a saturated 32-colour palette to push the flat look.

**B. Painterly dungeon** (the dark stone one). Small pixels, painterly shading, no outlines, textured stone floor with a visible grid, dark ambient with warm torch lights, high top-down camera.
- Painted sprites on stone tiles from your Drive `board/` folder, torch lights, dark mood.
- Painted voxels on the same board and lights.
- The stone board under our bright paper lighting, so it fits the white/black UI you asked for.

**C. Iso pixel** (the desert GIF, Pathway-like). True 45° isometric, warm limited palette, dithered ground, thin outlines only on silhouettes, chunky terrain blocks with side faces.
- Painted voxels, iso camera, warm palette, outline.
- Painted sprites, iso camera, warm palette.
- Painted voxels, iso camera, no palette (cleaner colours).

A trade-off to keep in mind: B's dark mood conflicts with the bright minimal UI, which is why I added the bright variant. C's 30° camera brings back some overlap, which the outline and palette must carry.

Also done since last update: the default camera now sits at ~54°, so pieces no longer cover the row behind them, and the painted blue/red sprites are the default sprite set. Still running: the voxel painting for Cel, and three Opus reviews of the Drive folders (art stuff, board, cards).

<result>Done. Dev server stopped, temp probe removed.

## Knobs added to `Style` (`src/render/styles.ts`)
`camera {elev,azim,zoom}` (degrees; drives the initial pose, `resetView`, `flip`), `tiles 'flat'|'edged'|'stone'`, `lights 'bright'|'torch'`, `paletteName 'db32'|'endesga32'|'warm'`, `outlineColor`, `rim` (art px → world via frustum/canvas/zoom), `spriteOutline`, `boardSide`, `shadow`. Renderer changes are all data-driven (`applyBoard`/`applyLights`/`applyCamera` in `applyStyle`); no fork. `edged` = 32×32 nearest canvas texture with a 1-texel ring; `stone` = the Drive JPGs at 256² (9–30 kB) in `public/textures/`; `spriteOutline` = 8-way alpha dilation into a canvas at texture-load time (`src/render/sprites.ts`); `shadow` = one shared radial-gradient disc. `ENDESGA32` + `WARM32` added to `palette.ts`; `BoardRenderer.screenOf(sq)` added so the shot tool can aim crops.

## Presets (all nine in the Style select and `?style=`)
- **A** `chunkyVoxel` px3 / 2-band toon / edged tiles / rim 1 · `chunkySprite` px3 / edged / dilated sprite outline · `chunkyPalette` = chunkyVoxel + Endesga-32.
- **B** (all elev 60, stone tiles, shadows) `dungeonSprite` px2 torch · `dungeonVoxel` px2 torch · `dungeonBright` px2 bright rig.
- **C** (all elev 30 / azim 45 / zoom 0.85, boardSide 0.8, shadows) `isoVoxel` warm palette + rim · `isoSprite` warm palette + sprite outline · `isoClean` no palette + rim.

## Screenshots — `docs/styleboard/img/r2-&lt;key&gt;.png` + `-crop.png`, manifest `docs/styleboard/round2.json`
Look right: **chunkySprite, dungeonSprite, dungeonBright, isoSprite, isoVoxel, isoClean**. The tile ring reads in A, the torch glow + stone read in B, and C is a proper 45° diamond with side faces (first pass clipped both edges at zoom 1.15 — fixed to 0.85 and re-shot).
Weak but not broken: **chunkyVoxel/chunkyPalette** (2-band toon under the bright rig clips the white army to flat cream) and **dungeonVoxel** (at 60° the pieces read as discs). `chunkyPalette` also loses the tile ring on dark squares — Endesga-32 maps ring and tile to the same navy; noted in its blurb.

## Not done / caveats
- The painted voxel models (`public/models/*.json`, rewritten at 06:37 — they do carry `accent`/`shade`, so they are painted, not monochrome) are squat with oversized round bases; every voxel preset reads as a blob from above. I confirmed this is upstream, not my change: the untouched `cel` preset shows the same shapes. The C presets at 30° are the only ones that show the sculpts in profile.
- The inverted hull on voxel pieces comes out speckled rather than solid (occupancy-gradient normals tear it); I left `rim` at 1 art px rather than thickening the tear.
- Left alone deliberately: light intensities. Dropping them would make 2-band toon band properly, but it would visibly change the five existing presets. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx vitest run` are green, and cel/hd/voxel/db32/sprites re-render identically.</result>

## 2026-09-13T14:11:46.979Z — assistant (source line 1908)

The nine options are live. Contact sheet is above; the full renders with crops sit next to each reference on the style board, and every option can be played in-engine.

- Style board (round 2 at the top): https://claude.ai/code/artifact/b4800dc6-5092-4468-b637-c0a3c7da0236
- Game, any option via the Style select or `?style=` — for example https://claude.ai/code/artifact/7acdadad-e068-4a2e-a6a3-1635a2c3b1da?style=isoSprite

**A. Chunky outlined** (row 1)
- A1 Chunky voxel: 3-px pixels, two shading bands, dark ring on every tile. Flattest, but the sculpts turn into discs from above.
- A2 Chunky sprite: painted sprites with a thick outline on the ringed tiles. Crispest silhouettes of the nine.
- A3 Chunky + Endesga-32: A1 quantised to a poster palette. Unified, but the tile ring vanishes on dark squares.

**B. Painterly dungeon** (row 2)
- B1 Dungeon sprite: stone tiles from your Drive board folder, four warm torches, contact shadows. Most atmospheric; far corners go dark.
- B2 Dungeon voxel: same board with the sculpts. They catch the light but read as blobs at 60°.
- B3 Dungeon bright: the stone board under our bright paper lighting. Keeps the textured floor and the white/black UI.

**C. Iso pixel** (row 3)
- C1 Iso voxel: true 45°/30° isometric, tall board block, warm 32-colour ramp, outline. Best sense of place, and the only camera that shows the sculpts in profile.
- C2 Iso sprite: outlined painted sprites on the same iso board. Closest to the reference.
- C3 Iso clean: iso without the palette, so the painted colours survive.

My pick: **C2 Iso sprite** or **A2 Chunky sprite** for readability, with C1 if you prefer the 3D sculpts. The from-above voxel options (A1, A3, B2) will stay weak until the sculpts' round base discs are trimmed, which I can do next if you choose a voxel direction.

## 2026-09-13T14:16:35.298Z — assistant (source line 1916)

Both kinds are there. The board is 3D in all nine options; the pieces differ:

- **3D voxel models** (built from your STL sculpts, 36 voxels tall, painted from the colour guides): A1, A3, B2, C1, C3. They turn with the camera when you orbit.
- **2D sprites** (billboards cut from the PSD renders, painted from the colour guides): A2, B1, B3, C2. They always face the camera, so orbiting changes the board but not the piece drawing.

The 3D ones currently read worse from above because the sculpts carry a large round base disc and the top-down cameras flatten them. C1 and C3 show them in profile, which is why those two read best among the 3D options.

## 2026-09-13T14:29:02.914Z — assistant (source line 1975)

B2 is now the target; three Opus agents are building in parallel on disjoint files. What the game still needs, and what's being done now:

**In progress (this round)**
- **Look**: B2 becomes the default style, the sculpts' base discs get trimmed so pieces stop reading as blobs from above, camera lowered to ~52°, torch ambient lifted so corners stay readable, board coordinates on the frame.
- **Game loop**: undo/takeback, game-over dialog with Rematch and New game, resign, autosave and restore on reload, captured-pieces rows, a piece info card on select, copy moves, Esc to cancel.
- **AI**: real evaluation (piece-square tables for all 11 pieces, mobility, king safety), transposition table, better move ordering, quiescence pruning, mate-distance scoring, time management, plus mate puzzles and a self-play test to catch rule bugs.

**After that, still open**
- A full game vs the AI end to end in the browser, then republish as v0.5.
- Variant config in the engine so kings' powers and card effects can be switched on per game (Phase 2).
- Sound effects, a tutorial for the fairy pieces, and online play are unscheduled.
- Drive: the concept-art folder is still downloading; once reviewed, I curate the keepers into `art-src/` and delete the raw download.

I'll report when the three builds land and the game is republished.

## 2026-09-13T14:43:24.293Z — assistant (source line 2012)

Eight Opus agents are running now. Plan is written to docs/SIM-PLAN.md and tracked in TASKS.md (Phase 1d).

**Research (4 agents, reports to docs/research/)**
- `chess960.md`: FIDE rules, the 960 positions, published engine and human balance data per position, patterns behind interesting vs drawish setups, no-castling and other variant findings (including DeepMind's AlphaZero balance study).
- `variant-balance.md`: how designers and engine authors measure balance, formal "interest" metrics (Browne's drama, lead change, uncertainty, decisiveness, etc.), statistics for AI-vs-AI testing, a proposed fun score with initial weights.
- `fairy-values.md`: closest known analogues of Archer, Paladin, Guard, Maester, Beast, published value methods, first estimates in pawn units, hypotheses to test.
- `sim-methodology.md`: cutechess/fastchess/SPRT formulas ready to port, whether Fairy-Stockfish can cross-check any piece, successive-halving experiment design, record schema.

**Balance lab stage 1 (1 agent, building now)**
- Headless runner in `src/sim/` with a 16-worker pool, seeded, resumable JSONL records per game (moves, per-ply evals, captures, fairy events, end reason), an analyzer producing balance (Wilson CI, Elo), decisiveness, length, per-piece stats, eval-trajectory metrics and a first fun score, ranked per back rank. Run with `npm run sim`, then a 200-game smoke run.

**Stage 2, after the AI upgrade lands**: a `Rules` config in the engine so every rule toggle (archer checks, beast chains, guard immunity, maester swaps, bishop colours, promotion set) can be A/B tested, then the four experiments in the plan: piece-value swaps, a 500-arrangement sweep with successive halving, rule toggles, and a results dashboard.

Meanwhile the three game builds (B2 look, game loop UX, AI tier 2) continue.

<result>Written: `/Users/za/Documents/king down chess/docs/research/sim-methodology.md` (220 lines, 31 cited sources). No other file touched.

**Summary (10 lines)**

1. Engine-match tools (cutechess-cli, fastchess, c-chess-cli, OpenBench, Fishtest) all reduce to four things we need: paired games, pentanomial counts, SPRT, and adjudication rules — the rest is a UCI harness we cannot use.
2. All formulas are captured as runnable TypeScript pseudocode: Elo from score, Wilson intervals, LOS, pentanomial `sigma_pg = sqrt(2*var)`, normalized Elo `C = 800/ln10 = 347.44`, and the GSPRT approximation (4.14) with its clamp.
3. The budget formula is the key result: worst-case SPRT length is `1,046,535 / (nElo1 − nElo0)²` games, so a ±2 nElo test costs ~262k games and a ±5 test ~42k.
4. Error bars: `err95 ≈ 694.9 · z · sigma_pg / √games`, i.e. ±28 Elo at 400 games with a 30% draw rate — SIM-PLAN's 400-game piece-value runs cannot resolve a half-pawn.
5. Fairy-Stockfish cannot express the pieces that matter: no rifle capture (Archer), no friendly-hopping slider or self-destruct (Paladin), no capture immunity (Guard), no swap (Maester), no capture chains (Beast). Nearest Betza forms are listed; use it only as a standard-piece control.
6. Three real repeatability defects found in `src/ai/search.ts`: `timeMs` defaults to 1000 ms so `maxDepth` runs still stop on the wall clock; the TT/history/killers are module-level and never cleared, so game *n* depends on games 1..*n*−1 in the same worker; `src/ai/worker.ts` is a browser Worker, not a `worker_threads` one.
7. Recommended protocol: paired games with colours swapped, fixed depth with `timeMs: Infinity`, Fishtest adjudication (resign 600/3, draw 20/8 after move 34), SPRT for experiments 2 and 4, successive halving with η=2 and common random numbers for experiment 3.
8. Metric set taken from Ludii's actual source tree (Balance, Drama, LeadChange, Stability, Decisiveness, Clarity, BoardCoverage, BranchingFactor, Duration, Repetition, SkillTrace), plus a cheap depth-2-vs-depth-4 strategic-depth probe.
9. Record schema is a JSONL line per game with typed-array evals/branching/material, fairy counters and a replayable seed; compact to Parquet with DuckDB after each run, the same split Lichess uses.
10. Ten concrete SIM-PLAN changes are listed at the end, the load-bearing ones being: pair every game, replace fixed game counts with SPRT plus a cap, fix the "depth 3" determinism claim, and drop external match tools in favour of ~80 lines of ported statistics.</result>

## 2026-09-13T14:57:17.398Z — assistant (source line 2087)

Chess960 research is in (docs/research/chess960.md), with one finding that changes the plan: in the 100M-game Stockfish dataset, low draw rates correlate almost perfectly with a White advantage (r = −0.92), so "decisive" positions are mostly unfair ones, not fighting ones. The fun score will have to separate balance from decisiveness (for example, count positions where both colours win often). Other usable inputs: per-position datasets ready to download, the AlphaZero no-castling result (decisive games ×1.3, rook worth ~11% less), and twelve hypotheses phrased for our runner. Also passed the methodology findings to the AI and sim-lab builders (deterministic fixed-depth search, resettable tables, colour-swapped game pairs, adjudication defaults).

<result>`docs/research/balancing-frameworks.md` is complete — 220 lines, 39 cited sources, all seven sections (sources, systems, comparison table, research, industry practice, tooling, recommendations). No other file touched.

Summary (10 lines):

1. **Ludii is the prize and the trap.** It is the only system with a finished, named balance/quality metric set, and it publishes computed values per game (De Vasa Chess: Balance 0.78, Drawishness 0.02, BranchingFactorAverage 51.48). Its licence is CC BY-NC-ND 4.0, so we may run it but never ship or fork it.
2. I read the exact metric class names out of the Ludii source tree: `Balance`, `AdvantageP1`, `Drawishness`, `Completion`, `Timeouts`, `LeadChange`, `Stability`, `Drama`, `DecisivenessMoves/Threshold`, `BranchingFactor`, `DecisionFactor`, `MoveDistance`, `PieceNumber`, `SkillTrace`, `IdealDuration`. Port the definitions from Browne's papers, not from the source.
3. Ludii's per-ply reduction pattern (average, change, max, min, variance of any timeline) is the cheapest structural idea in the whole review — one helper in `analyze.ts`.
4. **No framework is worth hosting our rules in.** OpenSpiel (Apache-2.0) is the only legally usable one, but it costs a second rules implementation and ships no game-quality metrics. RBG, GDL, boardgame.io and Zillions add nothing; Polygames is archived.
5. Ludii could probably express all five fairy pieces (`moveAgain` for the beast chain, swap ludemes for the maester), but we did not verify it, and it is moot given the licence.
6. **Research defines balance as a 50% win rate almost everywhere**, and always adds a second objective because balance alone selects dull games. Published budgets are small — 10,000 games per evaluation is a large number in this literature, so our sample size will exceed the state of the art; our risk is agent quality.
7. **Restricted play (Jaffe 2012) is the most transferable idea**: handicap one agent and measure how much it loses. It answers "does this back rank reward skill", which no win rate can. Ludii's `SkillTrace` is the same idea.
8. **Industry practice transfers in two ways only.** Riot's 47–53% band and Supercell's 5–15% usage band show that a near-50% win rate plus a dominant usage share is still a balance failure. We have no pick rate, so piece usage (moves/captures/survival per piece type, already in SIM-PLAN §2) is the over-centralisation signal, and the matchup matrix is arrangement-vs-arrangement with the mirror diagonal as the fairness test.
9. **Almost every library is a formula we already have.** Elo-from-score, Wilson and pentanomial variance are lines, not dependencies (`sim-methodology.md` §2 has them). The one library worth installing is Python `choix` (Bradley-Terry) if we rank more than ~100 arrangements; Observable Plot for the report.
10. Two conflicts with existing plans, flagged in the doc: report balance and interest as two axes as well as the weighted "fun score" (SIM-PLAN §3), and confirm every headline result at a second search depth, because balance is agent-dependent.</result>

<result>Done. Final report:

**Eval** (`src/ai/eval.ts`, side-to-move relative): material P100 N320 B330 R500 Q900, **archer 430** (rifle capture ignores blockers and defenders, no recapture square), **paladin 470** (queen lines, but dies on capture and never checks), **guard 250** (uncapturable wall, zero offence), **maester 330** (commoner + king swap), **beast 350** (chains in crowded positions, stuck in open ones) — all named constants with the reasoning in the file header. PSTs for all 11 types, mirrored via `s ^ 56`, file-symmetric (random back rank), king MG/EG blended by non-pawn material. Plus slider mobility (B×4 R×3 Q×2 L×2; the paladin ray walks through friends), adjacent beast targets, king shield (pawn +12, guard +26), maester-near-king, tempo 10.

**Search** (`src/ai/search.ts`, 412 lines): iterative deepening + PVS, Zobrist TT (131k entries: key/depth/bound/best move, `src/ai/zobrist.ts`, 52-bit exact-double keys, no BigInt), ordering TT→MVV-LVA→2 killers→history, quiescence with stand-pat + delta pruning + full check evasions, mate-distance scores and pruning, 1-ply check extension capped at 2× nominal depth, repetition (search path + optional `opts.history` of `positionKey()` values) and 50-move draws, soft/hard time budget. In-place `apply`/`undo` on one scratch board with an incremental hash; `engine.ts` untouched.

**Repeatability (coordinator's three items, all in)**: `search(pos, { maxDepth: n })` with `timeMs` omitted sets the budget to `Infinity` — no clock, no early stop; call the new `resetSearchState()` first and the result depends only on (position, depth). No `Math.random`/`Date` anywhere (Zobrist = fixed-seed xorshift); only `performance.now()`, so `search` imports into plain Node; `worker.ts` is unchanged.

**Benchmark** (1 s, best/median of 10 runs, machine shared with other agents at load ~35): start position `RSAKGQLB` depth **4 → 6–7**, 129k → 191k nodes/s median (184k → 337k peak); middlegame depth **4 → 6–7**, 114k → 174k median (152k → 284k peak). Measured finding: `makeMove`'s allocation is *not* the bottleneck (`engine.perft(4)` 3.03M nps vs in-place 2.57M) — tier 1 lost its time to `ordered()` building three arrays per node. Hashing inside the legality probe made the first in-place version *slower* than the engine; splitting out a hash-free `applyQuiet` took the search from 137k to 337k nps. Numbers and method are in `docs/research/engine-ai.md` under "Tier 2 results (2026-09-13)".

**Tests** (`src/ai/search.test.ts`, 11): mates in one with a queen / with an archer (unblockable check) / mates in two and prefers the shorter mate / wins a beast chain instead of a single capture; leaves a king-defended pawn alone / moves an attacked queen somewhere safe / takes a free rook; evaluation is mirror-symmetric; avoids a repetition while winning; ignores the clock and repeats itself exactly after `resetSearchState`; 40 self-play games × ≤120 plies at 15 ms with every move checked against `legalMoves` (~72 s, 300 s timeout).

**Green**: `npx tsc --noEmit` exit 0, `npx vitest run` 53/53 across 4 files (including `src/sim/sim.test.ts` — my API change did not break it; `search`'s signature and result shape are unchanged, `history`/`resetSearchState` are additive).

**Skipped**: aspiration windows, null-move pruning (guards and paladins make zugzwang cheap), LMR, SEE. Values and weights are reasoned, not self-play tuned — that is the next cheap win, and the sim lab can now drive it with `resetSearchState()` + fixed depth.</result>

<result>Stage 1 of the balance lab is built, green, and has a smoke run on disk.

**Files** (all under `/Users/za/Documents/king down chess/src/sim/`): `rng.ts` (mulberry32), `weights.ts` (funScore weights + cp thresholds), `spec.ts` (RunSpec, CLI flags, back-rank sampler, pairing, Fishtest adjudication, `OUT_DIR`/`paths`), `game.ts` (one headless game + recorder), `worker.ts`, `run.ts` (worker pool, JSONL, progress, summary, resume), `analyze.ts` (report), `sim.test.ts` (15 tests). Also: `package.json` (+`tsx`, `@types/node`, `sim`/`sim:analyze` scripts), `tsconfig.json` (`types: [... , "node"]`), `.gitignore` (+`sim/`).

**Run:** `npm run sim -- --id smoke --games 200 --sample 50 --depth 3 --seed 1` then `npm run sim:analyze -- --id smoke`. tsx workers run TS with extensionless imports out of the box; no fallback needed. Vite never reaches `src/sim`.

**Smoke (200 games, depth 3, 50 back ranks, 16 workers): 8.8 games/s (22.6 s total), mean 123.5 plies (median 104, sd 65.6).** Reasons: adjudicatedResign 138, adjudicatedDraw 34, draw50 14, drawRepetition 7, plyCap 5, stalemate 2. White 0.540 (Wilson 0.471–0.608), +28 Elo ±40, decisiveness 69%, LOS 91.4%.

**Two engine findings you should route to the rules/AI agents:**
1. **Bug in `src/rules/engine.ts` — archers capture the king.** `genPiece` case `A` calls `leaper(..., ORTHO, ...)`, which emits a *displacement* capture on orthogonally adjacent enemies, but `isAttacked` models archers only via `ARCHER_SHOTS` (diagonal-adjacent + 2-orthogonal). So a king may legally stand beside an archer and be captured; the game then continues kingless (no mate is ever detectable). Hit 9 of 400 kings in the smoke. `docs/RULES.md` §3 says the archer captures "without moving", so the fix is a move-only leaper for `A`. I did not touch `src/rules/`.
2. Not passing `SearchOptions.history` cost 55% repetition draws; feeding it (guarded by `typeof`) moved decisiveness 36.5% → 69.0%.

**Coordinator guidance folded in:** `pairId`/`colourSwapped` + pentanomial/LOS/nElo/err95; `timeMs: Infinity` for fixed depth; guarded `resetSearchState()`/`positionKey` (both now exist and fire — same seed gives byte-identical games at 1 vs 4 workers); Fishtest adjudication defaults (600/3, 20/8 after ply 68) with flags; Ludii metrics drama, stability, boardCoverage, branchingFactor, duration±sd; schema renamed to the doc's names.

**Two deliberate deviations, both announced by the runner at startup:** colour-swapped pairs default **off** when both sides are identical — with a deterministic search and a mirrored back rank the swapped game replays the first move-for-move, halving the sample for nothing (`--pairs` forces them on; they auto-enable for asymmetric ranks or unequal AI settings). And `openingRandomPlies` defaults to **4** for the same reason.

**Skipped:** base64 typed-array packing for evals/material (records are plain JSON, ~6 kB/game — add when a run exceeds ~100k games); per-ply material timeline; SPRT/LLR (stage 2 needs it, the formulas are in the doc).</result>

**SIM-PLAN v2** (194 lines, Simplified Technical English) — rewritten around ten corrections to v1: colour-reversed pairs + pentanomial, SPRT with the GSPRT (4.14) LLR and the `1046535/Δn_Elo²` length rule, stated adjudication (600/3, 20/8 after move 34) recorded as a flag, capped games as their own class, Wilson only on true rates, **two axes reported apart** (balance vs Browne interest with the fitted signs: killer +0.20, fairy use +0.20, **lead change −0.16**, uncertainty-late +0.13 and top of the ablation, xDec +0.12, drama +0.12, permanence +0.07), decisiveness scored as the **residual after |White−0.5|** (Chess960 corr = −0.92), gates as reject-not-score, restricted play (depth 2 vs 4, 65% floor), Muller fixed-point piece values, static screen → playout pre-filter → successive halving budget table, MultiPV=2 decision cost, second-depth confirmation, and an out-of-scope table.

**Rules design** — `src/rules/rules.ts`: 12 fields, all defaulting to today's game, held in **one module-level object with `setRules()`**, not a `Position` field. Reason (documented in the file header): `canCapture`/`isAttacked`/`genPiece` take a bare `Uint8Array`, so a field would need a new parameter on them and on every caller including `src/game.ts` and `src/render/*`, which I must not touch; one stable object keeps property loads monomorphic and FEN round-trips untouched. Cost: one rule set per thread (fine — each sim worker is its own module instance; the browser never calls it). I **proved** default behaviour is unchanged: against a reconstructed pre-change engine, 4000 random boards / 266,659 moves / 3 gen modes / `isAttacked` on every square / `status` / `insufficientMaterial` are **identical**, and 240 depth-3 searches return the same move, score and node count. 54 old tests pass unchanged; 25 new tests added (79 total). `threefold` is honoured by the sim runner (`applyDrawRules`) because `Game` owns repetition.

**Commands** (all real, all run): `npm run sim -- --id smoke --games 200 --depth 3 --sample 50 --seed 1`; `--experiment values --games 200 --depth 3`; `--experiment sweep --arrangements 100 --rounds 2 --games 20`; `--experiment ab --rule beastChains=false --games 200 --sample 20`; `npm run sim:analyze -- --id smoke --confirm smoke-d4`. `--rule name=value` is repeatable on any run; `--multiPv` turns on decision cost.

**Results** (`docs/research/sim-results-2026-09-13.md`; 11,400 games, ~20 min, 8.4–9.4 games/s at depth 3): smoke White **0.542 (95% 0.485–0.600), +30 ± 40 Elo**, decisive 68.5% / draws 27.5% / capped 4.0%; at depth 4 White 0.545, draws 32.5% — White's edge is stable, draws rise with depth. Sweep: round 1 White score mean 0.545 ± 0.085 over 100 ranks (0.325 `GRNRAKAN` to 0.800 `BNMKGMLA`), round 2 0.516 ± 0.062 over 50 (0.375 to 0.688) — **both spreads are smaller than pure sampling noise (0.092 and 0.065), so the extremes are noise, not arrangements**; that sizes the real run rather than answering it. Piece values (100 pairs each, vs a knight): archer **−85 ± 45**, paladin +26 ± 48, guard **−200 ± 38**, maester +16 ± 40, beast **−179 ± 43** Elo; pawn-odds calibration over all eight files gives **1 pawn = 56 ± 25 Elo**, so implied values are L 3.67 ± 0.86, M 3.48 ± 0.71 (both matching the priors 4.0 and 3.5, against seeds 470 and 330), and A / G / S all **below 1.70** but outside Muller's ±1.5-pawn linear band, so they read as "far below a knight", not as numbers (seeds 430 / 250 / 350, priors 3.5 / 2.0 / 2.2). Rule A/B `beastChains=false`: games **+6.3 ± 5.9 plies**, branching **−0.3 ± 0.3**, balance and every interest term unchanged → the chain is flavour, as H7 predicted.

<result>Done. Final verification is clean: `npx tsc --noEmit` passes, `npx vitest run` shows 80 passed (79 original + 1 new).

**Pass-2 seeds (step 1).** L 370, M 350 = the pass-1 measurements. A 260, G 185, S 195 = midpoint of the research prior and the linear-band floor (knight − 1.5 = 1.70 pawns), which is the lowest value pass 1 can actually assert. That rule gives 185/195 for guard/beast rather than your illustrative 170/190 — 15 and 5 cp, well inside the resolution.

**Pass 2, depth 3** (500 games/arm, 1500 calibration; N from `463700·(1−d)/elo95²` with d≈0.15 → 438, rounded to 500; every bar landed ≤ ±29): archer **−134 ± 26**, paladin **+0 ± 29**, guard **−202 ± 26**, maester **+5 ± 28**, beast **−220 ± 23** Elo. One pawn = **64 ± 16 Elo**. Implied: L 3.20 ± 0.46, M 3.28 ± 0.43; A/G/S all bounded at **&lt; 1.70 pawns** (points −2.08/−3.14/−3.41 below a knight, outside the band).

**Depth 4** (120/arm): archer −117 ± 51, guard −241 ± 51, beast −199 ± 49. All three signs and sizes hold. The depth-4 pawn calibration did **not** resolve (30 ± 33 Elo at 360 games), so depth-4 rows are quoted in Elo only.

**Material regression** (new `sim:analyze --values`, `tanh(w·Δcounts + b)`, 23 220 games, bootstrap over pairs): A **1.09** [0.52–1.45], L **3.21** [2.89–3.52], G **−0.26** [−1.21–0.37], M **3.33** [2.96–3.74], S **−0.22** [−1.06–0.39]. It agrees with the odds match and does **not** rescue the bounds — `atanh(2μ−1)` is half the logit and Elo is the logit scaled, so the tanh link and the Elo conversion are the same transform. It also only identifies `w_X − w_N` and `w_P`; N/B/R/Q never differ at the start of any recorded game.

**Convergence.** Guard, maester, beast each moved less than their bar in pass 2 → converged, not re-seeded. Archer and paladin moved more, so pass 3 re-seeded those two only (A 170, L 320) and replayed their arms + calibration: archer **−139 ± 26** (was −134 at seed 260, −85 at 430 — the arm is nearly blind to its own seed over a 260 cp range, so the bound is the answer), paladin **−9 ± 29** → 3.06 ± 0.44, moved 0.14. All five converged. **Final constants, now in eval.ts: `ARCHER_V = 170, PALADIN_V = 320, GUARD_V = 185, MAESTER_V = 350, BEAST_V = 195`** — measurements for L and M, the band floor (largest value the data allows) for A/G/S, because pricing them at the extrapolation would make the engine give them away.

**Sweep** (60 ranks, 2 rounds, 80 → 160 games; σ = 0.41 → N ≥ 105 for sd ≤ 0.04; measured per-rank sd **0.031** at round 2, split-half 0.028). First round in this project where between-rank spread exceeds noise: 0.0335 across ranks vs 0.0308 sampling → **0.013 of real spread (~9 Elo)**. Real candidates (|score − 0.5| &gt; 2 sd): `MGKRGRNB` 0.588±0.029, `MQKASSRL` 0.584±0.032, `BKMLQRGA` 0.578±0.034, `ARLKBANN` 0.569±0.032 — all favour White, all fail the balance gate. Caveats are bigger than the effect: the pool's own White edge is 0.522 so 0.5 is the wrong null (only the first clearly survives, the second is borderline); round 2 is the half *selected* for balance in round 1; 30 ranks at 2 sd expect ~1.4 false positives; 160 games is ±42 Elo. Interest does separate (per-rank sd 0.008 vs 0.050 across ranks): top `NAGQKGBM` 0.503, `AKNNARLQ` 0.486, `KQMNGLAB` 0.486, `GRMGKNBB` 0.485, `MGKRGRNB` 0.475; bottom `AGSMRSMK` 0.337, `LGKBSMGR` 0.338, `SKANSGGR` 0.339, `MNABAKSM` 0.339, `SAMNGBKR` 0.364. Warning: the bottom five all draw 41–51 %, so the interest axis is currently mostly a draw-rate axis.

**Test change.** `search.test.ts` "evaluation is mirror-symmetric" now allows 1 cp instead of exact equality. Cause is not the values: the king term blends MG/EG by a fractional phase, so the total is a float summed in a different square order on the mirrored board, and the new values put `MID` exactly on a half-centipawn. Fixing it properly (round each king term, or use an integer phase) changes the evaluation and would invalidate every number measured today, so it is listed as open work instead. A real asymmetry is worth tens of centipawns, so the test keeps its power.

**Commands** (all in `docs/research/sim-results-2026-09-13.md` §9):
```
npm run sim -- --id values-d3-p2 --experiment values --games 500 --depth 3 --seed 1
npm run sim -- --id values-d4-p2 --experiment values --games 120 --depth 4 --seed 1 --pieces AGS
npm run sim -- --id values-d3-p3 --experiment values --games 500 --depth 3 --seed 1 --pieces AL
npm run sim -- --id sweep-p2 --experiment sweep --arrangements 60 --rounds 2 --games 80 --depth 3 --seed 7
npm run sim:analyze -- --values --boots 300
```
**Time:** 16 820 games, **30 m 15 s of play**, 33 minutes end to end. Code: `--pieces` subset flag in `experiments.ts`; `fitTanh`/`fitValues`/`--values` in `analyze.ts` (plain momentum GD from zero, not Adam — the design is rank-deficient and GD from zero stays in its row space, so the contrasts are exact); one new test plants a 2.00-pawn piece and checks recovery.

**Skipped:** Muller's step 2 (pawn-compensated arms to turn the three bounds into values), a 1500-game depth-4 calibration, paladin/maester at depth 4, and the mid-game-position regression that would recover N/B/R/Q against AlphaZero's 3.05/3.33/5.63/9.50 — all four written up as §16. Files touched: `src/ai/eval.ts`, `src/ai/search.test.ts`, `src/sim/analyze.ts`, `src/sim/experiments.ts`, `src/sim/sim.test.ts`, `docs/research/sim-results-2026-09-13.md` (§9–§17 appended, pass 1 intact).</result>

## 2026-09-13T18:25:28.821Z — assistant (source line 2600)

Buff measurements are in, and the run batch you asked for is designed and running. On your question first.

**Are the AI players smart enough?** Partly. They are fixed-depth-3 alpha-beta players with quiescence and a hand-written evaluation: tactically sound for one or two-move combinations, strategically naive. That is enough to trust coarse findings that also hold at depth 4 (which pieces are far below a knight, that beast chains are flavour), and not enough to trust fine ones (±0.5 pawn values, "fun" rankings, anything positional). Two things make them smarter cheaply, and both are scheduled: a strength ladder running now (depth 2 through 6, plus depth-5 re-checks of the headline numbers) tells us how much each ply buys, and then tuning the evaluation on the recorded games, which the research puts at +200 Elo for variants. A learned evaluation comes after that.

**Buff candidates for the three weak pieces** (300 games each, value in pawns, knight = 3.2):

| Piece | Today | Best single change | Combined |
|---|---|---|---|
| Archer | < 1.7 | step in 8 directions: **2.7 ± 0.6** | + shots at 2 diagonal: 4.6 (too strong) |
| Guard | < 1.7 | captures pawns or steps 2: no gain alone | both together: **1.8 ± 0.6** |
| Beast | < 1.7 | step in any direction: **2.1 ± 0.6** | + capture straight ahead: 2.9 |

The beast's handicap is its one-way move, not the blind spot. With all recommended changes on, White stays at 0.500 and draw rate is unchanged; games get shorter and branching rises. One caution: the guard pair can farm pawns without ever being taken, which triples dead endings in that test arm. Recommended set: archer steps any direction, guard captures pawns and steps 2, beast steps any direction. Nothing changes in the game until you decide; the toggles are ready.

**Batch 2** (docs/RUNS.md): R1 strength ladder and depth-5 confirmations, R2 pool compositions (standard-only, one fairy type at a time, fairy-heavy), R3 placement patterns (king or queen in a corner, bishops in corners, guard or maester next to the king, archers adjacent, beasts on the flanks) each against a matched control, R4 rule-toggle A/B, R5 evaluation tuning.
