<result>Done. `src/sim/analyze.ts` has since been fixed by its owner, so the tool's local port is now a decoupling choice rather than a necessity.

**Strongest findings** (all replicated across the two sweeps' *different* engines; `sweep-p2` 9 600 games / 60 ranks primary, `sweep-d3` 4 000 / 100 ranks replication):

1. **Pace is army value, not fairy count.** `armyValue` (measured pawns of the 7 non-king pieces) correlates −0.79 with mean plies and −0.69 with draw rate — beating `fairyCount` (+0.66, +0.54). The pool holds exactly seven standard pieces, so `QRRBBNN` at 32.00 pawns is the *only* all-standard army; every fairy piece makes the army lighter (range 30.48 → 16.48). Fairy count 2→6: draws 0.221→0.438, plies 109→180.
2. **Queen = −10.8 draw points, −27 plies.** One queen is worth ~two fairy slots of pace.
3. **Guards wall the game — H10 confirmed.** 0→2 guards: +10.0 draw points (replication +14.0), survival 0.966. Guard effects on *balance* do not replicate.
4. **The paladin is the only fairy piece that sharpens** (−6 draw points) and it fires: survives 7% of games, captures in 89% (H5 predicted &gt;30% never capture — it's 11%).
5. **Balance is flat.** No rank feature beats noise in both samples; between-rank spread ≈ 9 Elo.

**Dead weight:** the **beast** — utilisation 0.45 (a pawn is 0.43), never leaves its start square in 20% of games (31% from a-file, 30% from h), captures in 45%, chains ≥2 only 8.3% of the time. The **guard captures nothing in 13 120 games** (`guardCaptures:'none'`): immortal, inert, and 7.6% of promotions buy one anyway. **Placement:** a centre archer shoots 5.4× as often as an h-file archer (4 431 vs 827); corner archers/beasts are 2–8× more likely to be furniture. Sliders and knights are indifferent to their start file.

**Interest axis warning:** `nS` correlates −0.77 with interest because the +0.20 `minFairyUse` term is a *minimum*, and one beast drags it to 0.45. Strip that term and residualise on draw rate and the across-rank spread collapses from ±0.10 to ±0.019 against ~0.008 noise — **no back rank is distinguishable on interest right now.** Don't rank arrangements on it until the beast is fixed.

**Pilot (the one 100-game run, permitted):** value-matched court `AAGMMQN` (23.86 pawns) beat horde `SSLQBBG` (23.76) **0.790 ± 0.041** — ~+225 Elo where matched values predict zero, despite the horde holding more conventional material. The 1.70-pawn band floor badly overprices beast and guard in company.

**Batch 3:** 29 specs, 69 300 games, ~3.9 h at 5 games/s, ordered by information gain — the first four (50 min) separate army value from fairy count (`b3-val-*`, `b3-iso-*`), isolate the guard via the A→G swap at fixed value (`b3-guard0/1/2`), and certify the five uncertifiable ranks at 500 games (`b3-deep5`). Then armies, placement, rule removals on the buff set, mirror-vs-independent, no-beast pool, A–G adjacency, maester-beside-king, two paladins. Not run.

**Files:** `tools/mine.ts` (`npx tsx tools/mine.ts`, `--runs`, `--min`, `--section`, `--specs`) · `sim/specs/batch3/*.json` (29, all validated: 8 chars, one king, bishops opposite colours, notes carried inline) · `docs/research/sim-mining-2026-09-13.md` · `docs/RUNS.md` "Batch 3 (proposed)".

One lesson I did **not** write, since `LESSONS.md` wasn't in my write allowlist and is under concurrent edit — worth adding: *a tool that imports from `src/` breaks whenever another agent is mid-edit; for cross-cutting analysis tools, import only the stable constants and port the hot functions with a provenance comment.*</result>

## 2026-09-13T18:50:40.126Z — assistant (source line 2765)

The data mining is done (docs/research/sim-mining-2026-09-13.md), and it reshapes what to test next.

**What the 40,000 games say**
- **Pace is army value, not fairy count.** The lighter the army in measured pawns, the longer and drawier the game (r = −0.79 with length). Every fairy piece makes the army lighter than the all-standard rank. A queen alone is worth −11 draw points and −27 plies.
- **Guards wall the game**: two guards add +10 draw points; they survive 97% of games and, under today's rules, captured nothing in 13,000 games.
- **The paladin is the only fairy piece that sharpens** (−6 draw points), and it fires: it captures in 89% of games.
- **The beast is dead weight today**: never leaves its start square in 20% of games (30% from a corner), captures in 45%, chains in 8%. Corner archers shoot 5× less than centre archers.
- **Balance is flat across ranks** (about 9 Elo of real spread). The interest score was broken by a "weakest fairy piece" term, so no rank can be ranked on fun yet; the fix is in progress.
- **Value-matched armies are not matched**: a 100-game pilot of an archers-guards-maesters "court" beat a beasts-paladin "horde" 0.79, roughly +225 Elo, so the current floor values overprice beast and guard when they play together.

**Batch 3 proposed** (29 spec files, ~69,000 games, ~4 hours), ordered by information gain: separate army value from fairy count; isolate the guard by swapping archer for guard at fixed value; certify the five ambiguous ranks at 500 games; then thematic armies, placement (archer beside guard, maester beside king), rule removals on top of the buff set, mirrored vs independently drawn black ranks, a no-beast pool, and two-paladin ranks. It starts when batch 2 and the buffed-set campaign finish.

|---|---|---|---|

| Human=Black | ARKSSBML / GNSNRKBG / BARQKGNB | White mates (all) | 61 / 59 / 37 |
| Computer vs Computer | RMKRSNNG / QMBSKRGB / GMAKGRNM | repetition / repetition / 50-move | 132 / 74 / 385 |

CvC: move list grew every ply, dialog opened with a sensible result, Rematch worked in all three, HUD rAF latency 0–3 ms throughout. Fairy checks all screenshotted and matched the engine: archer shot, paladin self-removal, beast chain (full `Sd4xe5xf6` and short via "Stop chain here"), maester swap, maester–king long swap, promotion. Undo×5 (28→18 plies) and reload mid-game (20→20, identical FEN and move list) both cross-check clean; 800×600 and 390×844 both lay out correctly and still accept moves.

**Bugs, ranked:**
1. **High —** press New game / Undo / Resign while the computer's move is animating and the next game is silently corrupted: `commit()` (`src/main.ts:102`) has no `gen` guard around its `await view.animateMove`, so after `reset()` the stale commit restarts `maybeAi()` and **two AI loops run forever**. Both commit the same move; the second applies it from a now-empty square, deleting the piece and letting one side move repeatedly. Ghost entries like `" h1-g3"` appear in the move list. Reproduced 3/3 (`repro2.ts new|undo`) and 3/4 via the everyday "switch White to Computer, then New game".
2. **Medium —** promotion picker leaves the board live (`src/main.ts:143` awaits without setting `busy`): move another piece while it is open, then answer it, and both moves commit — `1. Ka1-b1 a7-a8=Q`, White moves twice, Black never does.
3. **Medium —** `Engine.cancel()` respawns the worker but the old search's fallback timer then nulls the *replacement* (`src/game.ts:114/123`), pushing later searches onto the main thread. Found by inspection; a 40-sample rAF probe did not show it, so the cost is unmeasured.
4. **Low —** changing the White/Black dropdowns never calls `view.flip()` (only `newGame()` does), so switching to Black mid-game leaves the board on White's side.
5. **Cosmetic —** at 390×844 the board sits high in its region with a black band below.

**Console: zero errors and zero page errors in every run**; only four SwiftShader "GPU stall due to ReadPixels" warnings per run, from the headless GL stack, not app code.

## 2026-09-13T19:32:44.942Z — assistant (source line 2800)

Full-game QA is done: 8 complete games through the real UI (human as White and Black, computer vs computer), every fairy event cross-checked against the engine, zero console errors. It found one serious bug and four smaller ones, and an Opus agent is fixing them at the root:

1. **Serious**: pressing New game, Undo or Resign while the computer's move is animating leaves a stale commit running, so two AI loops start and the second replays a move from an empty square, corrupting the game. Fix: generation guard around the animation await plus a single in-flight AI token.
2. Moving another piece while the promotion picker is open commits both moves.
3. Cancelling a search can knock the replacement worker back to the main thread.
4. Switching a human to Black mid-game doesn't turn the board.
5. On a 390-px phone layout the board sits high in its region.

I'll rebuild and republish once the fixes are verified. The simulation campaigns keep running meanwhile.

<result>All five fixes are in and verified.

**Fixes** (only `src/main.ts`, `src/game.ts`, `src/game.test.ts`, `src/style.css` touched; `index.html` needed no change)

1. HIGH — `commit()` (`src/main.ts:105`) now captures `const g = gen` and returns after `await view.animateMove(...)` when it changed. Root cause: an unguarded await let a stale `commit` resume into the *new* game, re-run `save()` and start a second `maybeAi()` loop, whose move was applied from an empty square. `gen` is now the single in-flight token — every await in `commit`/`maybeAi`/`choose` drops out on it.
2. HIGH (same bug, engine half) — `Engine.think` (`src/game.ts:100`) resolves only while `id === this.id`, so a search cancelled by `reset()` never answers on either the worker or the inline path. The check lives in `think`, where all callers route through, rather than in each caller.
3. MEDIUM — the fallback timer checks `live()` **before** touching state (`src/game.ts:116`). Root cause: the dead worker's timer fired 2.5 s after `cancel()` and nulled `this.worker`, which by then was the replacement — every later search ran on the main thread.
4. MEDIUM — `choose()` sets `busy = true` around the promotion picker, and `pickPromotion` now resolves `null` through a `closePromo` handle that `reset()` calls. Root cause: the await left `onSquareClick` live, so a second move committed and the stale promotion committed after it.
5. LOW — `orient()` helper (`src/main.ts:181`) replaces three copies of the flip condition and is called from the White/Black `onchange` as well as `newGame()` and startup.
6. COSMETIC — `src/style.css` mobile query: the board region is capped to a square (`--band: min(100vw, 58vh)`), the surplus goes to the panel, and `--board-lift: 0.075` pulls the canvas up so the drawn board centres. The lift is a measured calibration constant, commented as such.

**Tests added** (`src/game.test.ts`): `Engine.cancel` drops a cancelled search's late result (fails on the old code: "expected true to be false"); a cancelled search's fallback timer leaves the replacement worker alone, using a `FakeWorker` stub + fake timers.

**Verification** — `npx tsc --noEmit` clean; `npx vitest run` 90/90 across 4 files. Playwright (dev server on 5232, `/@vite/client` stubbed): bug 1 New game ×5 and Undo ×5 mid-animation → 10/10 clean, no ghost LANs, no console errors; human-vs-computer Undo mid-animation hands the turn back to White and zero further AI moves in the next 6 s. <result>All done. Final state:

**Headline differences that beat noise** (flag rule `|Δ| &gt; 2×combined se`, errors clustered on the back rank):

- **Pool composition dominates everything.** All 8 test pools flag against the full-pool control on decisiveness, draws, capped share and length, monotone in fairy density: standard-only `QRRBBNN` is 86.8% decisive / 87 plies / 0.2% capped, the control 62.2% / 138 / 5.9%, fairy-heavy 39.8% / 172 / 14.5%.
- **Only one pool moves balance:** `+L` (paladin) at 0.548 ± 0.009, **+33 ± 12 Elo** vs the control's +12 ± 12. Per-piece OLS: +0.020 white score per paladin — the only piece that does this.
- **Guard, archer and beast are the draw engine.** Per extra piece: −0.141/−0.114/−0.102 decisive, +0.09/+0.06/+0.06 draw, +18/+21/+15 plies, +3–5 points capped. The guard makes 0.00 captures and survives 97–99% (`guardCaptures` is `none`).
- **Maester swaps look like a shuffle engine:** 6.4/game in the control, 25.1 in the fairy pool.
- **R3 placement is nearly all confounded by composition.** The raw "guard beside the king → +6.5 draw points" was the extra guard (1.60/rank vs 0.75), not the square. An OLS over 180 full-pool ranks with piece counts + placement flags leaves only four small real effects: archers adjacent (−0.032 decisive, +0.031 draw), queen in a corner (−0.026 draw), beasts on a/h (−0.021 capped), guard beside king (−4.8 plies, +0.012 score).

**Did not beat noise:** every set's balance, including king corner vs king d/e (Δ +0.014 ± 0.009). Chess960 §7.4 (corner bishops → dull and fair) and §7.9 (king placement matters more for us) do not transfer; §7.6 (adjacency irrelevant) is contradicted — archers adjacent is the largest surviving placement effect; §7.5 half transfers (draw half yes, balance half no).

**Confirmed as asked:** the standard-only pool obeys bishops-opposite-colours — `QRRBBNN` has exactly 7 letters so all are drawn, every rank has exactly two bishops, and 20,000 samples gave 0 same-colour pairs. `tools/ranks.mjs` was verified to reproduce `sampleBackRank` letter-for-letter on the same seed.

**Total run time:** 2 h 36 m 47 s, 17 runs × 3,200 games = 54,400 games, 3.8–9.0 games/s (machine shared).

**Cut for time / scope:** I merged R2's pool (b) with R3's unconstrained control into one run rather than drawing a separate matched control per constraint — one shared control at the same seed makes every comparison like-for-like and saved ~8 runs (~1.3 h). R3's full interest-term breakdown lives in the per-run `sim/out/*.report.md` rather than the doc, for the 160-line limit.

Files: `/Users/za/Documents/king down chess/docs/research/sim-configs-2026-09-13.md` (160 lines), `/Users/za/Documents/king down chess/tools/ranks.mjs`, 17 specs in `/Users/za/Documents/king down chess/sim/specs/cfg-*.json`. Two things for your eye before anything ships: the board tiles derive from textures.com stock photos, and the Imperator fonts carry no licence. Only the buffed-set campaign is still running.

**Values (buffed rules, depth 3, 500 games/arm, `--eloPerPawn 64`)** — every piece moved less than its own error bar, so one pass closed the fixed point:
`--values A=280,L=310,G=170,M=330,S=215` (archer 2.82±0.44, paladin 3.10±0.45, maester 3.28±0.44, beast 2.17±0.41 now inside the linear band; guard still a bound &lt;1.70, re-checked at seed 1.70 and unchanged). Defaults in `src/ai/eval.ts` untouched.

**A/B headline** (150 ranks × 100 games/arm, 30 000 games, paired on the same openings): White score 0.524 → 0.521, **paired difference −0.002 ± 0.009 — the buff does not move balance**. §18.3's −0.075 ± 0.037 was a 30-rank artefact; treat it as retracted. Draws +0.037 ± 0.019, capped −0.029 ± 0.007, mean plies −4.7 ± 2.6, branching +5.2 ± 0.4. Beast moves 3.63 → 10.80, archer survival 73% → 50%. Interest (residualised on draw rate) +0.005 ± 0.002.

**Degeneracy — this is the finding.** `guardCaptures=pawns` + `guardStep=2` creates an uncapturable pawn harvester: 3.41 guard captures a game, **43.7% of games have a guard taking ≥3 pawns**, and those games draw 46.7% / end dead-material 18.1% against 29.0% / 4.8% otherwise. That is the entire draw-rate rise. Three LAN excerpts in the doc; all three drawn. Beast ten-capture chains exist but are rare (0.83% of games) and are a highlight, not an exploit.

**Depth-4** (60 ranks × 20 games/arm): no metric changes sign. White score −0.020 ± 0.029 agrees. But draws (+0.116 ± 0.050), guard captures (5.15) and rampage games (59.4%) all get **worse with depth** — the harvester is a property of the rule, not of a weak engine.

**Sweep** (100 ranks, 2 rounds, 16 000 games): between-rank sd 0.029 is still below the 0.032 sampling noise at 160 games, so ranks remain indistinguishable. Only `KLSNMRQA` (0.591, +2.53 sd) passes the 2-sd flag and it fails the balance gate. Residualised interest extremes span ±0.015 — noise.

**Recommendation in the doc:** adopt `archerMove=any` and `beastMove=any`; hold the guard pair pending one run each of `guardCaptures=pawns`+`guardStep=1` and `guardStep=2`+`guardCaptures=none`.

**Run time:** 53 940 games, 184 min engine time; ~3 h 50 wall including tests and analysis.

**Cut:** A/B at 150 ranks not 200 (sanctioned), depth-4 at 20 games/rank not 40 — machine ran 4.8 games/s against the usual 9. Neither changes a verdict.

**Two notes.** (1) The coordinator's `minFairyUse` → mean change is in `src/sim/weights.ts`/`analyze.ts`; every table prints both columns. It matters: the buff's "interest gain" was +0.060 ± 0.010 on the old minimum term and +0.005 ± 0.002 on the mean — the old term was measuring one beast waking up. (2) My first values run silently lost all four `--rule` flags because zsh does not word-split an unquoted `$VAR`; `parseFlags` now throws on a key containing whitespace and `run()` prints the live rule diff, so it cannot happen again. <result>Those `smoke.*` files predate this session — leaving them. Everything is verified.

**Guard verdict.** Adopt `guardCaptures='pawns'` + `guardStep=2` + a new `guardCaptureLimit=1`. Odds vs knight, 300 games each, buffed base at −207 ± 32: pawns-only +49 ± 45, step2-only −10 ± 45, the degenerate pair +98 ± 47, **the pair capped at 1 capture a side +54 ± 46**. On 40 ranks × 100 games the cap takes guard captures a game 3.55 → 1.08 and rampage games **44.4 % → 0.0 %**, dead endings 10.6 → 7.0 %, draws +0.050 ± 0.019 against the pair's +0.116 ± 0.035, and the guard still walks 14.3 squares a game. New finding: `guardCaptures=pawns` **alone** already harvests in 21.5 % of games, so §18's "+4 Elo, harmless" reading of it is wrong in a normal game. The guard stays under 1.70 pawns under every rule tried.

**R4 toggles that matter (buffed base, 4 000 games each).** Only `guardImmune=false` changes the game: decisive **+0.042 ± 0.018**, plies **−7.6 ± 2.6**, capped **−0.015 ± 0.007**, dead endings 4.8 → 3.6 %. `archerChecks=false` moves the wrong way (plies **+5.5 ± 2.6**, more ply caps). `maesterLongSwap=false` measures nothing, against H9's predicted 15–40 Elo. `promotionSet=standard` costs 0.1 of a legal move. `bishopsOppositeColours=false` is not a game rule — it only decides which ranks exist; 38 of 40 ranks are drawn identically and play byte-identical games, so its "significant" flags are collapsed intervals, not effects. No toggle moves White's score or residualised interest past 0.004.

**2017 / 2021 / buffed.** Archer: −160 ± 34 / **−244 ± 29** / −34 ± 35 Elo; moves 14.40 / 6.64 / 15.95; shots 2.67 / 1.17 / 2.88; survival 72.9 / 72.7 / 50.4 %. Beast: −225 ± 30 / **−225 ± 29** / −72 ± 35; moves 4.10 / 9.01 / 11.42; captures 0.60 / 0.51 / 1.57; survival 68.9 / 63.5 / 51.6 %. The 2021 archer is a real 84 ± 45 Elo weakening; the 2021 beast is worth exactly the same as the 2017 one at 2.2× the activity. Both sets sharpen the game against 2017, the buff set twice as hard.

**Lab changes.** `guardCaptureLimit` (per-side counter beside `RULES`, documented ceiling: the search sees the real game's count, not its own line's); `--baseRule` / `--baseValues` / `--baseId` move an A/B's control arm and share it across runs (36 000 games saved); the four 2021 toggles, with `isAttacked` now walking shot tables negated and per colour; `run()` applies the spec's rules before sampling back ranks. 6 new tests, 96 green, `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, dashboard rebuilt.

**Total: 54 100 games in 79 minutes** (11.4 games/s, 16 workers). Report: `/Users/za/Documents/king down chess/docs/research/sim-rules-2026-09-13.md` (180 lines). ## 2026-09-14T00:05:51.409Z — assistant (source line 3218)

The last rule batch is in (54,100 games, 79 minutes; docs/research/sim-rules-2026-09-13.md; dashboard republished at 230,000 games), and it settles the guard:

**Guard**: capturing pawns with a 2-square step is fine once a guard may capture only once. The cap removes the harvester entirely (44% → 0% of games), keeps the guard mobile, and lands at +54 ± 46 Elo over today's guard. Pawn capture without a cap harvests even alone (21% of games), so the earlier "harmless" reading was wrong.

**Other rule toggles** (each 4,000 games): only "guard can be captured by anyone" changes the game (+4 points decisive, 8 plies shorter), but that erases the guard's identity, so I'm not taking it. Archer-checks-off makes games longer, the wrong way. The maester long swap, standard-only promotion and bishop colours measure nothing.

**Your 2021 definitions vs 2017 vs the buffs**: the 2021 archer is 84 Elo weaker than 2017; the 2021 beast is worth the same as 2017 but twice as active. The buff set sharpens the game about twice as hard as either.

**Decision, being applied now**: archer and beast step in any direction; guard captures a pawn once in its lifetime and steps up to 2; AI values updated to the measured ones. The 2017 rules stay available via `?rules=2017` and the 2021 set via `?rules=2021`. An agent is implementing the per-guard "spent" flag, re-measuring it, updating the rules doc and piece guide, and rebuilding; I'll publish v0.6 when it's verified.

<result>Done. Report:

**Spent-flag design** — `SPENT = 32` (bit 5) in the piece byte (`src/rules/engine.ts`). `typeOf` already masked it; `colorOf` now masks too (`(p&gt;&gt;4)&amp;1`). One shared `landed(mover, m)` sets it and is called by `makeMove` **and** the search's `apply`/`applyQuiet`, so make/unmake is exact — the old per-side counter made the search optimistic by one capture per line. `canCapture(att, vic)` now takes the attacker *byte* (the `by` parameter is gone); `zIndex` hashes the flag, and the spent half of the Zobrist table is drawn **after** the turn keys so every pre-existing key keeps its value (verified: 5 stored `abb-base` games replay move-for-move). FEN marker `H`/`h`; a promoted guard starts unspent. `GUARD_CAPS` deleted, `guardCaptureLimit` narrowed to `0 | 1` (one bit holds one capture).

**Per-guard vs per-side** (docs/research/sim-rules-2026-09-13.md §6, 4 300 new games / 6.5 min; `abb-base` resumed from store):
- Odds vs knight, 300 games: per-guard **−147 ± 34** vs per-side −153 ± 33, base (2017 guard) −207 ± 32 → Δ **+60 ± 47** vs +54 ± 46.
- Paired over the same 40 ranks × 100 games: guard captures/game 1.079 → **1.342** (+0.262 ± 0.146); games with ≥3 captures 0/4000 → **4/4000** (0.1%, vs 44.4% uncapped); dead endings 7.0% → **6.5%** (−0.005 ± 0.008); draws 0.310 → **0.308** (−0.002 ± 0.013). Against the buffed base: White −0.002 ± 0.012. Verdict: ship it — same balance, no harvester, exact in the search.

<result>Done. Final report:

**Texel tuning — shipped (+113 Elo at depth 3, +117 at depth 4)**

- **Data**: only **8,600** of 196,260 recorded games carry the exact v0.6 rule set (an empty rule diff means "the defaults of that hour", and they changed mid-campaign — `ab-buff-big` predates the guard capture limit). So I played 24,000 more (`tune-data`, 34 min) and sampled **583,868 quiet positions from 28,600 games** (58,267 held out by game, not by position); quiet = not in check and `evaluate()` within 50 cp of a full qsearch. Stored in `sim/tune/` (git-ignored).
- **Tuner** (`src/sim/tune.ts`): eval re-implemented as a 408-parameter vector with exact gradients (phase coupling included); `src/sim/tune.test.ts` proves it **bit-for-bit equal to `evaluate()` on 1,000 random positions**, checks the gradient against finite differences, and round-trips the vector. Full-batch Adam on 16 worker threads, L2 toward the current values, pawn frozen at 100 to keep a centipawn meaning what delta pruning/adjudication/Elo-per-pawn assume.
- **K = 0.667**. MSE train **0.133212 → 0.130223**, validation **0.134855 → 0.131611** (shipped fit, λ=1e-4).
- **Key finding**: validation MSE anti-correlates with strength. λ=0 had the best held-out error (0.129924) and **lost** its pilot (−23 ± 48); λ=1e-4 and λ=1e-3 each won by +85, and λ=1e-4 beat λ=1e-3 head-to-head (+81 ± 36). λ was therefore chosen by match, not by MSE.
- **Measured** (tuned vs untuned, colour-swapped pairs, fresh seeds, TT cleared each ply on both sides): **depth 3 +113 ± 27** (400 games, 0.657, LOS 100%), **depth 4 +117 ± 33** (200 games, 0.662). Shipped via `npm run tune:apply`; untuned copy kept at `sim/tune/eval-before.ts`.
- **Value shifts**: N 320→296, B 330→313, R 500→472, Q 900→909, A 280→**295**, L 310→318, G 170→**124**, M 330→**291**, S 215→**261**. Tempo 10→5, mobility B/R/Q/L 4/3/2/2→**1/1/0/0** (four times too big by hand), shield 12/26/5/8→9/25/5/4, maester 14/7→11/7, `PHASE_MAX` 5600→5712. Tables moved ≤2.5 cp a square except the pawn (7.9): **+38 on a2, −8 on d2**, because a maester swap drags pawns onto rank 1 in 14% of sampled positions.
- **Odds match re-run** (5 × 300 games, depth 3, `--eloPerPawn 64`, tuned eval both sides): archer 2.82→**3.43 ± 0.54**, paladin 3.10→**2.21 ± 0.52**, guard &lt;1.70→**&lt;1.46** (still a bound), maester 3.28→**2.18 ± 0.53**, beast 2.17→**2.67 ± 0.54** pawns, knight anchor 3.20→2.96. Three rows moved by more than a combined error bar; Muller's fixed point is no longer converged under the new engine.
- **Tests**: `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, **102 vitest green** (97 + 5 new). `search.test.ts` needed no change. One `sim/sim.test.ts` expectation was rewritten to compare against `ARCHER_V`/`GUARD_V` instead of the literals 280/170/110 — it tests the `setPieceValues` plumbing, and pinning it to tuned constants would break on every future fit.
- **Skipped**: per-group λ search (one scalar λ over six fits); no re-fit of K after tuning; the depth-4 match is 200 games as specified, so its ±33 is the widest number quoted.

## 2026-09-14T01:55:16.304Z — assistant (source line 3311)

The evaluation tuning shipped and the game is republished as v0.6.1 with it (same link). Numbers (docs/research/sim-tuning-2026-09-13.md):

- Trained on 584,000 quiet positions from 28,600 games under the v0.6 rules (24,000 played fresh, since the earlier ones used older rule sets).
- Tuned engine vs untuned: **+113 ± 27 Elo at depth 3, +117 ± 33 at depth 4**. The best-fitting model on held-out data actually lost its match, so the regularisation was chosen by match results, not fit.
- What it learned: mobility weights were four times too big by hand; a pawn on a2 is worth more than expected because maester swaps drag pawns back to rank 1.

**Design values re-measured with the stronger engine** (pawns; knight = 3.0): archer 3.4, beast 2.7, paladin 2.2, maester 2.2, guard below 1.5. Paladin and maester lost about a pawn compared with the depth-3 untuned numbers, archer and beast gained. This is the depth-dependence the ladder warned about, so values are now quoted only from the tuned engine.

Running now: batch 3 (the 29 mined configurations, re-based on the v0.6 rules and the tuned engine, about 2–3 hours), plus a depth-4 replay of the most and least interesting ranks to test whether the interest ordering survives a stronger search. Dashboard is at 223,000 games.

**Beat noise (2× combined error)**
- **Guards are the draw engine.** 0→2 guards at ~27 pawns, fairy count fixed: draw 0.268→0.469 (**+20.1 points**, double H10's +10), capped 0.6 %→16.1 %, +47 plies. Balance flat.
- **Fairy count matters at constant value.** Army held inside 0.6 pawns, fairy 0→4: −9.0 decisiveness, +6.6 draw points, +31 plies (every step flagged on length). So the mining's raw ladder is ~half material, ~half pieces.
- **`guardImmune=false` is the cheapest anti-draw lever**: capped 7.8 %→4.6 % (under the gate), +5.1 decisiveness, guard survival 92 %→66 %, no balance cost.
- **A maester beside the king = +0.021 White score (+15 Elo)** — the largest placement effect the project has measured.
- **Two paladins vs two maesters at an identical 29.1 pawns**: White 0.581 vs 0.494 (+61 Elo), +0.200 decisiveness, 26 plies shorter. The tuned table prices them equal; the games are not.
- Court vs horde: court **0.566 ± 0.019 (+46 ± 13 Elo)**, not the pilot's 0.790. The tuned values explain +26 of it; residual +20 ± 13.
- Corner placement now costs only 6–7 plies (beast and archer both), so `archerMove=any`/`beastMove=any` already paid the 2017 corner debt.

**Did not beat noise**
- **Army value does not drive pace** (reverses the mining headline): fairy set held at A,A,M, 30.6→21.2 pawns gives +2.7 draw points (n.s.) and +1 ply.
- `archerChecks=false` and `maesterLongSwap=false`: nothing flags; the long swap reads +2 ± 8 Elo, excluding H9's 15–40.
- H4 (archer beside a guard): nothing. The five sweep "extremes" collapse to a 0.041 spread at n=500. Mirrored vs independent ranks: 1.7σ, bound 40 Elo.

**Interest ordering** — survives a ply, not the rules. Spearman ρ(depth 3, depth 4) = **+0.81** residualised (+0.70 raw); ρ(tuned d3, untuned pre-buff d3) = **−0.16**. The top-20/bottom-20 gap of +0.093 ± 0.015 in `sweep-p2` reads −0.003 ± 0.007 at depth 3 and −0.003 ± 0.005 at depth 4: the old ranking counted beasts (2 in the top 20, 25 in the bottom 20) and `beastMove=any` erased the deficit. ## 2026-09-14T05:00:46.306Z — assistant (source line 3461)

Decisions logged and the guard is being reverted to the immortal wall (no captures, one step) with a republish; the guard study, the paladin/maester buff campaign and the learned-evaluation AI work start as soon as that lands, so they run on the final rules.

**Current fairy-piece rules** (after the revert; the 2017 book, plus two measured changes marked *)

| Piece | Moves | Captures | Special |
|---|---|---|---|
| Archer | 1 square in any direction* | without moving: an enemy on a diagonally adjacent square, or exactly 2 squares away orthogonally; blockers ignored | gives check the same way |
| Paladin | like a queen, jumps over friendly pieces, blocked by enemies | by moving onto the enemy | cannot capture a king, so never gives check; removes itself after capturing |
| Guard | 1 square in any direction, empty squares only | never | cannot be captured, except by a king; blocks like any piece |
| Maester | 1 square in any direction | onto an adjacent enemy | onto an adjacent friend = swap places; if maester and own king both stand on their first rank they may swap at any distance |
| Beast | 1 square in any direction*, empty only | any of the 7 adjacent squares that are not straight ahead | after capturing it may keep capturing from the new square in the same turn, never a king as a continuation |

*2017 had the archer stepping only orthogonally and the beast only straight forward.

**Kings' powers** (2017 rulebook; one of two chosen after setup; not in the game yet)

| King | Power A | Power B |
|---|---|---|
| Frost | Freeze: an enemy piece (not king) cannot move on the opponent's next turn | Ice Wall: a friendly piece (not king) cannot be captured on the opponent's next turn |
| Flame | Strike: move any own piece (not king) as if it were a queen; counts as a turn | Haste: move one piece twice in a single turn |
| Stratus | Flight: move any own piece (not king) to any empty square in own half; counts as a turn | Sacrifice: swap any own pawn with any own piece captured earlier |
| Mud | March: any pawn may move two forward | Leap: own pieces may jump over own pawns |
| Spirit | Holy Light (always on): king cannot be captured by pawns and cannot capture pawns | Mercy (always on): king moves 1 or 2 squares, cannot capture, jumps friends |
| Shadow | Death Touch (always on): king captures adjacent enemies without moving | Darkness (always on): own pawns move diagonally and capture straight ahead; no double first move |

Rulebook use counts: Freeze 2, Ice Wall 2, Strike 1, Haste 1, Flight 1, Sacrifice 1, March 3, Leap 3. The 2014 card prototypes had different, economy-style king powers (Mud: save unspent actions; Cumulus: move 2 tiles).

**Cards** (from the 2014–2015 card game; costs and tolls dropped)

| Card | Effect as printed |
|---|---|
| Salvation | revive a taken piece |
| Shield | taking your pieces costs more (harder to capture) |
| Strike | take without moving |
| Curse | spell cards can apply to any piece (control an enemy piece of the same type / ignore a card's text in older drafts) |
| Firewall | calling cards can apply to a piece in the capitol |
| Haste | cure a stunned piece (older drafts: copy another piece's range) |
| Flight | move a piece anywhere |
| Growth | calling cards cost less (draw) |
| Rage | +1 move / +1 take (extra capture) |
| Mirror | copy a card |
| Control | control a friendly piece in range |
| Sacrifice | swap a pawn with a captured piece |
| Rescue | keep an effect for another turn |
| Leap | range not obstructed |
| Burn | capture inside the capitol zone |
| Sky Lift, Earth Quake, Fire Starter, Frost Bite | element cards: swap two units; shove adjacent units; fire zone; freeze in range |

Unit cards give each piece a built-in effect: Bow (archer) has Strike, Block (guard) has Shield, Bash (paladin) has Leap, Cog (maester) has Control, Beast has Rage; standard units get +1 move or +1 take (Pike, Steed, Cross, Rock, Thorn).

## 2026-09-14T06:08:35.722Z — user (source line 3549)

## 2026-09-14T06:09:48.836Z — assistant (source line 3576)

The guard study and the paladin/maester grid are still running; I'll explain those the same way when they land. Here is everything else so far, in plain terms.

**How the lab works.** The computer plays against itself, hundreds of thousands of games, with the same starting setup played once from each side so neither colour gets an unfair set of positions. We count wins, draws and game lengths, and every number comes with a margin of error. Where I say "beats noise", the effect is bigger than that margin.

**The first player has a small edge.** Whoever moves first scores about 52 to 48, similar to ordinary chess. No rule change we tested moved this.

**What each fairy piece is worth.** We measure worth by giving one side a fairy piece and the other a knight and seeing who wins. With the computer at its current strength: archer about a knight and a bit more, beast a bit under a knight, paladin and maester each about two thirds of a knight, guard well under half a knight. The guard's low number is expected: it never captures, so its value is in blocking, which this measurement barely rewards.

**Why the archer and beast changed.** Under the 2017 rules both were worth about half a knight, mostly because they moved so slowly they sat unused; the beast never left its square in one game out of five. Letting them step in any direction made them proper pieces and did not change who wins. Your 2021 versions went the other way: the archer became weaker still, the beast the same as 2017 but busier.

**What makes games long and drawn.** Guards, above all: two guards on the board add about twenty draws per hundred games, because walls that cannot be removed lock positions. Having many light fairy pieces instead of standard ones also slows the game down. Queens do the opposite; a queen is the single biggest sharpener.

**The guard episode.** We tried letting the guard capture pawns. It became a pawn-harvesting machine that nobody could stop, and nearly half the games were affected, so we took it out. Your call to keep it as a pure immortal wall stands, and it can no longer be a promotion target.

**The paladin is the balance lever.** It is the only piece that tilts games toward the first player, and two paladins beat two maesters clearly even though the tables say they are worth the same. That is why we are testing your paladin variants now.

**Where pieces start hardly matters.** King in the corner or the centre, queen in the corner, bishops in corners: no measurable difference. Two small effects survived: a maester next to the king slightly helps the first player, and two archers side by side add a few draws. The mix of pieces matters far more than their order.

**Beast chains** are flavour: turning them off only makes games longer.

**Other rules we tested and can leave alone:** archer giving check, the maester's long swap with the king, which pieces pawns may become, bishop colours. None changed the results.

**How good is the computer?** Each extra level of look-ahead makes it much stronger, so it is far from perfect. But the conclusions above held when we gave it more look-ahead, so I trust them. Rankings of "which setups are the most fun" did not hold up and are not ready to use. We also taught the computer better piece and position values from its own games, which made it clearly stronger, and a learned evaluation is being built now to make it stronger still.

## 2026-09-14T06:19:18.655Z — assistant (source line 3616)

Your message ended at "3." so that part didn't come through; answers to 1 and 2 below.

**1. Second player gets two moves at the start**

Yes, this is a known idea. In ordinary chess it appears as "two-move odds", a handicap a stronger player gave a weaker one (the extra move usually restricted so it could not cross the middle of the board), and in the variant Marseillais Chess, where both players make two moves per turn and White's very first turn is a single move to balance it. Engine analysis of ordinary chess puts a free extra move at close to a full pawn of advantage, while the first-move edge is about a third of a pawn, so the expectation is that a double first turn over-compensates and hands the edge to the second player. It is cheap to measure, so I've queued it: the rule (Black's first turn is two moves, the first may not give check) will be played on 4,000 games with the full pool and 4,000 without the paladin, and I'll report the scores.

Without the paladin the first player's edge shrinks but does not vanish, from data we already have (score out of 1, error about ±0.008):

| Pool | First player's score |
|---|---|
| Standard pieces only | 0.533 |
| Standard + 2 archers | 0.516 |
| Standard + 2 guards | 0.525 |
| Standard + 2 maesters | 0.509 |
| Standard + 2 beasts | 0.519 |
| Standard + 1 paladin | 0.548 |
| Full pool | 0.522 |

So the paladin roughly doubles the edge; the rest is the ordinary chess first-move advantage. A gentler compensation than a whole extra move would be draw odds for the second player (a draw counts as their win), which tournaments already use; with our draw rates that also over-compensates. The double-turn run will tell us how far.

**2. Three options for the guard**

- **A. The Wall (today).** Moves one square, never captures, can only be taken by a king. Cleanest identity. Cost: it is the main source of draws (about ten extra draws per hundred games per guard), and a pair can lock a position.
- **B. The Warden.** Moves up to two squares in any direction (a short queen), never captures, can only be taken by a king, and may never stand on its own second rank (the pawn row), if that is what you meant by "never in the second row". The reach makes it an active blocker: it can seal a file, escort a pawn or deny a promotion square in one move instead of two, and the second-rank ban stops it from becoming a permanent shield in front of the king. Two-square movement measured neutral for piece value; its effect on draws is being measured in the guard study, and I'll add the second-rank ban as a test once you confirm the meaning.
- **C. One guard per army.** Keep the piece exactly as it is, but the pool holds one guard instead of two. The draw drag comes mostly from pairs, so this halves it without touching the piece's rules. It also frees a pool slot for another piece.

My lean is B if the study shows the extra reach makes guards more active without adding draws, otherwise C. Option "anyone can capture it" is off the table per your decision.

## 2026-09-14T06:26:12.209Z — assistant (source line 3637)

Partly right, and it's now being tested directly rather than argued.

Your mechanism is plausible: because the paladin jumps its own pieces, it can strike from the back rank on move one, without spending moves on pawns, so whoever moves first gets the first threat. Two other mechanisms fit the data at least as well:

- **The first favourable trade.** A paladin is worth about two thirds of a knight, but it removes whatever it lands on and dies. Paladin for knight, bishop or better is a gain for the paladin's owner, and the player who moves first gets to force that trade first. This doesn't depend on jumping at all.
- **Initiative amplifier.** A piece that threatens from long range behind an intact pawn wall makes tempo matter more; in chess terms, the paladin makes the position "sharper", and sharper positions favour the side on the move.

The paladin grid now separates these. Every cell (jumps friends yes/no × removed after capture yes/no × may capture the king yes/no) is also played on the pool where every setup has exactly one paladin, and I record the White score plus when the paladin first moves, first captures and first trades itself, and whether its first move jumps the pawn rank. If the White edge collapses when jumping is removed, you're right; if it collapses when the kamikaze removal is turned off, it's the trade mechanism; if neither, it's the initiative effect. Results with the grid.

| King swap from anywhere, not only the first rank | 2.5 |
| Swap places with an adjacent enemy | 2.1 (the AI never used it) |

3. **A — patterns.** The **shield** (guard beside own king) is 86.3% / 82.0% of guard sides, starts in the opening, and reads a lower score and more draws in both pools. **Wall** is the drawiest row. The **anvil** (archer or beast takes a piece pinned beside a guard) is the one pattern that pays: +0.03 to +0.11 score, both pools. **escortPromo** scores 0.888→0.673 but that is selection. ## 2026-09-14T07:47:12.158Z — assistant (source line 3912)

All agents have finished; nothing is running. Last result, the guard study (docs/research/guard-study-2026-09-13.md), in plain terms:

- **Immortality does not hurt the fun.** With one guard per army the cost is small: two guards used to cost about nine decisive games per hundred and 3% stuck games, inside our limits. What drags games out is a guard that can *reach* or *eat pawns*, not one that cannot be captured.
- **How to use an immortal guard well.** The paying pattern is the anvil: park the guard next to an enemy piece so it cannot step away, then take it with an archer shot or a beast. Using the guard as a bodyguard next to the king is what players and the AI do by default, and it scores worse; a guard placed four or more files away from its king makes games about ten points more decisive with no balance cost. Escorting a pawn to promotion with the guard does not work.
- **On the two-square guard, the two studies agree on the data and differ on the judgement.** The Warden test liked it for activity (four times as many moves, more shielding) at the cost of longer games and more guard-caused draws; the guard study weighs those draws as the whole problem and says keep the one-square Wall. My synthesis: keep the Wall as the default; the immortality is what you wanted, it measures fine, and the extra reach buys motion but pays in draws.
- **A correction to the batch-3 report**: its guard rows ran while the capturing-guard defaults were in force, so they described the wrong piece and are void; the run classifier now reads each run's moves rather than its label.

Paused here. Open for your decisions when you return: publish v0.6.3 (one guard per army, no guard promotion, +94-point evaluation), paladin and maester buffs (recommended: paladin survives pawn captures, maester swaps with any friend), the double-turn measurement, and more AI training data.

## 2026-09-13T11:46:26.972Z — user (source line 4321)

Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page - what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in. Format is not part of this read: author HTML, and publish Markdown only when a loaded skill explicitly instructs it - a Markdown publish keeps its filename as its title and takes almost none of the craft below, and is never a way to save time.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first - CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world - its materials, instruments, vernacular - is where distinctive choices come from. Whatever the treatment, carry at least one detail only this subject would have - its real units and scales, its document conventions, its terms of art - as content, not ornament; it costs a plain page nothing. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. Google Fonts is the one font host the Artifact CSP admits - link it directly (`<link rel="stylesheet" href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css2?family=...&display=swap">`); a face from anywhere else must be inlined as a @font-face data URI or it falls back silently. Either way, declare a real fallback stack. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Load libraries, don't paste them.** When the page genuinely needs a library - React, a charting or highlighting package - load its UMD build from cdnjs (only the script - a library's stylesheet still has to be inlined) with one pinned `<script src="https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/...">` placed before the inline script that uses its global, instead of inlining the library's source or hand-writing a stand-in; the Artifact tool's description lists the few other script hosts the CSP admits. The page's own CSS and JS, its images and its data ship with the page. Most pages need no library at all - reach for one only when it carries real weight.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject - the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states, not two: an explicit choice stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting stamps *nothing* - most viewers see the un-stamped document, where only `prefers-color-scheme` separates light from dark. Structure the CSS token-level for all three: the bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice beats a dark OS; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also wins in the other direction. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block - a color whose only definition sits behind `[data-theme]` never applies in the un-stamped state, and the page renders one theme's text on the other theme's ground. Two more rules keep each theme resolving as a set: the artifact composites over a ground the viewer paints in *its* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token - a transparent body silently borrows the host's ground; and every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never a literal that only works in one theme. Declare every token in the bare `:root` block before any media or `[data-theme]` block redefines it - a color that exists only inside one of those blocks is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first - don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme - then skip the media query and stamps entirely but still paint the background and every color explicitly, so the page holds on either host ground; make it a choice, not an omission.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content - tables, code, diagrams - gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Compose repeated things as one object.** Cards in a row, label/value pairs down a list, badges on siblings: same edges, baselines and inner padding from one to the next, and a recurring element sits in the same place on each. Let content set a container's height and pick a column count the items fill, so nothing stretches over dead space or sits alone in a row. Text that can outgrow its track wraps or scrolls in its own container; clipped text is a bug.

**Not everything is a card.** Border, fill, radius and shadow each say "separate object" - spend them by role, lifting the one thing that needs it, instead of one radius and one shadow stamped on every block, which flattens the hierarchy. Lead with big-number tiles only when those figures are the point of the page.

**Draw charts to the scale.** One scale places marks, ticks and labels, and every label names a value the chart reaches; chart text takes its color from the theme tokens so it reads in both themes; marks, labels and edges stay clear of one another and inside the drawing's bounds - in SVG, leave room in the viewBox for the outermost labels and give every drawn shape an explicit fill.

**Show the page at rest.** Everything meant to be read is visible once the page has loaded, without scrolling to trigger it - that first still frame is what a thumbnail, a shared link, and a skimming reader all get. A section may animate in, but from a visible resting state, never parked at `opacity: 0` waiting on an observer. Size a hero to what it holds, not to the viewport; a `100vh` opener pushes the page itself out of that first frame. A tool or app opens in a realistic working state - the user's real data where it exists, otherwise example rows, a loaded sample, a form someone plausibly filled, plainly marked as examples and never passed off as the user's own figures - so the first look shows what it does; an empty shell waiting for input shows nothing.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly - their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out - a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen - name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it - no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Name the page like a product, not a caption.** The `<title>` is the artifact's name in the gallery and the browser tab, and it sets the reader's first impression of care. Give the page a real name: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific to the subject - or, for a page that exists to answer one question, that question itself, which is then the page's name. Stop at the name - a title that carries its own explainer after a dash or colon reads as generated filler. The name must also identify the page among many: in the gallery it sits beside dozens of other artifacts, and a generic category label that could sit on any of them fails as a name just as surely as an appended explainer. When a candidate title pairs the name with a generic word - a greeting, a category, a page-type label - the name is the half to keep; a trim that drops the identity and keeps the generic word produces exactly the title that could sit on any page. And the rule removes explainers, it does not impose brevity: a multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished, and shortening it further only makes it generic. The one-sentence publish `description` is where the explanation belongs; the gallery shows it right under the title.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number - a pill, a chip, a severity stripe - so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Start with what the viewer should be able to do on the page, not only what they will read: if it should take input, keep what people change for whoever opens it next, show live data, or ask Claude something, load the `artifact-capabilities` skill now and design around what it makes available to this user; a page that is only read needs none of that.

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan - a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4-6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles - a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

**Write, look once, publish.** Before publishing you may look at the rendered page once - one screenshot of the local file, or the Artifact tool's preview where it offers one - then one pass of edits for what it shows, without a second look. For a page that charts real numbers, take that look rather than skip it, and spend it on the chart. Don't build a test loop around your own file: no repeated screenshots, no pulling the script out to run it through node, no scripts that probe the DOM. That loop spends the session re-checking what a careful write already settled, while the user waits for a link. Then publish, check once any `window.claude` call the preview couldn't run, and stop: the live page is the review surface, and further polish is the user's to ask for. If the user reports something visibly broken - a clipped column, unreadable text, a control that does nothing - fix that and republish once.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world - headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

<result>Report complete at `/Users/za/Documents/king down chess/docs/research/pixel-styles.md` (1072 lines).

**Root cause is not the style — it is four measured faults:**

- Pieces render at **34.7 virtual px** on desktop and **18.9 on a phone** (portrait sizes the ortho camera by `size/aspect`, blowing the view to 14.5 units); target is 48–64. At **0.96 px per model voxel**, the 37-voxel sculpts are pure aliasing.
- `RenderPixelatedPass` ends `gl_FragColor = texel * Strength` — a multiply. It **structurally cannot outline the dark army**.
- Indigo on a dark tile is **1.01 : 1** contrast.
- DB32 nearest-colour matching speckles single surfaces across unrelated hues.

**Tested offline** against `public/sprites/`: 56 px + 4 toon bands + bespoke per-material ramps + a 1 px contrast rim reads as hand-made pixel art. Gain order: **rim &gt; ramps &gt; resolution** — the two cheapest changes are the biggest.

**Recommendation:** toon-pixel hybrid (`MeshToonMaterial` 4-band + inverted-hull outline + `pixelSize` 2 desktop / 1 phone). Of the three sprite schemes on disk use **ivory-charcoal**; `blue-red` puts both armies at the same luminance (1.11 : 1).

Report covers 9 styles with references, recipes, costs, mobile fit, three rankings, a full colour system with hex values, and 5 mock-up parameter blocks. Two research subagents were still running at completion; the report does not depend on them.</result>
