<result>Research complete. Three families below, with real URLs and explicit "unverified" flags. Four of your premises turned out to be wrong — those are flagged inline and summarised at the end.

---

# A) Pre-rendered 3D-to-sprite pipelines

## Donkey Kong Country (Rare, 1994)

**Pipeline (verified).** Models built as NURBS in Alias PowerAnimator on SGI Challenge workstations (~£80,000 each, bought c. 1992), textured, boned, keyframed, rendered frame-by-frame, then compressed with Rare's proprietary "Advanced Computer Modelling" (ACM). A dedicated machine ran ACM with "a proprietary utility similar to Deluxe Paint." Renders took hours; artists left machines overnight. ROM 4 MB; team ~20; ~18 months. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donkey_Kong_Country, https://retromo.substack.com/p/donkey-kong-country-snes-1994

**The two conversion steps, in the artists' own words** (https://episodiccontentmag.com/2016/02/19/dkcountry1_chapter2/):
- Frame decimation — Steve Mayles: "If I handed Chris Sutherland a disk with a 16-frame walk animation, he'd probably only use every other frame." He adds it "would really wound you up when you made this great-looking animation, only for the programmers to chop it to pieces."
- Background tiling — Chris Sutherland: "taking a single pre-rendered background screen and then cutting it up into squares with the correct colour palettes that could replicate that image on the SNES," then "find places where pieces of the image could be repeated elsewhere to save video [memory] space."

**Pixels tall.** Donkey Kong idle ≈ **38×42 / 39×40 px**; Diddy ≈ **35×36**; Cranky 44×56; Expresso 45×65; Enguarde 62×49; Rambi 54×47. Measured from the asset files catalogued at https://www.mariowiki.com/Category:Donkey_Kong_Country_assets. On a 256×224 screen, DK is ~18% of screen height.

**Colours per character.** Hard hardware ceiling: SNES sprites are 4bpp — **16 entries per palette, index 0 transparent, so 15 visible colours**, chosen from 8 sprite palette slots (128 sprite colours total). Sprite tile sizes 8×8 / 16×16 / 32×32 / 64×64, two sizes selectable per game; 128 sprites in OAM, 32 per scanline. https://megacatstudios.com/blogs/retro-development/snes-sprite-engine-design-guidelines, https://www.copetti.org/writings/consoles/super-nintendo/

The important consequence: because the sprite is palette-**indexed**, you can re-upload CGRAM every frame and give each animation frame its own bespoke 15-colour palette. The Killer Instinct SNES patch community does exactly this — "dynamically refresh the player palette each frame to match the existing animation, as 15 colors are more than enough for one sprite frame" (https://www.zeldix.net/t2002p100-killer-instinct-arcade-mega-patch). That is the real answer to "how many colours per character" for this family: 15 *at any instant*, not 15 for the whole character.

**The quantise step.** PowerAnimator's colour quantiser reduced true-RGB renders to 256 colours before the platform-specific pass. This is repeated widely but only forum-sourced (https://www.neogaf.com/threads/the-making-of-donkey-kong-country-killer-instinct-pre-rendered-graphics.1607594/) — treat as **unverified**. Community accounts also say Rare scaled renders to the 8×8/16×16 tile grid and then hand-edited pixels on top (https://www.vg-resource.com/archive/index.php/thread-31192.html, 403s to automated fetch).

**What actually makes or breaks readability in this family.** The best single technical source is the pre-rendered/digitized chapter at https://nfgworld.com/grafx/games/NewBook/PreRendered/PreRendered.pdf. Concrete, quotable findings:
- **Quantisers allocate colours by pixel count**, so a feature occupying 10% of the image loses its colours to the 90%. Verbatim: "if 90% of the image needed red shades then the remaining 10% lost out. Usually this resulted in faces lacking detail, and *Donkey Kong's frickin' eye*." This is the characteristic failure mode of the whole pipeline — small high-salience features (eyes, hands, weapon tips) die in quantisation.
- **Fringing**: sprites are rendered against a matte colour, which "will forever be an inconsistent outline surrounding the sprite, unless it's carefully trimmed." Midway's fix was **rim lights on the models** — a highlight rather than a shadow at the contour, which both kills fringing and doubles as a readability outline.
- Hard palette-reduction numbers: **Primal Rage arcade sprite = 59 colours; SNES port = 14; Mega Drive = 11.**
- Frame-count economics: Cave's *Ketsui* pre-rendered ship uses **7 frames per turn direction**; hand-pixelled *Raiden* uses **2**, mirrored. *Mario vs. Donkey Kong* spends **8 frames on standing still**.
- Palette-swap trick: render **black-and-white sprites and add colour later** (used by Clay Fighter).

## Dead Cells (Motion Twin, 2017)

**Pipeline (primary source, Thomas Vasseur's own deep dive).** https://www.gamedeveloper.com/production/art-design-deep-dive-using-a-3d-pipeline-for-2d-animation-in-i-dead-cells-i-, full walkthrough mirrored at https://sudonull.com/post/14066-Dead-Cells-Using-3D-Pipeline-for-2D-Animation

1. Draw a **2D pixel-art model sheet** first (this step is usually omitted from summaries — the design is authored in pixels, not in 3D).
2. Build model + skeleton from that sheet in **3DS Max**, export **FBX**.
3. A **homebrew renderer** "renders the mesh in a very small size and without antialiasing, giving us that pixelated look."
4. Animate pose-to-pose on keyframes; interpolation frames added only "before or after the key frames. Never in-between."
5. Export each frame as **PNG + a normal map**; shade with "the simplest toon shader"; blend modes and hand-made VFX layered on top.

**Pixels tall: 50.** Vasseur, verbatim: "if the character's [height] in a game is only 50 pixels, then [spending] a heap of time [on the model is wasteful]."

**Your premise about a hand-clean pass is wrong.** Vasseur lists it as an *unsolved cost*, not a step. They could not kill "flickering pixels" automatically, they corrected manually only where forced, and they accepted "disappointing level of details" because hand-cleaning "contradicted their speed-focused workflow." Same sources.

**Colours.** There is **no indexed palette**. Backgrounds and decorations are hand-pixelled in Photoshop, then redrawn as **normal maps** so a dynamic 3D light system tints and reliefs them at runtime — "a torch on the left of a statue will light up the statue from the correct direction while respecting its base colours." Art direction is "saturated palette + Celtic architecture + alchemy." https://80.lv/articles/interview-with-the-developers-of-dead-cells. Per-character colour counts and total frame counts: **unverified**.

**Why it reads.** Runtime lighting, not the sprite, supplies contrast; a 50px silhouette with strong VFX for attack telegraphs.

## Octopath Traveler — this does NOT belong in family A

Octopath is not a 3D-to-sprite pipeline. It is **hand-drawn pixel art, rigged as an articulated puppet, then billboarded into a 3D scene.** Two Japanese primary sources say so explicitly:

- CGWorld, from the *Game Graphics 2019* book (https://cgworld.jp/feature/201908-gg2019-oct.html), verbatim: 「キャラクターの基本サイズは18×32となっており、子供、男性、大柄男性で幅と高さが数ピクセル変わっている。アニメーションの矩形範囲としては最大128×128だが、テクスチャパッキングする際に詰められている。」 — **base character size 18×32 px**, varying by a few pixels for child/male/large-male builds; **animation bounding box max 128×128**, packed at texture-pack time. Animation uses 「Photoshopのパペットワープ機能」 — **Photoshop Puppet Warp** — to reassign animation patterns instantly across differently-designed characters.
- Denfaminicogamer (https://news.denfaminicogamer.jp/kikakuthetower/231218g): pixel art is decomposed into a 「多関節モデル」 (multi-joint model), animated from the rig, then **pixel-level breakage is fixed by hand**. Acquire chose this because they are a 3D polygon studio, not a pixel-art studio.

Independent measurement of rips agrees: OT1 field sprite **16×34 px**, 27 colours/frame (sheet 81 colours, 8-bit indexed); OT1 battle sprite **34×51**; OT2 battle sprite **34×56 native**. https://www.spriters-resource.com/nintendo_switch/octopathtraveler/

**The 3D/effects half.** UE4. Verbatim from Square Enix's 4Gamer Business technical article (https://business.4gamer.net/article/1810/18101801/): 「UE4のPaper FlipBook機能により2D画像を2Dスプライトに動きアニメーションを使用している」 (Paper Flipbook drives the sprites); 「ポストプロセスエフェクトには、UE4の標準機能でColorGrading、Bloom、DOF、Bokeh、Lens Flares、Ambient Cubemap、Vignetteを使用している」 — the full post stack is **ColorGrading, Bloom, DOF, Bokeh, Lens Flares, Ambient Cubemap, Vignette**, via stock PostProcessVolume; Maya for prototyping. Tilt-shift plus point lights casting character shadows onto 3D terrain: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HD-2D

Producer Masashi Takahashi's own definition: "we left in a good amount of pixel aesthetic and compounded that with a three-dimensional environment, and we created this incredibly lush visual style that we call HD-2D." He also rejected the obvious alternative — simply making thicker pixels "ends up looking worse somehow." Pixel artist **Shizuka Morimoto**: "At first, I started using some techniques to add dramatic lighting to the 2D art, but Asano told me that wasn't enough… Once I started incorporating some 3D elements, it really gave the visuals a sense of depth." https://nintendoeverything.com/octopath-traveler-devs-on-character-origins-visual-style-initial-hd-rumble-plans-much-more/

**Scaling mechanism.** Sprites are nearest-neighbour textures scaled and rotated in a PBR renderer; **temporal AA** hides the resulting artifacts. This is why Octopath's "pixels" are not a consistent size on screen. https://www.neogaf.com/threads/the-last-night-dev-tim-soret-on-pixel-art-filtering-and-where-octopath-traveler-went-wrong.1463728/ Digital Foundry: native **720p docked / 1024×576 handheld, 30 fps** (https://nintendoeverything.com/octopath-traveler-technical-analysis/).

## Contrast cases: Blasphemous and Owlboy

**Blasphemous (The Game Kitchen).** Pure hand-pixelled, no 3D. Aseprite for drawing/animation, sheets imported into Unity; Photoshop and Pixaki/iPad Pro also used by pixel artist Andrey Gogiya (https://www.behance.net/gallery/85604481/Pixel-arts-and-animations-for-game-Blasphemous). Animator Raúl Vivar works by manipulating existing frames for fast iteration (https://www.gameanim.com/2021/02/19/blasphemous-pixel-art-animation-time-lapses/). Designer interview on the "purist" approach: https://www.hollywoodreporter.com/movies/movie-news/blasphemous-designer-purist-approach-games-pixel-art-style-1238370/ (paywalled). **Sprite dimensions, colour counts and frame counts: unverified** — no dev source publishes them. A Behance commenter guesses 64×64 for the Penitent One; unconfirmed.

**Owlboy (D-Pad Studio).** Nine years, 2007–2016, all pixel art hand-drawn in MS Paint then Photoshop CS3 (https://www.dpadstudio.com/presskitOwlboy.html). The studio **coined "hi-bit"** and defines it as "any pixel art game that operates beyond the limitations of the old 8/16/32-bit consoles" — their blog also names **640×360 as the emerging base resolution** for the category (scaling cleanly to 720p/1080p/4K), citing Hyper Light Drifter, Iconoclasts, Shovel Knight. https://dpadstudio.com/Blog/postHibit.html. Owlboy's own internal resolution is widely reported as 640×360 with Otus **39 px tall**, but the only source I could trace is a bachelor's thesis (Olli Heikkinen, *Hi-Bit Pixel Graphics*, Theseus 10024/501981, 403s to fetch) — **treat the 39 px as unverified**. Andersen's stated animation technique is sub-pixelling and in-between colours to imply motion between whole pixels.

---

# B) 32-bit isometric pre-rendered

## Diablo 1 (Blizzard North / Condor, 1996)

**Directions: 8.** The 1994 Condor pitch doc, verbatim: "The three-quarter perspective requires eight directions of movement, three of which can sometimes be flipped. Each monster will have multiple graphic deaths and will require at least 50 frames of animations." 640×480 SVGA. https://archive.org/stream/diablo_pitch/diablo_pitch_djvu.txt. Confirmed in the shipped engine: `enum class Direction { South, SouthWest, West, NorthWest, North, NorthEast, East, SouthEast }` (https://github.com/diasurgical/devilutionX/blob/master/Source/engine/direction.hpp).

**"3D Studio for Diablo 1" is unverified.** No primary source names the tool. It *is* verified for Diablo II — postmortem, verbatim: "Almost all of *Diablo II*'s in-game and cinematic art was constructed and rendered in 3D Studio Max," and "An in-house tool would render the files from many different angles (eight for all monsters, 16 for player characters)." 640×480, 8-bit colour. https://www.gamedeveloper.com/design/postmortem-blizzard-s-i-diablo-ii-i-

**Sprite widths** (from the original data tables, https://github.com/diasurgical/devilutionX/blob/master/assets/txtdata/classes/warrior/sprites.tsv):

| class | stand | walk | attack | bow | death |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| warrior | 96 | 96 | 128 | 96 | 128 |
| rogue | 96 | 96 | 128 | 128 | 128 |
| sorcerer | 96 | 96 | 128 | 128 | 128 |

Monsters use **only three widths across all 112 rows**: 96 px (9), 128 px (69), 160 px (34) — https://github.com/diasurgical/devilutionX/blob/master/assets/txtdata/monsters/monstdat.tsv. **Frame heights are not stored** in CL2 (derived from decoded pixel count ÷ width), so "96×96 square" is an assumption, **unverified**.

Warrior frame counts: idle 10, walk 8, sword attack 16, axe 20, bow 16, block 2, death 20, cast 20, recovery 6.

**Tiles.** Base CEL frame **32×32 px**; floor/wall-bottom split into two 32×16 halves. Subtile ground layer **64×32**; full subtile **64×160** (10 frames) or **64×256** (16, Town/Hell). TIL megatile **128×192** (Town/Hell 128×288). https://github.com/savagesteel/d1-file-formats/blob/master/PC-Mac/CEL.md, .../MIN-TIL.md

**Palette handling — all of Diablo's colour work is index remapping.**
- PAL = 768 bytes, 256 colours, 3 bytes RGB; `offset = 3 × index`. .../PAL.md
- **TRN = 256 bytes, one index→index remap.** This is Diablo's team-colour analogue: monster variants are the *same art* plus a different TRN. Zombie / Ghoul / Rotting Carcass / Black Death all share `zombie\zombie` art with trn `—`, `bluered`, `grey`, `yellow`. Fallen One / Carver / Dark One share `falspear`. Skeletons: `skelaxe\white`, `\skelt`, `\black`. .../TRN.md plus the `trnFile` column of monstdat.tsv
- **Lighting is also remapping**: 16 levels × 256-entry tables (`LightsMax = 15`, `LightTableSize = 256`). https://github.com/diasurgical/devilutionX/blob/master/Source/engine/lighting_defs.hpp
- **Colour cycling is confined to low indices**: caves `CycleColors(1, 31)`; crypt lava `CycleColorsReverse(1, 15)` + glow `(16, 31)` — "Lava has 15 colors and the glow 16, so the full animation has 240 frames before it loops"; hive `(1, 8)` + `(9, 15)`. https://github.com/diasurgical/devilutionX/blob/master/Source/engine/palette.cpp

## Fallout 1/2 (Interplay)

**FRM format** (https://fodev.net/files/fo2/frm.html, https://github.com/falltergeist/libfalltergeist/wiki/Frm-file-format). Fixed **0x3E-byte header**: version, fps, action_frame, frames_per_direction, `int16 x_offsets[6]`, `int16 y_offsets[6]`, `uint32 data_offsets[6]`. Frame record: width, height, size, x, y, then one **palette index byte per pixel**.

**Directions: 6.** "Rotations use six directions: 0 northeast, 1 east, 2 southeast, 3 southwest, 4 west, and 5 northwest" (https://fodev.net/files/fo2/map.html). Critter art also splits into `.fr0`–`.fr5`. Equal consecutive `data_offsets` means that rotation reuses the previous one's frames — the engine's cheap mirroring.

The anchor is "the center of the bottom edge of the visible frame," and per-frame x/y are **motion deltas, not crop origins**. That is how feet stay planted while the bounding box changes size.

**Tile size 80×36 — verified from engine source, not a wiki.** `squareTileToScreenXY()` in https://github.com/alexbatalov/fallout2-ce/blob/main/src/tile.cc steps `+48/−12` on one diagonal and `+32/+24` on the other: 48+32 = **80** wide, 12+24 = **36** tall. Corroborated by `static int _intensity_map[3280]` = 80 × 41.

Two separate grids per elevation: **200×200 hex for objects, 100×100 square for floor/roof tiles.**

**Palette.** 256 entries, index 0 transparent, 255 black. Cycled ranges (https://fodev.net/files/fo2/fo_colors.html):

| indices | n | meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 229–232 | 4 | green — radioactive slime |
| 233–237 | 5 | blue — computer screens |
| 238–242 | 5 | fire #1 |
| 243–247 | 5 | fire #2 |
| 248–253 | 6 | brown — shores/water |

Lighting is a 2D LUT: `intensityColorTable[colorIndex][intensity &gt;&gt; 9]`. **Fallout has no team/player colour system** — nothing reserved for faction tint.

**3D tool: unverified and probably not 3D Studio.** The NMA thread reports Lightwave plus Photoshop touch-up (https://www.nma-fallout.com/threads/what-was-the-technology-behind-fallout-1-2-s-graphics.218299/ — 403s to automated fetch; open manually before citing).

## Baldur's Gate 1/2 (Infinity Engine) — the richest palette-range system of the set

**BAM v1** (https://gibberlings3.github.io/iesdp/file_formats/ie_formats/bam_v1.htm). 20-byte header; frame count, **max 255 cycles**, compressed-colour-index byte. Frame entry carries width, height and **signed centre X/Y**. Palette = **256 RGBQUADs, BB GG RR AA order**. Transparency index = "the first occurrence of RGB(0,255,0)"; if absent, index 0. RLE: any non-transparent byte is literal; transparent index followed by `x` means `x+1` transparent pixels. **BG2 caps displayed frames at 256×256.** Actual avatar pixel size is per-frame variable — **no fixed sprite dimension; unverified.**

**Directions: 9 stored → 16 rendered.** "Most modern animation types use 9 base directions (S, SSW, SW, WSW, W, WNW, NW, NNW, N) with eastern directions calculated by the engine through mirroring." https://gibberlings3.github.io/iesdp/file_formats/ie_formats/ini_anim.htm

**The colour-range map, exact** (from NearInfinity's `SpriteDecoder`, the reference implementation: https://github.com/Argent77/NearInfinity/blob/master/src/org/infinity/resource/cre/decoder/SpriteDecoder.java):

```java
if (locationIndex &gt;= 0 &amp;&amp; locationIndex &lt; 7) retVal = 4 + locationIndex * 12;
final int srcOfs = 4;  final int dstOfs = 88;
final int srcLen = 12; final int dstLen = 8;
palette[2] = 0xFF000000; palette[3] = 0xFF000000;
```

- **7 colour locations × 12 palette entries**, starting at index 4: `4–15`, `16–27`, `28–39`, `40–51`, `52–63`, `64–75`, `76–87`.
- **Indices 88–255 are auto-generated blend ramps** — all 21 pairs of the 7 ranges get an 8-entry interpolation. 88 + 21×8 = 256 exactly. This is why IE characters stay clean where two dyed materials meet.
- **Indices 2 and 3 forced to opaque black** (shadow).

Location meanings (opcode 51, https://gibberlings3.github.io/iesdp/opcodes/bgee.htm): `00` belt buckle/amulet, `01` **minor colour**, `02` **major colour**, `03` skin, `04` strap/minor trim, `05` armour, `06` hair. Equipment has its own blocks: weapon 16–22, shield 32–40, helmet 48–54. Values live in CRE header bytes `0x2c` metal, `0x2d` minor, `0x2e` major, `0x2f` skin, `0x30` leather, `0x31` armor, `0x32` hair (https://gibberlings3.github.io/iesdp/file_formats/ie_formats/cre_v1.htm), each selecting a gradient id from a 256-entry table (`00h–73h` valid everywhere, `78h–FFh` EE-only).

**Worth stating in your report:** IE areas are **painted backdrops cut into a 64×64 grid**, not a tiled isometric ground. TIS tiles are 64×64, **each with its own 256-colour palette**, 5120 bytes per tile (1024 palette + 4096 indices), index 0 transparent. https://gibberlings3.github.io/iesdp/file_formats/ie_formats/tis_v1.htm

## Age of Empires 2 (SLP) — the player-colour trick you asked about

**Verbatim from the openage format docs** (https://github.com/SFTtech/openage/blob/master/doc/media/slp-files.md):

&gt; "In AoK and SWGB, blue player colors begin at index 16 in the palette, red at index 32, green at index 48, yellow at index 64, orange at index 80, cyan/teal at index 96, purple at index 112, and grey at index 128."
&gt; "The real palette index is `player_color_palette_index + player * 16`, where `player` is the player ID you're drawing for (1-8)."

So **indices 16–143 are eight contiguous 16-entry player blocks.** The SLP stores only a **0–15 offset inside the block**; the artist paints a 16-step ramp and the engine picks the base. That is why AoE2 units keep full shading under any team colour. (AoE1:DE uses separate player-colour palettes, indices 0x00–0x7F.)

Commands:
- `0x06` player-colour draw, `0x0A` fill player colour
- `0x0B` shadow draw, against a **shadow palette that is a darkened copy of palette 50500**
- **`0x4E` Outline 1**, `palette_index = player * 16` — "the outline that surrounds a unit and is seen when a unit is obstructed, such as when it is behind a building or tree… Its color is determined by the team color of the player." **This is the single most explicit readability mechanism anywhere in the three families.**
- `0x6E` Outline 2, `palette_index = 243`

**Directions: 5 rendered → 8 shown.** "each animation has 10 keyframes and 5 directions. The other 3 directions are generated later by flipping the sprite on the y axis… 50 frames per SLP." Projectiles: 35 keyframes × 5 = 175 frames.

**Correct your 96×48.** openage's terrain doc gives a rhombus of **97 px across × 49 rows** (its diagram annotates the widest row as the 25th of 49); note the doc's prose labels are transposed and contradict its own diagram. `frame_id = (x % tc) + ((y % tc) * tc)`, `tc = 10` for the 100-tile terrains; terrain.drs holds 27 SLPs. https://github.com/SFTtech/openage/blob/master/doc/media/terrain.md

Per-frame `slp_frame_info` stores int32 width, height, hotspot_X, hotspot_Y — **sizes are per-frame variable, there is no single "unit sprite size."**

**AoE2 Definitive Edition.** Only sourced original-scale figure is **~100 px high** per unit, from the DE team. Verbatim from https://www.gamedeveloper.com/design/rebuilding-a-classic-in-i-age-of-empires-ii-definitive-edition-i-: "Literally every asset in the game had to be remade"; "We didn't have any of the original assets. Those were all lost"; roughly **four times the fidelity**, worked from the old sprites as reference to preserve silhouettes so players still recognise "that unit as being a camel… 20 years later"; and "This game is still 2D. A lot of people think it's 3D now, but it's actually 2D sprites."

Format evolution:
- **SMX** (launch): up to 3 layers per frame — main graphic, shadow, outline — selected by a `frame_type` bitfield, per-frame `palette_number`. Still indexed. https://github.com/SFTtech/openage/blob/master/doc/media/smx-files.md
- **SLD** (build 66692+): **abandons indexed palettes.** DXT1/BC1 main + damage mask, DXT4/BC4 shadow + **player-colour mask**. Up to 5 layers. Player colour is now "technically greyscale images… treated as an index for looking up the actual player color value from a palette." https://github.com/SFTtech/openage/blob/master/doc/media/sld-files.md
- **32 directions** is an **AoE1:DE** figure, not AoE2 DE: "Where the original units would only turn in 8 directions, units now… 32 directions," and "every asset in the game has been rendered 3 times" for HD→4K. https://www.forgottenempires.net/age-of-empires-definitive-edition-is-it-a-3d-or-a-2d-game **Whether AoE2 DE also uses 32: unverified. Do not transfer the claim.**

## Heroes of Might &amp; Magic III

**First, a correction: the HoMM3 adventure map is not isometric.** It is a square **32×32 px** grid (VCMI draws road halves as `Rect(0,16,32,16)` / `Rect(0,0,32,16)` — https://github.com/vcmi/vcmi/blob/develop/client/mapView/MapRenderer.cpp). Battles are a flat hex stage.

**DEF palette = 256 RGB triplets.** Per-sprite header carries format, width, height, fullWidth, fullHeight, leftMargin, topMargin — **per-frame variable, no fixed creature size (unverified)**. https://github.com/vcmi/vcmi/blob/develop/client/render/CDefFile.cpp

**Reserved indices, exact** (https://github.com/vcmi/vcmi/blob/develop/clientsdl2/render/ScalableImage.cpp, comment "First 8 colors in def palette used for transparency"):

| idx | magic RGB in the DEF | engine substitutes |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | (0,255,255) | full transparency |
| 1 | (255,150,255) | shadow border, α=64 (battle + map) |
| 2 | (255,100,255) | shadow border, α=64 (fog of war) |
| 3 | (255,50,255) | shadow body, α=128 (fog of war) |
| 4 | (255,0,255) | shadow body, α=128 (battle + map) |
| 5 | (255,255,0) | **selection / owner flag colour** |
| 6 | (180,0,255) | shadow body below selection, α=128 |
| 7 | (0,255,0) | shadow border below selection, α=64 |

**Player colour = indices 224–255** (https://github.com/vcmi/vcmi/blob/develop/client/render/Graphics.cpp):

```cpp
SDL_SetPaletteColors(targetPalette, palette, 224, 32);  // 32-entry owner ramp
SDL_SetPaletteColors(targetPalette, &amp;color,   5,   1);  // single flag colour at index 5
```

The 32 colours come from `DATA/PLAYERS.PAL` — 8 players × 32 colours, 4 bytes each (4th byte is flags, not alpha), from file offset 24. Unowned uses `config/NEUTRAL.PAL`. Flat player colours: red `ff0000`, blue `3152ff`, tan `9c7352`, green `429429`, orange `ff8400`, purple `8c29a5`, teal `099ca5`, pink `c67b8c`, neutral `848484`.

Net: HoMM3 reserves **8 indices for transparency + two-level shadow, 32 for the owner ramp, 1 for the flag** — 41 of 256.

**3ds Max pipeline: unverified.** The best retrospective (https://www.gamedeveloper.com/production/heroes-of-might-magic-iii-remembering-erathia-15-years-later) names lead artist Phelan Sykes, assistant lead Rebecca Riel, illustrator George Almond, lead animator Adam McCarthy, and "over 10,000 pieces of artwork" — but no software, resolutions or frame counts.

## Disciples II

Weakest evidence of the six, and the weakest structural fit (flat 2D battle stage, 2D strategic grid). **3ds Max pre-render, sprite dimensions, colour counts, frame counts, team colour: all unverified.** GameSpot Designer Diary #9 is the right primary source but 403s: https://www.gamespot.com/articles/disciples-ii-dark-prophecy-designer-diary-9/1100-2801857/. Assets are in `.ff` archives; tools that could reverse the numbers: https://github.com/Vanosz/Disciples2-ROTE-Editor-Releases, https://github.com/VladimirMakeev/D2ModdingToolset. Reference sheets: https://www.spriters-resource.com/pc_computer/disciplesii/

## Facing-direction table

| game | rendered | displayed |
|---|---|---|
| Diablo 1 | 8 | 8 |
| Diablo II | 8 monsters / 16 players | same |
| Fallout 1/2 | 6 | 6 |
| Baldur's Gate | 9 | 16 (mirrored) |
| AoE2 | 5 | 8 (mirrored) |
| AoE1 DE | 32 | 32 |
| AoE2 DE | unverified | unverified |

## What actually keeps units legible on a tiled ground

Ranked by strength of evidence, all verified above:

1. **A dedicated outline layer in the owner's colour** (AoE2 `0x4E`, carried forward into SMX and SLD as a first-class layer). The only mechanism in the whole set documented *as* a readability feature.
2. **A dedicated shadow channel.** AoE2 `0x0B` against a darkened palette; HoMM3 bakes shadow into indices 1–4 at 25%/50% black so the artist paints shadow *as index* and the engine controls opacity.
3. **Team colour as a reserved ramp, never a flat colour.** AoE2 128 indices (16–143), HoMM3 32 + 1, Infinity Engine 84 base + 168 auto-blended.
4. **Lighting by index remap.** Diablo 16 levels × 256 tables; Fallout `intensityColorTable[colour][intensity&gt;&gt;9]`. Quantised contrast bands preserve silhouette edges at small size.
5. **Silhouette preservation as an explicit remaster constraint** (AoE2 DE).

**Explicitly not verified:** top-lighting conventions, fixed key-light angles, outline-width rules, or any written silhouette guideline from any of these six teams. The per-direction sprite sets *imply* a fixed camera and fixed light, but no document I reached says so. Do not invent one.

---

# C) HD-2D and modern chunky pixel

Numbers marked **[measured]** come from pixel-measuring community rips (bounding box per frame, unique RGB per frame, edge-pixel histogram, integer-upscale test). Rips may be post-render captures, so colour counts are softer evidence than dimensions.

## Octopath Traveler I / II

Covered in full under family A above (18×32 authored base size; **[measured]** field 16×34 / battle 34×51, OT2 battle **34×56 native**, ~10% taller — which matches director Miyauchi's stated reason: raising character height was needed because "if we only increased the resolution of the background, the characters would look strange," and combat presentation changes a lot with height. https://nintendoeverything.com/octopath-traveler-ii-devs-on-the-games-evolved-use-of-hd-2d-and-more/).

**[measured] outline.** Dominant edge colour `#181818` (RGB 24,24,24), with hue-shifted dark variants (`#301810` warm brown, `#2A1A18` red-brown) on warm regions — black outline with **selective colour-shifted outlining**. 72–77% of edge pixels below luminance 32; 88–96% below 64. That very hard contour is what lets a 16 px-wide sprite survive on a lit, bloom-heavy 3D diorama.

Producer Takahashi on why UE4: "UE4 is very good at both 3D environments and 3D graphics." Asano, 2026: "HD-2D, which was born with Octopath Traveler, has gained recognition over the past eight years" (https://www.dualshockers.com/square-enix-hd-2d-remakes-teased-by-octopath-traveler-asano/). Producers have said HD-2D "costs more than you'd think" (https://nintendoeverything.com/triangle-strategy-producers-say-hd-2d-costs-more-than-youd-think-internal-teams-shared-knowledge-to-help-with-its-implementation/).

## Triangle Strategy (Artdink / Square Enix)

The single most useful entry for your tactics-grid question, because the pixel artist gave literal numbers.

**Outline, verbatim** (Nintendo DREAM pixel-art interview, https://www.ndw.jp/trianglestrategy_pixelart-interview/): 「キャラクターを縁取るアウトラインの一番暗い色は真っ黒ではなく、RGB（※）すべての値が24の黒を使用しています。」 — **the darkest outline colour is not pure black; it is RGB(24,24,24).** Shadows use near-black greys rather than pure black.

**Directions, verbatim, same source:** 「最初に衣装を着せていないキャラクターを8方向分描き、Photoshop上でグルグル回して確認して違和感が出ないように調整しました。」 — base undressed characters drawn in **8 directions**, then rotated in Photoshop to check for discomfort. But: 「スケジュールや工数の都合で8方向のグラフィックを描いたのはセレノア・ロラン・フレデリカ・ベネディクトの4人だけなんです」 — **only four characters (Serenoa, Roland, Frederica, Benedict) actually shipped full 8-direction graphics**, for schedule reasons. Sole tool: Adobe Photoshop. Adding one pose meant duplicating it across every character.

**[measured]** unit sprites **17–22 px wide × 35–41 px tall** (typical 19×38), **29–48 unique colours per frame**, true 1× pixel grid (0.2% of 2×2 blocks uniform). Edge colours `#181818` plus heavy hue-matched dark outlines (`#3E1818`, `#562323` on Frederica's red dress) — more selective outlining than Octopath, because units must read against many ground types from four camera angles. https://www.spriters-resource.com/pc_computer/trianglestrategy/

**Why Artdink got the job**, and the design rule that falls out of it (https://nintendoeverything.com/triangle-strategy-devs-on-how-the-game-uses-accurate-hd-2d/): Square Enix had several studios make HD-2D demos; Acquire judged only Artdink's "accurate," because Artdink "prepared a deformed image at the writing material level, before adding realistic effects to 'build it up'." Asano: "If you reduce a photorealistic image, it looks as if you're simply lowering the image quality." And the explicit fidelity cap: **"As the proportions change to look more realistic, the resolution increases and the pixels become smaller, making them look more like illustrations rather than pixel art."** They capped sprite fidelity on purpose.

The rotatable camera was the hard part — Asano: "Octopath Traveler had a fixed camera, but in tactical RPGs, you need to be able to rotate the map 360 degrees." https://www.destructoid.com/triangle-strategy-interview-producers-asano-arai-square-enix-hd-2d-tactics-rpg/

**Grid takeaway: ~19 × 38 px is what Square Enix shipped as a readable unit on a rotatable tactics grid.**

## Live A Live remake (2022)

Best technical source is Square Enix's own **UNREAL FEST WEST '22** talk, written up at https://gamemakers.jp/article/2023_02_21_30973/. Concrete techniques:

- **Deliberately weaker DOF and vignette** than Octopath ("strong DoF + vignette + shallow tone colour"), because the camera moves backwards often and the game wants a hot, not dreamy, feel. Chromatic aberration added as a modern touch.
- **Boss silhouette trick:** the main dot art is displayed with **two additional enlarged copies of the same dot art stacked behind it** to strengthen the contour.
- **Grounding indoors:** no DirectionalLight indoors, so circular shadows are drawn as **decals**, intensity tuned per map.
- **Readability on the checker battle grid:** players could not tell which squares a monster occupied, so **the shadow cast on grid tiles was made part of the attack-valid range** — the shadow *is* the hitbox display.
- Pipeline: GrayBox → rough model/light using the original pixel art → final model + sprite after level sign-off, because HD-2D uniquely needs modellers and pixel artists on the same asset.
- Rendering: moved from **deferred to mobile forward** mid-project; **FXAA and screen percentage both held at 100%** to preserve dot fidelity.
- Scale: 560+ skills, 307 built from reusable subsequences across motion (99), effects (177), camera (111), hit lighting (68), screen effects (13), field lighting (3).

**[measured]** field/NPC sprites **19–23 × 27–32 px** (typical 20×28), **21 colours per frame**, 46 per character sheet, native 1×. Outline `#181818` plus warm dark variants. So Live A Live is the **chunkiest and lowest-colour** of the Square Enix HD-2D set, sitting *below* Octopath's field height. https://www.spriters-resource.com/nintendo_switch/livealive/

Tokita on why HD-2D over 3D: a full 3D remake "would have changed the look too much." https://nintendoeverything.com/live-a-live-dev-on-remakes-origins-why-its-hd-2d/ SNES-vs-remake pixel delta: **unverified**.

## Sea of Stars (Sabotage)

Sabotage's own press kit: "Our custom-made render pipeline allows the creation of a breathtaking world coming to life by pushing the limits of 2D pixel-art games," with full dynamic lighting. https://sabotagestudio.com/presskits/sea-of-stars/

Thierry Boulanger: "With the bigger pieces, we really went full-on pixel art. No shortcuts." / "It's full screen, the pixel art is point by point." / animations "took weeks, they took over a month, each of them" / on lighting: "It gave volume to the environment." https://screenrant.com/sea-stars-interview-thierry-boulanger/ One engineer (Jeakim Hamel) owned dynamic lighting from 2018 onward, and "the pipeline and all production assets had to answer to the technical needs of the dynamic lighting." https://mobilesyrup.com/2023/09/11/sea-of-stars-level-design-sabotage-studio-thierry-boulanger-interview/ Art overview: https://www.megavisions.net/the-art-of-sea-of-stars-a-sea-of-pixels/

**Unverified and commonly mis-stated:** normal maps (every "Sea of Stars uses normal maps" source is inference; Sabotage only say "custom render pipeline" + "dynamic lighting"); **internal resolution** — the widely repeated **640×360 traces to Steam forum speculation, do not print it**; sprite dimensions and colour counts (no rips available).

## Eiyuden Chronicle: Hundred Heroes

Unity, fully 3D environments with 2D pixel characters. Producer/AD Junichi Murakami: they chose "an evolved pixel art style that does not depict everything in a realistic style," and Murayama insisted it read as "100% new artistic creation," not nostalgia. Character formats deliberately differ per character so personalities read distinctly. **Merging 2D sprites with 3D environments was "actually very, very, very hard" and took about two years**, because 3D camera angles must not break the 2D read. https://news.xbox.com/en-us/2024/04/23/talking-creative-minds-behind-eiyuden-chronicle-hundred-heroes/, https://www.cgmagonline.com/interviews/eiyuden-chronicle-hundred-heroes-murayama/

Suikoden lineage is literal: Murayama (design/scenario), **Junko Kawano** (character design, Suikoden I &amp; II), Murakami, Komuta.

**Sprite resolution, colour count, outline: all unverified.** No rips, no dev figures.

## Tactics Ogre: Reborn — and the answer to "upscaled or redrawn"

**It is explicitly not HD-2D.** Producer Hiroaki Kato: "No, we didn't think about making it HD-2D." They considered 3D, rejected it, and kept the original's "really well-done pixel art," bringing "that sort of resolution and that sort of fidelity to new hardware." https://www.nintendolife.com/news/2022/09/tactics-ogre-reborn-producer-we-didnt-think-about-making-it-hd-2d Minagawa (AD) and Yoshida (character art) returned.

**[measured] — this settles it:**
- PSP *Let Us Cling Together* (2010) battle frames: **~14–18 × 26–27 px**, 21–32 colours/frame. https://www.spriters-resource.com/psp/tacticsogreletusclingtogether/
- Reborn battle frames: **60–68 × 100–112 px** (typical 64×104), **27–32 colours/frame**, 448–454 colours per full sheet. https://www.spriters-resource.com/pc_computer/tacticsogrereborn/
- Ratio is almost exactly **4× linear** — but **not** a nearest-neighbour 4× upscale: only **36% of 4×4 blocks are uniform**, while **84% of 2×2 blocks are uniform**. A naive 4× filter would score ~100% on the 4×4 test. Reading: the art sits on an effective **2× chunky pixel grid with a real minority of 1×-resolution detail and cleanup** — i.e. redrawn at higher resolution in the original style, exactly as claimed.
- **Outline is the interesting deviation:** edge pixels are overwhelmingly `#302820` (48,40,32) — a **dark warm grey-brown, not black**. Mean edge luminance 57–58 vs Octopath's 32–38. Reborn deliberately softens the contour relative to the HD-2D games.

Pre-release detail comparison: https://wccftech.com/tactics-ogre-reborn-early-comparison-highlights-redrawn-sprites-and-backgrounds/

## Into the Breach (Subset Games)

**Grid: 8×8 tiles per battle.** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Into_the_Breach The small board made 100 hand-designed maps cheaper than procedural generation: https://thinkygames.com/games/into-the-breach/

**Justin Ma on readability — the quotable material** (https://www.gamedeveloper.com/design/-i-into-the-breach-i-dev-on-ui-design-sacrifice-cool-ideas-for-the-sake-of-clarity-every-time-):
- "As a game design principle, we would sacrifice cool ideas for the sake of clarity every time."
- "Our requirement that the player has to understand what's going on in any situation restricted our game design options considerably."
- On the playtest failure that forced it: "You could just see all these damage indicators all over the map. We were fine with that because we knew exactly what every enemy did… but it was very clear, as soon as we handed it to someone else, that we needed to make it very clear who was doing what."
- On animated tooltips: "I still think it's the most important decision we made about this. You could type out a hundred times, 'Damages a tile and pushes adjacent tiles,' but showing that little animation of them moving is a thousand times more effective."

GDC 2019 "Into the Breach Design Postmortem" (Matthew Davis) is paywalled: https://www.gdcvault.com/play/1025772/-Into-the-Breach-Design; free write-up https://80.lv/articles/gdc-2019-an-inside-look-at-into-the-breach

**[measured] mech sprite size** (bounding boxes from game-ripped PNGs via the wiki's MediaWiki API): **33–47 px wide × 26–50 px tall, typical ~42 × 36**. Combat Mech 42×35, Artillery 44×34, Siege 41×26, Jet 35×42, Electric 44×50; Vek: Beetle 39×33, Hornet 33×49, Scarab 36×39. **Colour counts and outline treatment unverified** (Cloudflare blocked downloads; assets live in `resource.dat`, extractable with the FTL unpacker: https://steamcommunity.com/app/590380/discussions/0/2650805212055481389/). Modder-practical figure: custom mechs built at 60×52, needing normal / no-shadow / hangar-highlight / idle / broken / water / broken-water variants (https://subsetgames.com/forum/viewtopic.php?t=32923).

**The design lesson, and it is the most transferable one here:** ITB puts a ~42×36 px unit on an 8×8 board and then puts *all* tactical state into overlays — arrows, damage previews, tile highlights, animated tooltips. The sprite only has to answer "which mech is this": silhouette plus one accent colour. The UI carries everything else.

## Unicorn Overlord (Vanillaware)

**Not pixel art.** Hand-painted parts animated as jointed puppets. Kamitani, in Gamasutra's "King of 2D" (https://www.gamedeveloper.com/business/king-of-2d-vanillaware-s-george-kamitani): the studio built a **proprietary character editor over about a year, "largely based off Flash and other well-known packages"**; complex creatures that look 3D use a hand-shaping method they call **"tebineri"**; and critically — **original artwork is drawn at roughly double display size and compressed down**, which is why moving to HD "wouldn't require substantial additional effort."

Unicorn Overlord specifics: conceived by **Takafumi Noma** in 2014, who was director, programmer and lead artist; ~10-year development. https://www.inverse.com/gaming/unicorn-overlord-interview-vanillaware, https://automaton-media.com/en/news/20240307-28225/

**Any pixel resolution figure for Unicorn Overlord: unverified.** The only defensible statement is Kamitani's "~2× display size, then compressed."

---

# Minimum pixels per character for silhouette readability

**There is no canonical industry rule.** I checked the canonical references directly and they give principles, not thresholds. Report it that way.

**What named artists actually say:**

- **Raymond Schlitter (SLYNYRD)** is the most concrete. His working standard is **16×32 px** — "1 tile wide by 2 tiles tall is one of the most common sprite sizes, and is the size I've chosen" — sized from the tile unit, because "basing the character size on tile units allows the sprite to naturally fit in the grid-like structure of the tile based world." Head takes **a third to half** of sprite height to keep expression readable. https://www.slynyrd.com/blog/2019/10/21/pixelblog-22-top-down-character-sprites His floor case runs an **8 px-tall figure** on an 8×8 tilemap, where "everything is there for basic readability… the sense of wonder is peaked by the lack of detail." https://www.slynyrd.com/blog/2023/11/26/pixelblog-47-tiny-pixels
- **Kiwi (2dwillneverdie)** gives the one hard threshold I found: **"When the sprite's under 50 pixels, don't draw leopard spots."** Below that the goal shifts from accuracy to instant recognition: "Use fewer details and more contrast. Use fewer colors that aren't close to each other." He calls ~120 px "an absolutely fantastic size" for fighter sprites. https://2dwillneverdie.com/tutorial/fix-my-sprite-volume-3/
- **Derek Yu**: start small, because "the hard part is learning how to cram a lot of detail into a few pixels," and "even large sprites never usually exceed 200 by 200 pixels." http://makegames.tumblr.com/post/42648699708/pixel-art-tutorial
- **"Pixel Parmesan"** refuses a number and gives a two-question framework instead: *what is the smallest necessary detail, and how many pixels does that detail need in this style?* Default heuristic: "if in doubt, go smaller." https://pixelparmesan.com/blog/choosing-the-right-resolution-for-your-pixel-art
- **Arne (androidarts)** — the most-cited pixel art tutorial — gives **no numeric minimum at all**. It is explicit about contrast instead: "it's possible to skip outlines all together and simply rely on color contrast to separate things." https://androidarts.com/pixtut/pixelart.htm
- **Cyangmou's "Readability – Pixel Art Style Possibilities"** is the one tutorial explicitly on this topic, indexed at https://lospec.com/pixel-art-tutorials/readability-pixel-art-style-possibilities-by-cyangmou — **the DeviantArt original is now 404**, so I could not read the chart and will not paraphrase it.
- **Michafrar, *Pixel Logic*** has a dedicated **Ch. 4 "Readability"**. https://pixellogicbook.com/

**The empirical anchor is stronger than any tutorial.** Shipped, verified numbers:

| use | size |
|---|---|
| readable tactics unit on a grid | **~16–22 × 27–41 px** (Octopath field 16×34, Live A Live 20×28, Triangle Strategy 19×38) |
| battle-scale pixel character | **~34 × 51–56 px** (Octopath) |
| chunky mech on an 8×8 board | **~42 × 36 px** (Into the Breach) |
| high-res tactics unit | **~64 × 104 px** (Tactics Ogre Reborn) |
| side-scroll action character | **50 px** (Dead Cells), **~38–42 px** (DKC), **39 px** (Owlboy, unverified) |
| historic tactics reference | FFT sheet **256×488**, body-part assembled |

Note that everything under 22 px wide is humanoid/biped. Into the Breach buys readability with a **wider, blockier mass** plus overlays, not with more pixels.

**From the strategy-art side (qualitative but well-sourced).** Blizzard artist Matt McDaid names readability as the single most important element and lists six sub-elements: **"Scale &amp; proportions, Silhouette, Lighting, Color, Exaggeration, Composition."** His working method is consolidating high-frequency detail into low-frequency detail — "If you're texturing a 10 × 2 piece of wood… and it has 4 small nails hammered through the end, maybe just paint 2 larger, chunkier ones instead?" https://80.lv/articles/matt-mcdaid-mastering-the-stylized-art Riot states the silhouette test outright: "Silhouettes are the single most important thing for champion recognition in League. This is basically a champion's shadow: What do they look like shrouded in darkness?" — with one "defining primary characteristic that's unique to that champion" that never changes. https://www.leagueoflegends.com/en-us/news/dev/clarity-in-league/

**Do not cite** spritegen.io, sprixen.com, sprite-ai.art, pixnote.net, spritesheets.ai or generalistprogrammer.com. They dominate current search results for "minimum sprite size," have no named author, and confidently state fabricated figures like "6–12 colours per character."

---

# Shades per material

**Derek Yu gives the hard rule you were looking for:** "Use at most two shades of dark (dark and really dark), and two shades of light (light and really light) on top of your foundation color." That is a **5-step maximum ramp: base + 2 shadow + 2 highlight.** Plus: "The more colors you use, the more distracted your sprite will look. To make a sprite stand out, use only two or three main colors." No pillow-shading, no gradients. http://makegames.tumblr.com/post/42648699708/pixel-art-tutorial

**A step-count table with explicit use cases** (https://art-pia.com/guide-light-shadow-en.html — a studio guide, not a first-party game source, so weight it accordingly):

| steps | use | quoted |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | basic shapes | "splits into two faces; flat" |
| **3** | **standard** | "the minimum for volume" / "the standard for most motifs: characters, clothing, tools" |
| 4 | focal points | "the focal point of the picture, metal or ceramic surfaces" |
| 5+ | large subjects | "a large canvas showing a single subject" |

Its budgeting rule is the useful part: "the single highest-return color you can spend is the third step," and **"five motifs at three steps each will beat one motif at five steps."**

**Per-character limits from named artists.** Schlitter, for a 16×16 style: **"I limited the palette to 3 colors in addition to the outline per character"** (https://www.slynyrd.com/blog/2022/1/25/pixelblog-36-8-bit-adventure). Kiwi cites an artist holding **"no more than 5 different colors per palette"** on 48×48 sprites (https://2dwillneverdie.com/tutorial/fix-my-sprite-volume-3/). Saint11 keeps it non-numeric: "keep your color count low," ramps belong on rounded shapes while flat faces stay uniform to avoid banding (https://saint11.art/pixel_art_articles/article4/).

**Palette-construction ramps are a different number.** Schlitter's general-purpose palette uses **9 swatches per ramp, ~20° hue shift per step, 8 ramps, 128 colours** — but he adds "I never use a lot of colors for any one image." Do not confuse ramp length with per-sprite usage. https://www.slynyrd.com/blog/2018/1/10/pixelblog-1-color-palettes Arne: "Unless you are working with very small palettes (say, 3 colors), you probably need to set up color ramps," never build ramp ends from pure black/white, hue-shift instead — skin is "shadow a grayish warm purple… mid tone orange, and the light almost yellowish." https://androidarts.com/pixtut/pixelart.htm

**Cross-check against shipped work [measured].** A whole Octopath character sheet is **81 colours** across every frame and direction; one field frame uses **27**; Live A Live field frame **21**; Tactics Ogre Reborn 64×104 battle frame **27–32**; Triangle Strategy 19×38 unit frames **29–48**. So production sprites land at roughly **20–35 colours per whole character**, which across 5–8 materials is **3–5 shades per material** — Derek Yu's rule, confirmed empirically.

Curated named-artist shading tutorials: https://lospec.com/pixel-art-tutorials/tags/shading (ENDESGA's "Quicktip: Highlights" is specifically about shading changing per material).

---

# Four premises in your brief that are wrong

1. **Octopath Traveler is not a 3D-to-sprite pipeline.** It is hand-drawn pixel art, decomposed into an articulated puppet, animated with Photoshop Puppet Warp, hand-fixed at pixel level, then billboarded in UE4. Remove it from family A; it only belongs in C.
2. **Dead Cells has no systematic hand-clean pass.** Vasseur lists hand-cleaning as a cost they *refused*, accepting flickering pixels and reduced detail in exchange for speed.
3. **Tactics Ogre Reborn is explicitly not HD-2D** — producer Kato says so directly. It is also not an upscale: the 4×4 uniformity test (36%) rules that out.
4. **AoE2's tile is ~97×49, not 96×48**, and **HoMM3's adventure map is a square 32×32 grid, not isometric.** Also, Unicorn Overlord is not pixel art, and Diablo 1's "3D Studio" attribution is unverified (it is verified for Diablo II only).

**Sources that block automated fetching** and are worth opening in a browser yourself: the Unreal Engine Octopath II developer interview (https://www.unrealengine.com/en-US/developer-interviews/octopath-traveler-ii-builds-a-bigger-bolder-world-in-its-stunning-hd-2d-style), the NMA Fallout technology thread, the Disciples II Designer Diary #9, Square Enix's OT2 press hub, and the Hollywood Reporter Blasphemous interview.</result>

# A) HD PIXEL-3D / PIXEL-SNAPPED 3D

## The core technique (verified, three parts)

**1. Low-res render target + nearest filtering + integer upscale.** Render the 3D scene to a small render texture, point-filter it, blow it up by an integer factor. Non-integer scaling produces columns that scale 2× while others scale 3×, which warps sprites as they scroll — https://notkey.studio/en/tutorials/choosing-the-right-render-resolution-for-a-pixel-art-game/

**2. Texel-snapped camera + sub-pixel screen shift.** This is the whole trick and it is two steps, not one. David Holland states it plainly: "Snap the camera to a view-aligned, texel-sized grid. This removes pixel creep, but movement is no longer smooth. Take the render output and shift it back in screen space by the snap error, regaining smooth movement." — https://www.davidhol.land/articles/3d-pixel-art-rendering/

The exact 2D math (yal.cc, the original reference Holland cites via aarthificial): make the render surface `game_width + 1, game_height + 1`; each frame `camera_set_view_pos(floor(x), floor(y))`; then draw with `draw_surface_part(view_surf, frac(x), frac(y), game_width, game_height, 0, 0)`. One extra pixel of render, fractional remainder as draw offset — https://yal.cc/gamemaker-smooth-pixel-perfect-camera/

**3. Depth + normal edge detection for outlines.** Depth buffer gives object silhouettes; normal buffer gives interior creases and edge highlights.

## Virtual resolution — verified numbers

| Value | Source |
|---|---|
| **320×180** is the canonical base: exact 6× to 1080p, 8× to 1440p, 12× to 2160p — covers ~80% of Steam displays with no fractional scaling | https://notkey.studio/en/tutorials/choosing-the-right-render-resolution-for-a-pixel-art-game/ |
| **640×360** — David Holland's actual working resolution (quoted while discussing god-ray cost: "Even at 640x360, this was just way too expensive") | https://www.davidhol.land/articles/3d-pixel-art-rendering/ |
| **320×180 → 1920×1080 at 6×**, Godot stretch mode `viewport`, scale mode `integer`, default texture filter `Nearest` | https://notkey.studio/en/tutorials/godot-low-res-pixel-perfect-rendering-and-smooth-camera/ |
| 384×216 and 480×270 are the "wider field of view" alternatives; trade sprite prominence for context | same notkey article |

t3ssel8r's own resolution: **unverified** — his devlogs are YouTube-only and YouTube is not fetchable from here.

## Outlines — the verified method

**David Holland's implementation** (the most detailed public writeup of the t3ssel8r-style outline): "I wrote a standard edge detection shader, using the screen depth and normals textures. To make the outlines a single pixel thick, I used a kernel consisting of just the up, down, left, and right texels. The depth texture is mainly used to generate the object outlines, while the normals texture helps with highlighting edges. **Convex edges are isolated by taking the cross product of neighbouring texels**, and grading it against a number of arbitrary heuristics." He is explicit about the limits: "I'm not sure it's quite possible to create a perfect outline shader that works for all models and all camera angles at this low resolution. Low poly models with clearly defined outer edges suit best." — https://www.davidhol.land/articles/3d-pixel-art-rendering/

**Roystan's outline shader** is the canonical parameterised version, with real default values (this is what most implementations derive from) — https://roystan.net/articles/outline-shader/
- Roberts cross on depth: `edgeDepth = sqrt(pow(d1-d0,2) + pow(d3-d2,2)) * 100`
- Grazing-angle correction, which is the part everyone gets wrong: `NdotV = 1 - dot(viewNormal, -viewSpaceDir)`; `normalThreshold01 = saturate((NdotV - _DepthNormalThreshold) / (1 - _DepthNormalThreshold))`; `depthThreshold = _DepthThreshold * depth0 * (normalThreshold01 * _DepthNormalThresholdScale + 1)`
- Normal edge: `edgeNormal = sqrt(dot(nfd0,nfd0) + dot(nfd1,nfd1))`, then `edgeNormal &gt; _NormalThreshold ? 1 : 0`
- Combine: `edge = max(edgeDepth, edgeNormal)`
- Defaults: `_Scale 1`, `_DepthThreshold 1.5`, `_NormalThreshold 0.4`, `_DepthNormalThreshold 0.5`, `_DepthNormalThresholdScale 7`

## How the pixel grid is kept stable — the hard constraint

**Orthographic is effectively mandatory.** ProPixelizer's docs state it outright: pixel creep "can only ever be solved for orthographic projections" because object size stays constant as it moves across the screen; under perspective, objects change size with screen position and no single snap corrects all depths — https://sites.google.com/view/propixelizer/user-guide

Snapping has two levels:
- **Camera snap** (`CameraSnapSRP`) — snaps object positions before rendering, restores after, respects transform hierarchies.
- **Per-object snap** (`ObjectRenderSnapable`) — plus `Align Pixel Grid`, which aligns a child's pixel grid to a root transform. This is what stops a character's sword having a different pixel grid than the body. Also `ShouldSnapAngles` with a configurable `Angle Resolution`.
- https://sites.google.com/view/propixelizer/user-guide and https://propixelizer.github.io/docs/usage/pixelization/

**Rotation** is handled by snapping it too. three.js's own example uses `cameraRotationSnap: 9` degrees and `rotationSnap: 15` degrees for objects. The Godot t3ssel8r-style kit snaps camera rotation in "snappy 45-degree steps" — https://brunich.itch.io/3d-pixel-art-shadergame-kit-t3ssel8r-style-renderer

**Anti-aliasing: off, always.** This is a design invariant, not an optimisation. Adam Robinson-Yu on A Short Hike: the look required "flat cohesive shading and no anti-aliasing" — https://blog.playstation.com/2021/08/05/crafting-a-tiny-open-world-a-look-behind-the-scenes-at-the-creation-of-a-short-hike/. Dead Cells' offline renderer likewise "renders the 3D mesh at small sizes without antialiasing."

**Escape hatch for when snapping is too restrictive:** ProPixelizer's "pixel expansion" — draw objects as a dithered dot pattern at full resolution, then expand the dots into larger pixels in post. Costs one extra pass but allows per-object pixel sizes and smoother motion than grid-snapping permits — https://propixelizer.github.io/docs/usage/pixelization/. Also supports 4×4 dither patterns (thresholds 0–16) and palette LUTs from real devices (NES, PAL, GameBoy).

## Verified shipped titles (and corrections to your list)

| Title | What it actually is | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **A Short Hike** (Adam Robinson-Yu) | **The best verified shipped example.** Real-time 3D rendered to a low-res RenderTexture, no smoothing, flat shading, no AA, plus "a soft outline effect to objects to help them stand out, and stay readable with so few pixels." User-adjustable pixel size. Stated goal: "make a beautiful (and readable) 3D world using as few pixels as possible." | https://blog.playstation.com/2021/08/05/crafting-a-tiny-open-world-a-look-behind-the-scenes-at-the-creation-of-a-short-hike/ |
| **Dead Cells** (Motion Twin) | Offline 3D→sprite, not real-time. 3DS Max models → homebrew renderer at small size, AA explicitly off, cel shading, **normal maps exported per animation frame** so the 2D sprites get real-time toon lighting in-engine. Characters ~50px tall, 30 FPS animation, pose-to-pose then interpolation frames added after. | https://www.gamedeveloper.com/production/art-design-deep-dive-using-a-3d-pipeline-for-2d-animation-in-i-dead-cells-i- |
| **Songs of Conquest** (Lavapotion) | 2.5D, not 3D-to-pixel. Aseprite sprites on billboards in 3D terrain. 128×128px environment textures, ~16 per faction, 8 animation frames each, atlas-scroll animation in shader, real-time top-down lightmaps from a separate camera. Billboard rotation approximated by baking origin position into the generated meshes. | https://80.lv/articles/mixing-2d-billboards-and-3d-environments-in-a-game |
| **Octopath Traveler / HD-2D** (Square Enix + Acquire, UE4) | 2D sprites in fully-3D environments, tilt-shift camera with wide FOV, heavy DOF/bloom. A different family from pixel-snapped 3D. | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HD-2D , https://www.unrealengine.com/en-US/developer-interviews/octopath-traveler-ii-builds-a-bigger-bolder-world-in-its-stunning-hd-2d-style |
| **Anodyne 2** (Analgesic) | PS1/N64-era low-poly 3D, Unity. Aesthetic, not pixel-grid-snapped. | https://www.gamedeveloper.com/design/road-to-the-igf-analgesic-productions-i-anodyne-2-return-to-dust-i- |

**Corrections to the titles you suggested:**
- **Mina the Hollower** (Yacht Club) is **2D pixel-art sprites**, Game Boy Color–inspired — not pseudo-3D, not a pixel-3D renderer. Do not use it. https://store.steampowered.com/app/1875580/Mina_the_Hollower/
- **"Bad Cat" / "Sanctuary Moon"** — could not verify either as a pixel-3D title. Drop them.
- **Dungeon Clawler** — 2D. **Hypnospace Outlaw** — not 3D pixel rendering. Drop both.
- **t3ssel8r's game is unnamed** in every reachable source; he has never publicly titled it.

## Key primary sources for A
- David Holland's article (full text recovered; the site 403s WebFetch but serves to a browser UA): https://www.davidhol.land/articles/3d-pixel-art-rendering/
- His project repo: https://git.sr.ht/~denovodavid/3d-pixel-art-in-godot
- t3ssel8r channel: https://www.youtube.com/@t3ssel8r — key videos https://youtu.be/ERA7-I5nPAU ("Creating a Scene for my 3D Pixel Art Game"), and his Imgur writeup https://imgur.com/gallery/creating-pixel-art-scene-realtime-3d-qwhbHQq (not fetchable from here)
- t3ssel8r on volumetrics, quoted verbatim in Holland's article: "what I'm sampling is the light space depth map… based on world-space coordinates of a series of parallel planes aligned with the light direction to test whether each position is shadowed."
- Unity URP implementation with the same formulas three.js later shipped: https://kyriota.com/2022/08/02/UnityPixelatedArtStyleInURP/

# B) CEL-SHADED / TOON-BANDED 3D WITH OUTLINES

## Guilty Gear Xrd — the best source, and I have the full slide deck

The official handout PDF is public: **https://www.ggxrd.com/Motomura_Junya_GuiltyGearXrd.pdf** (4.9 MB, full speaker notes). Free video: **https://archive.org/details/GDC2015Motomura** (MPEG4 512 MB / OGG 244 MB). GDC Vault: https://www.gdcvault.com/play/1022031/GuiltyGearXrd-s-Art-Style-The. ArcSys announcement: https://www.arcsystemworks.com/guilty-gear-xrds-art-style-the-x-factor-between-2d-and-3d-talk-from-gdc-2015-is-now-available-online/

Verified numbers and methods, quoted from the deck:

- **Band count: 2. It is a hard `step`, not a ramp texture.** "In Cel-shading, the surface is either lit, or not, and no value in between." "In the main part of the calculation, a generic 'step' function is used to decide if that pixel is lit or not… What matters here are the following: The Threshold, the Light Vector, and the Normal Vector. And that's all." **Correct your brief here — GGXrd uses no shading ramp.**
- **Threshold control via vertex colour.** "We used a channel from the Vertex Colors as an offset on the Threshold. This made it possible for the artist to make certain areas on the mesh get darker more easily… The artist may even set the value to Zero, making the area always shaded no matter what." This is their answer to shading breakups.
- **Normal editing.** "To get rid of unintentional shading, we modified the normals on every major feature of the model. The faces of the Characters especially needed to be hand crafted to get a clean anime look." Done in Softimage with a live in-DCC shader preview matching the game.
- **Colour via two flat lookup textures, not a ramp.** "The base texture defines the color of the surface when it's lit. While the Tint texture defines how dark it gets when shaded. Multiplying the two textures, we get the shaded color." Both textures are "just square areas consisting of single colors… we simply use them as color lookups, and do not draw-in any details."
- **Outlines: inverted hull, in the vertex shader, width driven by vertex colour.** "we use a traditional 'Inverted Hull' method… A second set of darker polygons are generated in the shader and are expanded in the normals direction… we were able give the outline variable widths in places we saw fit, even erasing it completely at times." Why not post-process: "we could preview the result right in the modeling viewport."
- **Interior lines: axis-aligned beams + distorted UVs.** "All the lines are drawn as Axis Aligned beams on the texture, and the UV is lined up alongside them. How much the UV overlaps these beams, defines how thick that line becomes. As long as the pixels are Aligned to an Axis they don't get any jaggie." Downside accepted: heavily distorted UVs, which is fine because no detail lives in the textures.
- **Poly/bone counts:** "around **40000 triangles** in average" per character; "The bone count is around **500** per character in average."
- **No normal maps at all** on characters — vertex normals, vertex colours and UVs carry the data, because vertex attributes interpolate linearly and are resolution-independent at extreme close-ups.
- **No global lighting on characters.** "Each character has his or her own dedicated light vector that lights their idle pose in the best way."
- **Animation:** interpolation between keys is off entirely. "Every frame now is a key frame, and the animator poses the character in the best way possible." Plus deliberate per-keyframe mesh deformation "to add Imperfection," and heavy scale animation (which they had to implement themselves in UE3). Note: the deck does **not** state an "animate on 2s/3s" number — that claim is not in the primary source.
- Engine: Unreal Engine 3 + Autodesk Softimage. 1080p PS4 / 720p PS3, up from 480p sprites.

## Hi-Fi Rush — full GDC 2024 slide deck also public

**https://media.gdcvault.com/gdc2024/Slides/GDC+slide+presentations/Tanaka_Kosuke_3D_Toon_Rendering.pdf** (13.8 MB, full notes). Talk page: https://gdcvault.com/play/1034330/3D-Toon-Rendering-in-Hi. Video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gdBACyIOCtc. Summary: https://80.lv/articles/the-making-of-hi-fi-rush-s-3d-toon-rendering-style. Third-party notes: https://www.foth.top/article/gdc-2024-hifirush-toonrendering-notes/

- **Band count: 2, verbatim.** "Hi-Fi RUSH's toon shading uses a simple 2-tone lit/shade toon shading model." Key light is **forward-rendered in UE4's base pass** (not deferred) specifically to avoid engine modifications.
- **Outlines are POST-PROCESS, and split into two independent passes** — a normal outline and a depth outline. Confirmed by their G-buffer stencil bits: `HBK_AO_STENCIL_SKIP_NORMAL_OUTLINE 16`, `HBK_AO_STENCIL_SKIP_DEPTH_OUTLINE 32`. No inverted hull anywhere in the deck.
- **Shading-breakup control is a per-material stencil bitmask packed into unused PBR G-buffer channels.** They kept the stock UE4 layout (RGBA8 × 3) and repurposed `GBufferAO` and `Specular` as bitmasks: `SKIP_SHADOW_MAIN 1`, `SKIP_SHADOW_2/3/4 2/4/8`, `ENV_SKIP_AMBIENT_CUBEMAP 16`, `ENV_SKIP_STATIC_SHADOWMAP 32`, `ENV_SKIP_SSAO 64`, plus `CHARACTER_MASK 4`. "GBuffer stencil requires no additional render passes and is practically free." Artists set these per material instance, and some per mesh actor. **This is the single most portable idea in the deck** — one mask channel that lets art switch off outlines/AO/shadows per surface.
- **Character self-shadow** is plain `if (NdotL &gt; threshold)` on the **vertex normal** — everywhere except the face.
- **Face shadows use a "threshold map," not NdotL.** Authoring is fully documented: bake vertex-normal face shadows with a directional light swept 180° in **5° steps = 36 textures**; artists retouch each by hand; an in-house tool merges them into one texture with "distance field-like interpolation." At runtime: `lightDirHorizontal = Normalize(lightDir - Dot(lightDir, headBoneUp))`; `threshold = ArcCos(Dot(lightDirHorizontal, headBoneForward)) / PI`; then `height = TexSample(thresholdMap, uv); if (height &gt; threshold) baseColor else shadowColor`. Texture flips horizontally when the light crosses to the other side. Credited to miHoYo's Jack He, Unity Seoul 2018.
- **Comic shader:** halftone dots on lit areas, hatching lines on shadowed areas, both generated procedurally from SDFs — hatching is "the same shader code, just with UV.y fixed to 0." Anti-aliased with `smoothstep(Radius-Width, Radius+Width, Dist)` instead of `step`. Projected with **triplanar world-space UVs — "Only 3 planes, XY, YZ, XZ"** — using the G-buffer world normal, so patterns look printed on surfaces rather than stuck to the screen. Halftone size scales with scene luminance.
- UE4, **60 FPS at native resolution**, both characters and environment toon-shaded. Artists place "toon process box volumes" throughout levels to tune shading locally.

## Wind Waker

Accessible sources are thinner than the two above. Verified at the level of technique, not parameters: vertex lighting is computed normally, then the brightness value is **swapped for a value from a lookup texture holding the desired shading curve** (a ramp LUT — GameCube had no programmable shaders, so this is a TEV texture indirection), and shadows/highlights are then tinted rather than simply darkened. **One dominant light per object** — when several lights are present the game elects the strongest and uses only that, producing the harsh binary falloff. — https://boingboing.net/2020/01/25/this-video-explains-how-cel-sh.html , https://www.resetera.com/threads/video-how-the-wind-waker-defined-cel-shading.166993/

**Band count and outline method: unverified** from any primary or reverse-engineering source I could reach. Useful secondary analysis: https://medium.com/@gordonnl/wind-waker-graphics-analysis-a0b575a31127 (Medium blocks automated fetch). A working modern reimplementation with a documented feature list — two-tone highlight/shadow, soft band transition, rim highlight, time-of-day and weather palettes, multi-light (an extension beyond the original's single light) — is https://github.com/janhohenheim/bevy_wind_waker_shader

## Sable

Flat shading with thin black outlines, no shading apart from occasional dotting. The team's stated problem was depth readability under flat shading, solved by **layering three things**: shadows ("helps players figure out where they sit on a surface"), **per-biome fog** (called "really, really key" for mid-to-long distance, also drives the day/night cycle), and **outlines that fade with distance** — used both to convey perspective and to disguise pop-in. World is 10 km × 10 km. Outline implementation detects **depth or material discontinuities** (post-process, not inverted hull). — https://www.gamedeveloper.com/marketing/how-shedworks-refined-the-art-of-sable-in-pursuit-of-readability , https://www.cookandbecker.com/en/article/170/sable-exploration-through-line-art.html

**Exact line width, palette values and band count: unverified.**

## Borderlands

**Gearbox explicitly rejects the "cel-shaded" label.** The technique is "hand-painting with integrated inks": hand-drawn textures scanned and coloured in Photoshop, *plus* software that draws graphic-novel outlines around characters and objects, sharpens shadows, and draws lines on hills and inclines. The ink is authored into the albedo; the outline is a post-process on top. Randy Pitchford, 2009: "Borderlands is not cel-shaded." — https://www.destructoid.com/borderlands-explains-its-not-cel-shaded-actually-art-style/ , https://www.gamespot.com/articles/behind-borderlands-11th-hour-style-change/1100-6253257/

GDC 2010 talk: "Borderlands &amp; The 11th Hour Art Style Change (Don't Try This at Home)," Randy Pitchford and Brian Martel.

**Band count: not applicable** — the value structure lives in the painted texture, not in a lighting ramp.

## Dragon Quest XI

**No public technical breakdown exists.** The only first-party source is Epic's non-technical spotlight interview, which confirms UE4 and **Enlighten** for dynamic GI, and describes the central problem as blending Toriyama-style cel-shaded characters against photoreal environments without either clashing. Band count, outline method and shader details are all **unverified**. — https://www.unrealengine.com/spotlights/how-dragon-quest-xi-came-to-life-with-unreal-engine-4

## Jet Set Radio

Art-direction sources only; no technical documentation survives in accessible form. Verified: internally called "manga dimension"; Dreamcast implementation was "extremely challenging"; **characters shipped without outlines and sharp shading until the middle of development**, before the team had the tools. Director Masayoshi Kikuchi chose cel shading to express art director Ryuta Ueda's flat illustrated look. **Outline technique: unverified.** — https://segabits.com/blog/2022/01/25/exclusive-classic-sega-interview-smilebits-takayuki-kawagoe-talks-jet-set-radio-future-with-xbox-nation-magazine/ , https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_Set_Radio

## Band-count summary (verified only)

| Game | Bands | Outline method |
|---|---|---|
| Guilty Gear Xrd | **2** (hard `step`, vertex-colour threshold offset) | **Inverted hull**, vertex-shader extrude, vertex-colour width, can be zeroed |
| Hi-Fi Rush | **2** ("2-tone lit/shade") | **Post-process**, split normal-edge and depth-edge passes, per-material skip bits |
| Wind Waker | unverified (ramp LUT, one dominant light) | unverified |
| Sable | flat (no shading bands) | post-process depth/material discontinuity, fades with distance |
| Borderlands | n/a (painted inks) | post-process outline + inked albedo |
| DQXI, Jet Set Radio | unverified | unverified |

General reference on ramp construction (1D ramps are typically 256×1; use `texelFetch` rather than a filtered sample for strict cel; blur the ramp for soft cel): https://panthavma.com/articles/shading/toonshading/. Canonical parameterised toon shader with real defaults — `smoothstep(0, 0.01, NdotL)` for the band, `_Glossiness 32`, specular `smoothstep(0.005, 0.01, …)`, `_RimThreshold 0.1`, `_AmbientColor 0.4` — https://roystan.net/articles/toon-shader/
