# C) FLAT-SHADED VOXEL &amp; LOW-POLY TOY LOOK

## Why it reads as "toy," and the direct answer on outlines

Flat shading evaluates lighting once per polygon from the face normal, so every face is a uniform colour chip with a hard value step at each 90° corner — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shading. **That value step does the job an outline would do.** Of everything verified, **only Townscaper shows evidence of outlines**; Crossy Road, Minecraft, Teardown and Hytale have none documented anywhere.

The Townscaper outline is community-observed, not dev-confirmed: "It looks like the outlines are created from slightly enlarged geometry that's only visible from the backside" — classic inverted hull. https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=29074015

## Crossy Road (Hipster Whale) — PRIORITY, with one correction

**The tool is Qubicle, not MagicaVoxel.** No source ties Crossy Road to MagicaVoxel; that appears only in fan tutorials.

Verified:
- Unity engine — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crossy_Road
- Art by **Ben Weatherall in Qubicle**, learned within a year — https://www.pocketgamer.biz/making-of-crossy-road/ ; Qubicle's store page claims the title: https://store.steampowered.com/app/454550/Qubicle_Voxel_Editor/
- **The angled camera IS the art direction.** Matt Hall "wanted to rotate the camera because voxels look better at an angle." Andy Sum objected, then conceded: "there was no denying that it looked 100 times better."
- The readability rule is stated as a *gameplay* rule, by Sum: "I wanted to have really obvious hit boxes and sharp edges so that you could play the game in a precise way and easily judge whether or not you could make the right hop."
- 12 weeks total development — https://gdcvault.com/play/1021897/Crossy-Road-A-Whale-of
- Style taxonomy places it in **"vector voxel art": "uses only 90 degree bends when representing curves… a very clean style that is easy to read at varying scales,"** partly chosen for smartphone hardware — https://www.gamedeveloper.com/art/voxelart-styles-in-video-games

**Unverified — do not invent:** voxel resolution per character, colour count per character, orthographic vs perspective, FOV, tilt angle, light count/direction, shadow style, presence of outlines (nothing mentions them), and any explicit "no AA" rule. The GDC talk abstract covers design and monetisation, not art. The PocketGamer interview is the single best primary art source and it is short.

Community reverse-engineering (not authoritative): "Extremely lowpoly," "flat low polygon models with no curves," "Few if any textures," unlit/legacy-diffuse shader rendering "solid color and brightness regardless of lighting" — https://discussions.unity.com/threads/crossy-road-art-style-how.323323/

## Minecraft — the AO algorithm you can actually copy

Official: "Smooth lighting… blends light levels across block faces and darkens corners using ambient occlusion." "For each corner of a side, the game checks diagonally for full blocks… no full blocks meaning full brightness and both sides diagonally being full blocks meaning the darkest level possible." — https://minecraft.wiki/w/Light

Exact values: sample "the three adjacent blocks plus the block which touches the face." All four transparent → intensity **1.0**; all four opaque → **0.2**; the vertex value is the average of the four, then interpolated across the face by the rasteriser. — https://greyminecraftcoder.blogspot.com/2020/04/lighting-1144.html

The canonical implementation, from Mikola Lysenko who wrote the original Minecraft AO mod — https://0fps.net/2013/07/03/ambient-occlusion-for-minecraft-like-worlds/

```js
function vertexAO(side1, side2, corner) {
  if (side1 &amp;&amp; side2) return 0
  return 3 - (side1 + side2 + corner)
}
// mandatory anisotropy fix, or quads shade differently by triangulation:
if (a00 + a11 &gt; a01 + a10) { /* flipped quad */ } else { /* normal quad */ }
```

Greedy meshing numbers — https://0fps.net/2012/06/30/meshing-in-a-minecraft-game/ : 8×8×8 solid cube 3072 quads naive → 384 culled → **6 greedy**; sphere 4770 → 2100; noisy terrain 2198 → 1670. "The greedy mesh has no more than 8× as many quads than the optimal mesh." **Important tension: merging is blocked by differing colour, light value, normal AND per-vertex AO** — greedy meshing and vertex AO work against each other.

Per-face directional shading is real and configurable per element (`shade`, and `shade_direction_override` in JE 26.3) — https://minecraft.wiki/w/Model — but the commonly cited multipliers (top 1.0 / bottom 0.5 / N-S 0.8 / E-W 0.6) are **unverified from a primary source**.

## Teardown — the counter-example (raytraced, not flat)

Primary sources, all with real numbers:
- **Voxel size: 0.1 m (10 cm)** at default scale — `GetShapeSize` returns "the size of one voxel in meters (with default scale it is 0.1)" — https://teardowngame.com/modding/api.html
- Authoring in **MagicaVoxel**; max object 256³ voxels, "Objects of half that size in all axis (128 × 128 × 128) are generally OK" — https://www.teardowngame.com/modding/
- **Palette index *is* the material:** 255 indexed entries; "the color at index 9 is always grass, no matter what actual color it is." Each entry carries roughness, emissiveness, reflectivity and physical type. One byte per voxel. Spraycan shading eats 8 slots per material, "cutting the usable number of entries in the palette down from 255 to a mere 28." — https://blog.voxagon.se/2020/12/03/spraycan.html
- **Shadow volume at 5 cm resolution**, eight neighbouring voxels packed per byte in octree fashion, mips at 10/20/40 cm, final shadow texture "256 + 32 + 4 = 292 Mb." **AO = 5 rays per pixel.** Soft shadows by jittering light position (30 cm radius example), which also hides voxel-grid artefacts. **~9 ms at 1080p on a GTX 1080** for all AO + lighting + fog + reflections including denoise. — https://blog.voxagon.se/2018/10/17/from-screen-space-to-voxel-space.html
- Stratified hemisphere sampling; adaptive raymarch of **4–16 steps** by sample probability, halving diffuse cost; 4×4 denoise kernel; 16 area lights in the worked example — https://blog.voxagon.se/2018/04/24/stratified-sampling.html
- Frame teardown by Steven Wittens: **one draw call per voxel object, 36 vertices / 12 triangles = a box**, shader raymarches the 3D texture inside it; 2 extra mips to skip empty 2³/4³ blocks (dimensions must be multiples of 4); a 1-bit volumetric shadow map over the whole play area (Marina: 1752×100×1500 texture, 262 MB, each texel packing 2×2×2 voxels); AO cosine-weighted, **2 samples/pixel**; denoise blended with reprojected previous frame "at least 4 times per frame"; **TXAA** with per-frame camera jitter; emissive voxels do not self-illuminate, lights are placed by hand — https://acko.net/blog/teardown-frame-teardown/

## Townscaper (Oskar Stålberg)

- Grid: fixed hexagonal grid → quads → quads again → points moved. Wave Function Collapse picks modules per cell, then decorates. Marching-cubes-style **corner segment tiles**, not whole-cell tiles. Windows and doors cut with the **stencil buffer**. — https://www.gamedeveloper.com/game-platforms/how-townscaper-works-a-story-four-games-in-the-making
- **Palette: 15 building colours + ground** (index 0 red … 14 white, 15 ground) — https://townscaper.fandom.com/wiki/List_of_colors , https://github.com/alvaro-cuesta/townsclipper
- Brick texture trick, quoted: "Take a really low rez texture. Shrink every other pixel to the size of a single pixel on your screen. Now you got yourself a nice random brick texture. Townscaper does that dynamically in a pixel shader." WebGL reproduction: https://reindernijhoff.net/2021/11/townscapers-rendering-style-in-webgl/
- Later lighting panel with sun position; night auto-adds warm interior lights — https://80.lv/articles/townscaper-adds-lighting-options-flying-towns
- **AO technique: unverified.** A description circulates of a layered mesh reprojected to a texture on each edit, blurred, sampled in the vertex shader — no primary source found. Talk: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Uxeo9c-PX-w

## Hytale — the most explicit official art-direction document found

https://hytale.com/news/2025/12/an-introduction-to-making-models-for-hytale — all verbatim:
- **"We only use 2 primitives: Cubes (6 sides) Quads (2 sides)."** No spheres, edge loops, triangles or pyramids. Rationale: models stay easy to make, unwrap and animate without weight painting or rigging.
- **Texel density: characters/attachments 64 px per unit, props/blocks 32 px per unit.** Textures non-square, multiples of 32 px.
- Stretch limits: "avoid going under 0.7X and over 1.3X the stretch for a node in one axis."
- **Colour rule: "Avoid pure white and pure black, as this will break lighting ingame."** Shadows carry hue — "a hint of purple in the shadows" — not desaturation.
- **Lighting: "We paint lights and shadows inside textures and use real lights/shadows to bring everything together."** No PBR; "in-house light propagation techniques."
- Silhouette discipline: "We work as simply as possible, then slightly increase geometry and polygon count when needed to improve the silhouette," so "players can read the world they navigate, regardless of how cluttered the space is."
- Exact colour counts per texture, outline use, AO specifics: **not stated.**

## Remaining titles

| Game | Verified | Not verified |
|---|---|---|
| **Cube World** (Wollay) | Exists; voxel ARPG, procedural world "made up entirely of cubes" — https://store.steampowered.com/app/1128000/Cube_World/ | **Everything technical.** Wollay's blog is gone (picroma.com/blog 403, wollay.blogspot.com 404). No numbers exist in any reachable source. |
| **Riverbond** (Cococucumber) | Exists; destructible voxel world — https://blog.playstation.com/2019/05/09/introducing-riverbond-a-voxel-shoot-and-slash-featuring-indie-crossovers/ | No technical numbers. |
| **Trove** | Devs use **Qubicle**; community assets are `.qb` — https://trove.fandom.com/wiki/Common_Voxel_Editors | Per-asset voxel limits exist in their creation docs but the host 403s. No verified number. |
| **Staxel** | Exists — https://playstaxel.com/ | **No technical art documentation at all.** Drop it. |
| **Moonglow Bay** | Store copy says "Vivid Voxel Art" — https://store.steampowered.com/app/1361400/Moonglow_Bay/ ; "presented like a diorama with its isometric camera view" is a reviewer's phrasing, not the dev's | Tools, palette, lighting, AO all unverified. |

## The canonical flat-voxel lighting model (copy this)

Sean Barrett's `stb_voxel_render.h` — https://github.com/nothings/stb/blob/master/stb_voxel_render.h

Vertex format: "per-vertex 'lighting' or 'ambient occlusion' value (**6 bits**)" and "per-face **5-bit normal**." Lighting: "half-lambert directional plus constant, all scaled by vertex ao."

```glsl
vec3 ambient_color = dot(normal, ambient[0].xyz) * ambient[1].xyz + ambient[2].xyz;
ambient_color = clamp(ambient_color, 0.0, 1.0);
ambient_color *= amb_occ;
lit_color = albedo * ambient_color;
```
`ambient[0].xyz` = negated directional light dir (half-lambert, so nothing goes fully black); `ambient[1].rgb` = light colour; `ambient[2].rgb` = constant term — "light ranges from ambient[2]-ambient[1] to ambient[2]+ambient[1]." **One directional + one constant + multiplied per-vertex AO on a flat albedo. No shadow map required.**

## Style split worth flagging

https://www.gamedeveloper.com/art/voxelart-styles-in-video-games distinguishes two buckets you should not blend:
- **Pixelated Voxels (Flat Shaded):** "With any form of hard shadows applied the piece and style will be ruined." Minimal shadows means "the style benefits from vibrant colors." (Fugl, Voxelnauts.)
- **Vector Voxel Art:** angled camera, real lighting, 90° bends. (Crossy Road.)

So: "Crossy Road look" = flat normals + directional light + hard shadow. "Pure flat toy look" = flat normals + AO only + **no** directional shadow.

## Export pipeline reality

- **MagicaVoxel OBJ export** ships `.obj` + `.mtl` + a **256×1 PNG palette**, "Each pixel maps to a color in the palette." It merges same-colour adjacent voxels (2×2×2 cube → 12 triangles, the minimum) but **cannot merge across a colour change on the same face** — that is the real triangle driver. A moderately complex character lands ~**1,600 triangles**. — https://luciopaiva.com/magicavoxel-threejs-howto/
- **Qubicle** base edition exports unoptimised OBJ/FBX/Collada; the **Mesh Module DLC** unlocks low-poly mesh optimisation — https://store.steampowered.com/app/454550/Qubicle_Voxel_Editor/
- **Vertex colours beat a texture atlas** for this look: no UVs, no atlas bleed, no mip artefacts at small size, and you can merge many objects into one draw call. Cost: greedy meshing stops at every colour boundary.

# D) three.js / WebGL SPECIFICS

**Current release: r186** (`"version": "0.186.0"` — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/package.json). **Docs URLs changed**: the `threejs.org/docs/#api/en/...` hash form is obsolete; canonical is now `https://threejs.org/docs/pages/&lt;ClassName&gt;.html`.

## 1) MeshToonMaterial + gradientMap

Docs https://threejs.org/docs/pages/MeshToonMaterial.html · Source https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/src/materials/MeshToonMaterial.js

The actual GLSL — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/src/renderers/shaders/ShaderChunk/gradientmap_pars_fragment.glsl.js

```glsl
vec3 getGradientIrradiance( vec3 normal, vec3 lightDirection ) {
	// dotNL will be from -1.0 to 1.0
	float dotNL = dot( normal, lightDirection );
	vec2 coord = vec2( dotNL * 0.5 + 0.5, 0.0 );
	#ifdef USE_GRADIENTMAP
		return vec3( texture2D( gradientMap, coord ).r );
	#else
		vec2 fw = fwidth( coord ) * 0.5;
		return mix( vec3( 0.7 ), vec3( 1.0 ), smoothstep( 0.7 - fw.x, 0.7 + fw.x, coord.x ) );
	#endif
}
```

Four load-bearing facts:
1. **`dotNL` is NOT clamped** — it is remapped `[-1,1] → [0,1]`. The **midpoint texel (u=0.5) is the terminator**, and the entire left half of the ramp serves surfaces facing away from the light. This is why a 3-band ramp often looks like 2 bands.
2. **Only the red channel is read**, splatted to `vec3`. It is a monochrome irradiance multiplier, **not a colour LUT** — you cannot get purple shadows this way; tint via `color`/`map`.
3. `coord.y = 0.0` — it is 1×N along u only. Height must be 1.
4. Without a gradientMap the fallback is a hardcoded 2-tone `0.7 → 1.0` step with `fwidth` AA.

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/src/renderers/shaders/ShaderChunk/lights_toon_pars_fragment.glsl.js shows `RE_Direct_Toon` multiplies the ramp by `directLight.color` (so a high-intensity light blows past 1.0 and flattens your bands), and `RE_IndirectDiffuse_Toon` is **plain Lambert — ambient light is not banded**.

**Correction to your assumption: gradientMap must be `NoColorSpace`, NOT `SRGBColorSpace`.** Docs verbatim: "gradientMap represents non-color data. Any texture assigned must have `texture.colorSpace = NoColorSpace` (default)." Since it is the default, the correct action is to **not touch it**. Setting sRGB applies a decode that non-linearly crushes band positions.

Also: **`DataTexture` already defaults both filters to `NearestFilter`** (unlike base `Texture`). Build one like this:

```js
function makeGradientMap( bands ) {
	const data = new Uint8Array( bands );
	for ( let i = 0; i &lt; bands; i ++ ) data[ i ] = Math.round( i / ( bands - 1 ) * 255 );
	const tex = new THREE.DataTexture( data, bands, 1, THREE.RedFormat );
	tex.minFilter = THREE.NearestFilter;
	tex.magFilter = THREE.NearestFilter;
	tex.generateMipmaps = false;
	tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.ClampToEdgeWrapping;
	// colorSpace left at NoColorSpace (default) — non-color data
	tex.needsUpdate = true;
	return tex;
}
```

**Band behaviour, accounting for the remap:**
- **2 texels** — pure lit/unlit split at `dotNL == 0`. Hardest look. This is what GGXrd and Hi-Fi Rush do.
- **3 texels** (`0,128,255`) — texel 0 covers `dotNL ∈ [-1,-0.33]`, texel 1 `[-0.33,0.33]`, texel 2 `[0.33,1]`. Classic cel.
- **5 texels** — approaches a smooth ramp; reads as stylised soft shading rather than cel.
- Because half the texels serve the dark hemisphere, hand-write a non-linear ramp for a strongly-lit look: `new Uint8Array([0,0,0,90,200,255,255,255])` gives a deep flat shadow, tight terminator, flat highlight.

**Shipped gradient maps confirmed present:**
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/examples/textures/gradientMaps/threeTone.jpg
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mrdoob/three.js/dev/examples/textures/gradientMaps/fiveTone.jpg
- Directory: https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/tree/dev/examples/textures/gradientMaps
- They are **JPEGs**, so they carry compression ringing at band edges. Build a `DataTexture` for clean bands.

**Example page: `webgl_materials_variations_toon` no longer exists — it was renamed.** Use **https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_materials_toon** (subtitle "Toon Material with OutlineEffect"), source https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_materials_toon.html. Note the gradient-map loop in that example has two off-by-one bugs (`c &lt;= colors.length` and `/ colors.length * 256`) — use the corrected version above.

## 2) Inverted-hull outline

**`OutlineEffect.js` IS an inverted hull — confirmed.** Path `examples/jsm/effects/OutlineEffect.js` · https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/jsm/effects/OutlineEffect.js · Docs https://threejs.org/docs/pages/OutlineEffect.html · **WebGLRenderer only — will not work with WebGPURenderer.**

`createMaterial()` returns `side: BackSide`, and the vertex shader flips and extrudes:

```glsl
vec4 calculateOutline( vec4 pos, vec3 normal, vec4 skinned ) {
	float thickness = outlineThickness;
	const float ratio = 1.0;
	vec4 pos2 = projectionMatrix * modelViewMatrix * vec4( skinned.xyz + normal, 1.0 );
	vec4 norm = normalize( pos - pos2 );
	return pos + norm * thickness * pos.w * ratio;
}
```

**Thickness space — the important answer: neither view nor world space. It is clip space multiplied by `pos.w`, which cancels the perspective divide, so outline width in NDC is constant regardless of distance.** That is why the default is a tiny `0.003` — it is a fraction of screen height, not a world distance.

Parameters: `defaultThickness 0.003`, `defaultColor [0,0,0]`, `defaultAlpha 1.0`, `defaultKeepAlive false`. Per-material override goes through `userData`, not the constructor:

```js
mesh.material.userData.outlineParameters = { thickness: 0.01, color: [0,0,0], alpha: 1.0, visible: true, keepAlive: true };
```

Two gotchas: `outlineAlpha` is seeded from `originalMaterial.opacity` before overrides, so transparent materials get transparent outlines; and `defaultKeepAlive: false` purges the outline-material cache every frame for off-screen objects, which thrashes shader compilation in scenes with churn.

**Roll-your-own, shortest version** (avoids the per-frame cache entirely, shares geometry so costs no extra VRAM):

```js
function addOutline( mesh, thickness = 0.02, color = 0x000000 ) {
	const mat = new THREE.MeshBasicMaterial( { color, side: THREE.BackSide } );
	mat.onBeforeCompile = ( shader ) =&gt; {
		shader.uniforms.uThickness = { value: thickness };
		shader.vertexShader = 'uniform float uThickness;\n' + shader.vertexShader
			.replace( '#include &lt;begin_vertex&gt;', 'vec3 transformed = position + objectNormal * uThickness;' );
	};
	const outline = new THREE.Mesh( mesh.geometry, mat );
	mesh.add( outline );
	return outline;
}
```
This is **object-space** thickness (shrinks with distance). For constant screen width instead, after `#include &lt;project_vertex&gt;`:
```glsl
vec3 clipNormal = normalize( ( projectionMatrix * vec4( normalMatrix * objectNormal, 0.0 ) ).xyz );
gl_Position.xy += clipNormal.xy * uThickness * gl_Position.w;
```
`objectNormal` only exists after `&lt;beginnormal_vertex&gt;`, which is why replacing `&lt;begin_vertex&gt;` works and anything earlier does not.

**Pitfall list:**
1. **Hard/split normals tear the hull open** — the number one failure. A cube has 3–4 different normals per corner position; extruding each separately pulls corners apart into disconnected quads. Fix: `mergeVertices(geometry)` from https://threejs.org/docs/pages/BufferGeometryUtils.html then `computeVertexNormals()`, and use that geometry **for the outline only** so the beauty pass keeps its hard edges. For crisp silhouettes, store the smoothed normals in a second attribute (`aSmoothNormal`) instead.
2. **Non-uniform scale breaks object-space extrusion** — a mesh scaled `(1,1,5)` gets a 5× thicker outline on one axis. Normalise scale or extrude in view space via `normalMatrix`.
3. **Keep the outline opaque and depth-writing.** `depthWrite: false` or `transparent: true` makes it draw over the object or z-fight.
4. **Open geometry has no backface** — planes and cloth produce no outline. Inverted hull needs a closed manifold.
5. **Multi-mesh glTF outlines every child**, giving internal lines where parts meet. Merge children into one geometry for the outline at load time (merging at selection time is "terribly slow") — https://discourse.threejs.org/t/unsure-of-correct-approach-to-apply-outline-shader/68515
6. **No interior lines.** Inverted hull is silhouette-only. Creases need a post-process — the two are complements, exactly as GGXrd used hull outlines *plus* a separate interior-line technique.
7. **Skinned meshes** must be extruded after skinning or the outline detaches; `OutlineEffect` orders `&lt;skinning_vertex&gt;` before `calculateOutline`.

## 3) RenderPixelatedPass

Source https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/jsm/postprocessing/RenderPixelatedPass.js · Docs https://threejs.org/docs/pages/RenderPixelatedPass.html · Example https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_postprocessing_pixel · Example source https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_postprocessing_pixel.html

```js
constructor( pixelSize, scene, camera, options = {} )
```

| Param | Default | Range in the official example |
|---|---|---|
| `pixelSize` | required | `.min(1).max(16).step(1)` |
| `options.normalEdgeStrength` | `0.3` | `.min(0).max(2).step(0.05)` |
| `options.depthEdgeStrength` | `0.4` | `.min(0).max(1).step(0.05)` |

**Trap:** defaults use `||`, not `??`, so **passing `0` silently becomes the default**. To disable an edge type, set the property after construction. Use `setPixelSize()` at runtime — assigning `.pixelSize` does nothing until the next resize.

Render targets: both `NearestFilter`, both `HalfFloatType`; the beauty target carries a `DepthTexture`; the normal target has none. Sizing is `renderResolution = (width / pixelSize) | 0` (floored), and the `resolution` uniform is a `Vector4` packing `(w, h, 1/w, 1/h)`. The normal buffer comes from `scene.overrideMaterial = new MeshNormalMaterial()` — **view-space normals, encoded `[-1,1] → [0,1]`** — which means **a full second scene draw, so the pass costs 2× geometry throughput**.

**Edge detection, exactly.** 4-tap von Neumann neighbourhood (N/S/E/W, no diagonals), one low-res texel out.

Depth — sums only *positive* differences, so the edge fires on the **near** side of a discontinuity:
```glsl
float depthEdgeIndicator(float depth, vec3 normal) {
	float diff = 0.0;
	diff += clamp(getDepth(1, 0) - depth, 0.0, 1.0);
	diff += clamp(getDepth(-1, 0) - depth, 0.0, 1.0);
	diff += clamp(getDepth(0, 1) - depth, 0.0, 1.0);
	diff += clamp(getDepth(0, -1) - depth, 0.0, 1.0);
	return floor(smoothstep(0.01, 0.02, diff) * 2.) / 2.;
}
```
The tail **quantises to exactly three levels: 0.0, 0.5, 1.0** — deliberate, to avoid anti-aliased grey fringes. The `0.01/0.02` thresholds are on **raw non-linear depth**, tuned for the example's `near: 0.1, far: 10` ortho camera; with a perspective camera and a large far plane, distant edges stop being detected.

Normal — angular difference gated so only one side of a crease lights up:
```glsl
float neighborNormalEdgeIndicator(int x, int y, float depth, vec3 normal) {
	float depthDiff = getDepth(x, y) - depth;
	vec3 neighborNormal = getNormal(x, y);
	vec3 normalEdgeBias = vec3(1., 1., 1.); // This should probably be a parameter.
	float normalDiff = dot(normal - neighborNormal, normalEdgeBias);
	float normalIndicator = clamp(smoothstep(-.01, .01, normalDiff), 0.0, 1.0);
	float depthIndicator = clamp(sign(depthDiff * .25 + .0025), 0.0, 1.0);
	return (1.0 - dot(normal, neighborNormal)) * depthIndicator * normalIndicator;
}
// ...four taps summed, then:
return step(0.1, indicator);   // strictly binary
```
`normalEdgeBias` is **hardcoded to `vec3(1,1,1)`** — the source comment concedes it should be a parameter. It bakes a fixed directional preference into edge highlights, visible as asymmetry when you orbit. Fork the shader if you need symmetry.

Combination — depth edges **darken**, normal edges **lighten**, depth wins ties:
```glsl
float Strength = dei &gt; 0.0 ? (1.0 - depthEdgeStrength * dei) : (1.0 + normalEdgeStrength * nei);
gl_FragColor = texel * Strength;
```
It is a multiply on the existing colour, so hue is preserved — not a flat ink line. That is why `depthEdgeStrength` caps at 1 (reaching black) while `normalEdgeStrength` caps at 2.

**Camera snapping is NOT in the pass — confirmed, zero snapping logic.** Drop it into a project and the pixel grid will swim. All the snapping lives in the example HTML and you must port it. The key function shifts the **frustum** by the sub-pixel remainder rather than quantising the camera position, so motion stays smooth while the grid stays locked to world space (ortho only):

```js
function pixelAlignFrustum( camera, aspectRatio, pixelsPerScreenWidth, pixelsPerScreenHeight ) {
  const worldScreenWidth  = ( camera.right - camera.left ) / camera.zoom;
  const worldScreenHeight = ( camera.top - camera.bottom ) / camera.zoom;
  const pixelWidth  = worldScreenWidth  / pixelsPerScreenWidth;
  const pixelHeight = worldScreenHeight / pixelsPerScreenHeight;
  const camPos = new THREE.Vector3(); camera.getWorldPosition( camPos );
  const camRot = new THREE.Quaternion(); camera.getWorldQuaternion( camRot );
  const camRight = new THREE.Vector3( 1, 0, 0 ).applyQuaternion( camRot );
  const camUp    = new THREE.Vector3( 0, 1, 0 ).applyQuaternion( camRot );
  const fractX = camPos.dot( camRight ) / pixelWidth  - Math.round( camPos.dot( camRight ) / pixelWidth );
  const fractY = camPos.dot( camUp )    / pixelHeight - Math.round( camPos.dot( camUp )    / pixelHeight );
  camera.left   = - aspectRatio - ( fractX * pixelWidth );
  camera.right  =   aspectRatio - ( fractX * pixelWidth );
  camera.top    =   1.0 - ( fractY * pixelHeight );
  camera.bottom = - 1.0 - ( fractY * pixelHeight );
  camera.updateProjectionMatrix();
}
```
This is exactly the "snap then shift back by the snap error" technique from David Holland and yal.cc, expressed as a frustum offset.

Plus `pixelAlignCamera()`, which rounds spherical `theta`/`phi` to a rotation increment and `zoom` to a zoom increment. Example GUI defaults: `snapping: true, pixelAlignedPanning: true, pixelAlignedObjects: true, rotationSnap: 15, cameraRotationSnap: 9, cameraZoomSnap: 0.1`.

The animate loop uses **save → mutate → render → restore**, which is the part most people miss: snapped values are used only for the render, then reverted so `OrbitControls` keeps operating on smooth state.

Its texture helper is the counterpoint to gradient maps — colour maps **do** want sRGB:
```js
function pixelTexture( texture ) {
  texture.minFilter = THREE.NearestFilter;
  texture.magFilter = THREE.NearestFilter;
  texture.generateMipmaps = false;
  texture.colorSpace = THREE.SRGBColorSpace;
  return texture;
}
```

## 4) Other examples and low-res rendering

| Example | Page | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Toon + OutlineEffect | https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_materials_toon | https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_materials_toon.html |
| Pixelated pass | https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_postprocessing_pixel | https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_postprocessing_pixel.html |
| Sobel edge | https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_postprocessing_sobel | https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_postprocessing_sobel.html |
| Selective outline (OutlinePass) | https://threejs.org/examples/#webgl_postprocessing_outline | https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_postprocessing_outline.html |

There is no `webgl_postprocessing_edge` and no `cel` example. WebGPU siblings exist for all four. **Sobel is a luminance edge detector** — it traces texture detail and lighting gradients as well as geometry, so it is the wrong tool for clean cel outlines; the depth+normal approach is geometrically correct. Its `resolution` uniform must be updated on resize or edges get the wrong width.

`EffectComposer` — https://threejs.org/docs/pages/EffectComposer.html · `OrthographicCamera` — https://threejs.org/docs/pages/OrthographicCamera.html (ortho is effectively mandatory; `pixelAlignFrustum` has no perspective equivalent).

**Low-res rendering — two approaches, different jobs:**

(a) **Low-res canvas + CSS upscale.** Cheapest, but pixelates all canvas output and gives you no depth/normal buffers for edge detection.
```js
renderer.setPixelRatio( 1 );  // otherwise retina silently undoes the effect
renderer.setSize( window.innerWidth / SCALE, window.innerHeight / SCALE, false );
```
The `false` is load-bearing — docs: "Setting `updateStyle` to false prevents any style changes to the output canvas," so the backing store shrinks while CSS size stays. Pair with `canvas { image-rendering: pixelated; }`.

(b) **`WebGLRenderTarget` at low resolution + NearestFilter** — required for any edge detection. This is what `RenderPixelatedPass` does internally.
```js
const rt = new THREE.WebGLRenderTarget( width / SCALE, height / SCALE, {
	minFilter: THREE.NearestFilter,   // MUST override — default is LinearFilter
	magFilter: THREE.NearestFilter,   // the one that controls the upscale
	type: THREE.HalfFloatType,
	depthBuffer: true
} );
```
Two traps: **`minFilter` defaults to `LinearFilter`** on render targets (unlike `DataTexture`), and **`samples` defaults to 0 — leave it there**, MSAA anti-aliases exactly the hard pixel edges you want. Docs https://threejs.org/docs/pages/WebGLRenderTarget.html

**Flat shading in three.js costs nothing geometrically.** three.js does not split vertices; it derives the face normal from screen-space derivatives:
```glsl
#ifdef FLAT_SHADED
	vec3 fdx = dFdx( vViewPosition );
	vec3 fdy = dFdy( vViewPosition );
	vec3 normal = normalize( cross( fdx, fdy ) );
#endif
```
https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/src/renderers/shaders/ShaderChunk/normal_fragment_begin.glsl.js — so **you do not need `toNonIndexed()` + `computeVertexNormals()`**. Just set `flatShading: true`.

**`VOXLoader` already does greedy meshing** (`buildMesh()`; `VOXMesh` is deprecated) — https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/jsm/loaders/VOXLoader.js. It sweeps 3 axes, emits a face only when exactly one side is solid, greedy-merges the mask keyed on colour index, and outputs `position` + `index` + a per-quad `color` attribute converted with `SRGBColorSpace`, then `computeVertexNormals()` + `MeshStandardMaterial`. Because each quad owns its four vertices, normals come out flat regardless.

## 2026-09-14T09:02:54.310Z — assistant (source line 4961)

**Agreed on dropping the two-square guard.** Its cost was not plain draws (+1.0 draw point, inside the noise) but stuck games: more games hitting the move cap and more drawn games with the guard doing the holding (52% against 41%). Same thing to you as a player, so out it goes.

**The pawn-style double step is draw-safe but probably does nothing.** The drag came from an immortal piece with reach in the endgame. A double step allowed only from the home rank never reaches the endgame, so it cannot add draws. But it is also only one tempo of development for a piece worth under 1.5 pawns, and it depends on how you read it:

- **Slide** (middle square must be empty, exactly like a pawn): blocked by the pawn in front until that pawn moves, so it rarely comes up. I expect it to measure the same as the Wall.
- **Leap** (over the pawn, out to the third rank on move one): the reading that could matter. It lets the guard take an early anvil post in the centre, which is the one guard pattern the study found pays. That would show up as a few more decisive games, not fewer draws.

**Recommendation: worth one cheap run, low priority.** Adopt only if decisive share holds and guard activity rises; otherwise keep the Wall for simplicity. If neither reading moves anything, we drop the idea.

**Done now, nothing launched:**
- Lab toggle `guardDoubleFirst` (off, slide, leap) in [rules.ts](src/rules/rules.ts) and [engine.ts](src/rules/engine.ts), keyed on the home rank so no new state; tests added, 122 green, typecheck clean.
- Queue at [docs/QUEUE.md](docs/QUEUE.md): Q1 this test (two arms against the already-played Wall control, about 25 minutes), plus the earlier unrun items Q2 paladin buff, Q3 maester buff, Q4 double first turn, Q5 kamikaze-never diagnostic, Q6 AI data. The Warden extension pass is dropped. Say a name and I launch it.

**One question to settle when you can.** "Always just one guard per army" today means at most one: the pool deals a guard to about half of all armies. If you mean every army always has exactly one, like the king, the setup needs a reserved slot and the balance baseline shifts slightly. Which did you mean?
